) is a normal distribution almost surely, and hence is continuous. We treat the remaining two cases together by appealing to the Brownian Gibbs property in Theorem
2.14
for either
B
or
2^{-1/2}B
(where either
B=W^{n}
or
2^{1/2}B=\mathcal{B}
). By possibly increasing the size of
I
, we may assume
I=\left\llbracket 1,m\right\rrbracket
for some
m
. Let
[a,b]\subset(x,y)
.

By the Brownian Gibbs property, conditionally on the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}
generated by
B_{j}(t)
for all
(j,t)\notin\{i\}\times[a,y+1],
the process
B_{i}(t)-B_{i}(a),t\in[a,y+1]
is a Brownian bridge connecting
0
and
B_{i}(y+1)-B_{i}(a)
, conditioned so that the ensemble
B
remains nonintersecting. In particular, conditionally on the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
generated by
\mathcal{G}
and
B_{i}(y)-B_{i}(r),r\in[b,y]
, almost surely the distribution of

B_{i}(b)-B_{i}(a)

is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Finally,
\mathcal{F}_{[a,b]\times\{i\}}\subset\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, giving the result.
∎

Lemmas
2.15
and
2.16
together allow us to speak of a single optimizer or geodesic when considering last passage problems across these Brownian motions, Brownian melons, and the parabolic Airy line ensemble.

2.5
Melon geodesics and the Airy sheet

Recall the definition of the prelimiting extended Airy sheets

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=n^{1/6}\left(B^{n}[(2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n)\to(1+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{y},1)]-2k\sqrt{n}-n^{1/6}\sum_{i=1}^{k}2(y_{i}-x_{i})\right),

(18)

from Theorem
1.2
, where
B^{n}
is a collection of
n
independent two-sided standard Brownian motions. For thinking about the prelimiting sheets
\mathcal{S}^{n}
, it will be helpful to use an alternate formula for
\mathcal{S}^{n}
in terms of the prelimiting Airy line ensembles
\mathcal{B}^{n}
(defined in (
14
)).
Recall the Brownian
n
-melon
W^{n}=W_{0}B^{n}
.
When
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\cap[0,\infty)^{k}
, by Theorem
2.10
we have

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=n^{1/6}\left(W^{n}[(2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n)\to(1+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{y},1)]-2k\sqrt{n}-n^{1/6}\sum_{i=1}^{k}2(y_{i}-x_{i})\right).

Note that
\mathcal{B}^{n}
is just an affine transformation of
W^{n}
, given by (
14
).
Using the fact that last passage values commute with affine shifts, we have

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}^{n}[(\mathbf{x}-n^{1/3}/2,n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]-kn^{2/3}.

(19)

One of the main insights of
[
15
]
was finding a way to take a limit of the right-hand side of (
19
) for single points
x,y
in order to define the Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
in terms of the Airy line ensemble. The basic idea there was to carefully analyze the location and coalescence structure of geodesics across
\mathcal{B}^{n}
. The culmination of this analysis showed that if
\pi^{n}\{x,y\},\pi^{n}\{x,z\}
are the rightmost geodesics across
\mathcal{B}^{n}
from
(x-n^{1/3}/2,n)
to
(y,1)
and
(z,1)
, then with high probability
\pi^{n}\{x,y\}
and
\pi^{n}\{x,z\}
coincide outside of an
O(1)
region around the points
(y,1),(z,1)
. This suggests that difference

\mathcal{S}^{n}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}^{n}(x,z)

should converge to a difference of last passage problems in the Airy line ensemble. This, along with an estimate on the location of the paths
\pi^{n}\{x,z\}
, motivates the following definition of the Airy sheet on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}.

Definition 2.17
.

For a parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{B}
, we define the
half Airy sheet
of
\mathcal{B}
to be the function
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
specified by
the formulas

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(0,y)=\mathcal{B}_{1}(y)
for
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

For
x>0
and
y,z\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,z)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{B}[(-\sqrt{m/(2x)},m)\to(y,1)]-\mathcal{B}[(-\sqrt{m/(2x)},m)\to(z,1)].

(20)

•

For any
x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)=\lim_{a\to\infty}\frac{1}{a}\int_{-a}^{0}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,z)-(x-z)^{2}+\xi\right)dz,

(21)

where
\xi
is the expectation of the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution. Note that we could have integrated on the right-hand side of (
21
) over any interval of length
a
containing
0
.

It turns out that almost surely, all the limits above exist, and the resulting function
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}
is continuous. The existence of such an object follows from
[
15
, Theorem 8.3]
. The first bullet is part of
[
15
, Definition 8.1(ii)]
, the second bullet is
[
15
, Remark 8.1]
, and the third bullet is given by the second display in the proof of
[
15
, Proposition 8.2]
.

The half-Airy sheet can be extended to all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by a stationarity relationship, see
[
15
, Definition 8.1 and Theorem 8.3]
.

Definition 2.18
.

The
Airy sheet
is the unique (in law) random continuous function
\mathcal{S}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

•

\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(t+\cdot,t+\cdot)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}

•

\mathcal{S}|_{[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}
is a half Airy sheet.

Having defined the Airy sheet, we can now state the main convergence result from
[
15
]
. When stating this result, we also record convergence information for rightmost geodesics across
\mathcal{B}^{n}
(parts (ii, iii) below) which is the crucial input in defining the Airy sheet.

Theorem 2.19
.

For any subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
, there exists a further subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
and a coupling of
\mathcal{B}
, and
\{\mathcal{B}^{n}:n\in Y\}
such that the following statements all hold almost surely:

(i)

The pair
(\mathcal{B}^{n},\mathcal{S}^{n}|_{[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}})
converges in the uniform-on-compact topology to
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}})
. Here
\mathcal{B}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble, and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}
is the half-Airy sheet of
\mathcal{B}
.

(ii)

Let
Z^{n}_{m}(x,y)
denote the jump time from line
m+1
to
m
for the rightmost geodesic
\pi^{n}\{x,y\}
from
(x-n^{1/3}/2,n)
to
(y,1)
. For all
x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty),y\in\mathbb{Q},m\in\mathbb{N}
, the random variables
Z^{n}_{m}(x,y)
converge almost surely to limits
Z_{m}(x,y)
. Moreover,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{Z_{m}(x,y)}{\sqrt{m}}=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{2x}}.

(iii)

For every
x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
and
y<z\in\mathbb{Q}
, there are points
X_{1}<x<X_{2}
with
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
and
T<\min(y,z)
such that for all large enough
n
, we have

\Gamma(\pi^{n}\{X_{1},y\}|_{[T,y]})\cap\Gamma(\pi^{n}\{X_{2},z\}|_{[T,z]})\neq\emptyset.

Here recall that
\Gamma(\pi)
denotes the zigzag graph of
\pi
.

This coupling is constructed in
[
15
, Section 8]
(after Lemma 8.4). The construction there shows that condition (ii) above is satisfied. Property (i) of the coupling is shown as
[
15
, Lemma 8.5]
, and property (iii) of the coupling is shown in the proof of
[
15
, Lemma 8.5]
.
Note that the notion of a point ‘lying along the path
\pi
’ used in that proof means that a point is contained in the zigzag graph of
\pi
.
We remark that while the rationals
\mathbb{Q}
are used in the Theorem
2.19
, they play no special role. The theorem would still hold with any other countable dense set
D
in place of
\mathbb{Q}
.

To prove convergence of
\mathcal{S}^{n}
jointly over all
k
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we will similarly focus on understanding optimizers across
\mathcal{B}^{n}
. We use Theorem
2.19
of
[
15
]
as a starting point for our analysis. In the remainder of this section, we record a few auxiliary results from
[
15
]
that will also be needed in our analysis, along with some simple consequences of that paper.
We start with two technical lemmas that are stepping stones along the path to Theorem
2.19
.

For the first lemma, for a random array
\{R_{n,m}:n,m\in\mathbb{N}\}
, we write

R_{n,m}=\mathfrak{o}(r_{m})\qquad\text{ if for all }\epsilon>0\qquad\;\;\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(|R_{n,m}/r_{m}|>\epsilon)<\infty.

(22)

Lemma 2.20

(
[
15
, Lemma 7.1]
)
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. Then we have

\sup_{(x,y)\in K}\left|Z^{n}_{m}(x,y)+\sqrt{\frac{m}{2x}}\right|=\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{m})

and
Z^{n}_{m}(x,y)
is tight as a function of
n
for each fixed
m\in\mathbb{N},(x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

We also require a useful lemma about disjointness of geodesics.

Lemma 2.21

(
[
15
, Lemma 7.2]
)
.

Fix
x>0
and
y_{1}<y_{2}
. Then

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\pi^{n}\{x-\epsilon,y_{1}\}\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\pi^{n}\{x+\epsilon,y_{2}\}\text{ are essentially disjoint}\big)=0.

Note that these two lemmas are quite non-trivial. In particular, Lemma
2.20
requires the full power of a difficult structural theorem for the Airy line ensemble from
[
17
]
.

We also record a corollary of Lemma
2.21
that follows from symmetries of Brownian LPP.

Corollary 2.22
.

Fix
y_{1}<y_{2}
. Then

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\pi^{n}\{0,y_{1}\}\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\pi^{n}\{\epsilon,y_{2}\}\text{ are essentially disjoint}\big)=0.

Proof.

We write
\pi^{n}[x,y]
for the rightmost geodesic across the original Brownian motions from
(2n^{-1/3}x,n)
to
(1+2n^{-1/3}y,1)
.
By Lemma
2.21
and Lemma
2.11
, for any
x>0
we have

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\pi^{n}[x-\epsilon,y_{1}]\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\pi^{n}[x+\epsilon,y_{2}]\text{ are essentially disjoint}\big)=0.

for any
x>0
. Translation invariance of Brownian increments implies that the above statement holds for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, not just
x>0
, and monotonicity of geodesics (Proposition
2.1
) implies that the statement holds with
x-\epsilon
replaced by
x
. Setting
x=0
and translating back to the melon environment via Lemma
2.11
yields the result.
∎

Next, we record a few basic facts and symmetries about the Airy sheet.

Lemma 2.23

(see
[
15
, Lemma 9.1 and Remark 1.1.6]
)
.

The process
(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{S}(x,y)+(x-y)^{2}
is translation invariant in both
x
and
y
. Also,
\mathcal{S}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(-y,-x)
. Here the distributional equality is joint in all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{S}(0,0)
has GUE Tracy-Widom distribution, and hence satisfies the tail bound

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{S}(0,0)|>m)\leq ce^{-dm^{3/2}}

for universal constants
c,d>0
and all
m>0
.

Note that the tail bound in Lemma
2.23
on the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution goes back to
[
49
]
.

We end this section by giving a more flexible description of the difference
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,z)
defined in (
20
).

Lemma 2.24
.

Almost surely the following is true.
Take any
x\geq 0
and
z_{1}<z_{2}
.
Let
\pi:(-\infty,z_{1}]\to\mathbb{N}
be a nonincreasing cadlag function, such that
\displaystyle\lim_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\pi(y)}{2y^{2}}=x
. Then

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,z_{1})-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x,z_{2})=\lim_{y\to-\infty}\mathcal{B}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z_{1},1)]-\mathcal{B}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z_{2},1)].

(23)

Proof.

By applying Lemma
2.4
with the endpoint pairs
((y,\pi(y)),(z_{2},1))
and
((-\sqrt{\pi(y)/(2x+\delta)},\pi(y)),(z_{1},1))
, and sending
y\to-\infty
, we have that for any
\delta>0
,

\begin{split}&\liminf_{y\to-\infty}\mathcal{B}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z_{1},1)]-\mathcal{B}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z_{2},1)]\\
\geq\;&\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{B}[(-\sqrt{m/(2x+\delta)},m)\to(z_{1},1)]-\mathcal{B}[(-\sqrt{m/(2x+\delta)},m)\to(z_{2},1)]\\
=\;&\;\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x+\delta/2,z_{1})-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(x+\delta/2,z_{2}).\end{split}

Therefore by continuity of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}
, the right-hand side of (
23
) is bounded below by the left-hand side. For
x>0
, the opposite inequality holds by symmetric reasoning. For
x=0
, the opposite inequality holds since

\mathcal{B}[p\to(z_{1},1)]-\mathcal{B}[p\to(z_{2},1)]\leq\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{1})-\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{2})=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(0,z_{1})-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{B}}(0,z_{2}).

for any point
p\in(-\infty,z_{1}]\times\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, any path
\pi
from
p
to
(z_{1},1)
can always be extended to a path from
p
to
(z_{2},1)
by extending
\pi
to be equal to
1
on the interval
[z_{1},z_{2}]
. This picks up the increment
\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{2})-\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{1})
.
∎

3
Tightness

3.1
Tightness of prelimiting sheets

Recall from Theorem
1.2
the space
\mathfrak{X}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}.
Topologically,
\mathfrak{X}
is a disjoint union of certain subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2k}
. Let
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
be the space of functions from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
with the uniform-on-compact topology.
The main goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

The functions
\mathcal{S}^{n}
are tight in
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
.

Note that
\mathcal{S}^{n}
(from formula (
18
)) is not defined on all of
\mathfrak{X}
. To formally define
\mathcal{S}^{n}
as a random element of
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
, we arbitrarily extend
\mathcal{S}
to all of
\mathfrak{X}
in a continuous way so that
\mathcal{S}^{n}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
. For any compact set
K\subset\mathfrak{X}
,
\mathcal{S}^{n}|_{K}
is well-defined by (
18
) for all large enough
n
, so the arbitrary choice of extension does not affect any convergence or tightness statements.

Theorem
3.1
will follow from the deterministic bounds and inequalities in Section
2.2
, and explicit tightness bounds for the prelimiting Airy line ensemble, which we quote from
[
17
]
. For this proposition,
W^{n}
is a Brownian melon.

Proposition 3.2

(
[
17
, Proposition 4.1]
)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
c>0
. There exist constants
c_{k},d_{k}>0
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t>0,\;s\in(0,ctn^{-1/3}]
, and
a>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\left|W_{k}^{n}(t)-W_{k}^{n}(t+s)+\frac{s\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{t}}\right|>a\sqrt{s}\bigg)\leq c_{k}e^{-d_{k}a^{3/2}}.

We can translate Proposition
3.2
into a modulus of continuity on the prelimiting parabolic Airy line ensembles
\mathcal{B}^{n}
. To do this, we will employ a general lemma for establishing a modulus of continuity, also developed in
[
17
]
. This lemma will also be used later on when establishing a general modulus of continuity for the extended directed landscape.

Lemma 3.3

(
[
17
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

Let
T=I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{k}
be a product of bounded real intervals of length
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}
. Let
c,d>0
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be a random continuous function from
T
taking values in a vector space
V
with norm
|\cdot|
. Assume that for every
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, that there exist
\alpha_{i}\in(0,1),\beta_{i},r_{i}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{H}(t+e_{i}u)-\mathcal{H}(t)|\geq au^{{\alpha_{i}}})\leq ce^{-d{a^{{\beta_{i}}}}}

(24)

for every coordinate vector
e_{i}
, every
a>0
, and every
t,t+ue_{i}\in T
with
u<r_{i}
. Set
\beta=\min_{i}\beta_{i},\alpha=\max_{i}\alpha_{i}
, and
r=\max_{i}r_{i}
. Then with probability one we have

|\mathcal{H}(t+s)-\mathcal{H}(t)|\leq C\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|s_{i}|^{\alpha_{i}}\log^{1/\beta_{i}}\left(\frac{2r^{\alpha/\alpha_{i}}}{|s_{i}|}\right)\right),

(25)

for every
t,t+s\in T
with
|s_{i}|\leq r_{i}
for all
i
(here
s=(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
).
Here
C
is random constant satisfying

\mathbb{P}(C>a)\leq\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{b_{i}}{r_{i}}\right]cc_{0}e^{-c_{1}a^{\beta}},

where
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are constants that depend on
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k},k
and
d
. Notably, they do not depend on
b_{1},\dots,b_{k},c
or
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
c>0
. There exist positive constants
c_{k}
,
d_{k}
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t>0
,
s\in[0,ctn^{-1/3}]
, and
a>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\max_{t\leq x<y\leq t+s}\left|W_{k}^{n}(x)-W_{k}^{n}(y)+\frac{(y-x)\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{t}}\right|>a\sqrt{s}\bigg)\leq c_{k}e^{-d_{k}a^{3/2}}.

(26)

Proof.

We apply Lemma
3.3
with
k=1
,
\alpha_{1}=1/2,\beta_{1}=3/2
and the function
W_{k}^{n}(x)-x\sqrt{n/t}
. The assumption (
24
) is implied by Proposition
3.2
. Then we have

\left|W_{k}^{n}(x)-W_{k}^{n}(y)+\frac{(y-x)\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{t}}\right|\leq C\sqrt{y-x}\log^{2/3}\left(\frac{2s}{y-x}\right)

for all
1\leq x<y\leq 1+s
, where
C
is a random constant satisfying the tail bound on the right-hand side of (
26
) for some constants
c_{k},d_{k}
. The right-hand side above is bounded above by
C\sqrt{s}
for all
t\leq x<y\leq t+s
, yielding (
26
).
∎

We are now in a position to prove a two-point tail bound for
\mathcal{S}^{n}
. We first define the stationary version
\mathcal{R}^{n}:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2},

where
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Take any
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}<1
,
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}<n^{1/6}
and
a>0
.
Then

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|>a\sqrt{\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}})<ce^{-da^{3/2}},

for some constants
c,d>0
depending only on
k
.

In this proof and throughout the paper, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we write
-\mathbf{x}
for the unique element of
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
given by rearranging the coordinates of
-\mathbf{x}
. The basic idea of the proof of Lemma
3.5
is to reduce bounds on differences in
\mathcal{R}
to bounds on differences of lines in
W^{n}
by using deterministic last passage inequalities from Section
2.2
.

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

We first consider fixed
n
.
Recall (from Theorem
1.2
) that
\mathcal{S}^{n}
is defined using multi-point last passage values across a collection of independent two-sided standard Brownian motions
B=\{B_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
By using Lemma
2.6
repeatedly and changing one coordinate at a time, we have

|\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|<2k(2k+n)n^{1/6}\sup_{i,a,b}|B_{i}(a)-B_{i}(b)|+2n^{1/3}(\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|_{1}),

where the supremum is over all
1\leq i\leq n
, and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, such that both
a,b
are in either
[2n^{-1/3}(x_{i}\wedge x_{i}^{\prime}),2n^{-1/3}(x_{i}\vee x_{i}^{\prime})]
or
[1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}\wedge y_{i}^{\prime}),1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}\vee y_{i}^{\prime})]
.
Therefore by using standard tail bounds on Brownian motion increments, for any fixed
n
the bound in the lemma holds by taking
c
large and
d
small (depending on
n
and
k
).

For the remainder of the proof it suffices to consider
n
sufficiently large (depending on
k
).
By the triangle inequality, and by the symmetry
\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{R}^{n}(-\mathbf{y},-\mathbf{x})
, it suffices to prove the bound when
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
agree at all points except for a single coordinate
y_{\ell}<y_{\ell}^{\prime}
. Moreover, if we let
T_{c}
be the map translating all coordinates in a vector by
c
, then
\mathcal{R}^{n}(T_{c}\mathbf{x},T_{c}\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for all
c
, so we may assume
x_{\ell}=0
if we relax the norm bounds to
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}<2n^{1/6}
.
With these simplifications, the inequality is equivalent to

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|>a(y_{\ell}^{\prime}-y_{\ell})^{1/2})<ce^{-da^{3/2}}.

Now, by the representation (
19
) for
\mathcal{S}^{n}
, we can write

\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{A}^{n}[(\mathbf{x}-n^{1/3}/2,n)\to(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},1)]-\mathcal{A}^{n}[(\mathbf{x}-n^{1/3}/2,n)\to(\mathbf{y},1)],

(27)

where
\mathcal{A}^{n}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{B}^{n}_{i}(x)+x^{2}
.
As in Lemma
2.5
, let
\mathbf{x}^{L}
denote the first
\ell
coordinates of
\mathbf{x}
and let
\mathbf{y}^{R}
denote the last
k-\ell
coordinates of
x
. By two applications of that lemma, and (
27
), we have

\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{R},\mathbf{y}^{\prime R})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{R},\mathbf{y}^{R})\leq\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{L},\mathbf{y}^{\prime L})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{L},\mathbf{y}^{L}).

Now let
0^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{i}
denote the vector whose coordinates are all
x_{\ell}=0
. We can bound the left- and right-hand sides above using Lemma
2.4
applied to the points
0^{k-\ell+1}\leq\mathbf{x}^{R},\mathbf{y}^{R}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime R}
and
\mathbf{x}^{L}\leq 0^{\ell},\mathbf{y}^{L}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime L}
to get that

\mathcal{R}^{n}(0^{k-\ell+1},\mathbf{y}^{\prime R})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(0^{k-\ell+1},\mathbf{y}^{R})\leq\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\mathcal{R}^{n}(0^{\ell},\mathbf{y}^{\prime L})-\mathcal{R}^{n}(0^{\ell},\mathbf{y}^{L}).

By these inequalities and (
27
), it then suffices to bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{n}[(-n^{1/3}/2,n)^{k-\ell+1}\to(\mathbf{y}^{\prime R},1)]-\mathcal{A}^{n}[(-n^{1/3}/2,n)^{k-\ell+1}\to(\mathbf{y}^{R},1)]<-a(y_{\ell}^{\prime}-y_{\ell})^{1/2}),\qquad\;\text{and}\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{n}[(-n^{1/3}/2,n)^{\ell}\to(\mathbf{y}^{\prime L},1)]-\mathcal{A}^{n}[(-n^{1/3}/2,n)^{\ell}\to(\mathbf{y}^{L},1)]>a(y_{\ell}^{\prime}-y_{\ell})^{1/2}).

By Lemma
2.12
applied to
\mathcal{A}^{n}
, for any endpoint pair starting
(-n^{1/3}/2,n)^{i}
for some
i\leq k
, there is an optimizer that only uses the top
k
lines. By Lemma
2.6
, the above two probabilities are bounded by

\mathbb{P}\left(2k\max_{1\leq i\leq k,y_{\ell}\leq x<y\leq y_{\ell}^{\prime}}|\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n}(x)-\mathcal{A}_{i}^{n}(y)|>a(y_{\ell}^{\prime}-y_{\ell})^{1/2}\right).

Rewriting this probability in terms of
W^{n}
gives

\mathbb{P}\left(2k\max_{1\leq i\leq k,y_{\ell}\leq x<y\leq y_{\ell}^{\prime}}|n^{1/6}(W_{i}^{n}(1+2n^{-1/3}x)-W_{i}^{n}(1+2n^{-1/3}y))+x^{2}-y^{2}-2n^{1/3}(x-y)|\right.\\
\left.>a(y_{\ell}^{\prime}-y_{\ell})^{1/2}\right).

(28)

Now, using that
|y_{\ell}|<2n^{1/6}
and
|y_{\ell}-y_{\ell}^{\prime}|<1
, for any
y_{\ell}\leq x<y\leq y_{\ell}^{\prime}
and
n
large enough, by a Taylor expansion we have

\left|\frac{2n^{1/3}(x-y)}{\sqrt{1+2n^{-1/3}y_{\ell}}}-2n^{1/3}(x-y)+2(x-y)y_{\ell}\right|\leq 3n^{-1/3}(y-x)y_{\ell}^{2}\leq 12(y-x).

Therefore

\left|x^{2}-y^{2}-2n^{1/3}(x-y)-n^{1/6}\frac{2n^{-1/3}(y-x)\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{1+2n^{-1/3}y_{\ell}}}\right|<|x^{2}-y^{2}-2(x-y)y_{\ell}|+12(y-x)\\
\leq(y-x)(|x+y-2y_{\ell}|+12)\leq 14(y_{\ell}^{\prime}-y_{\ell})^{1/2}.

(29)

The conclusion then follows by combining (
28
), (
29
), Corollary
3.4
, and a union bound.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{S}^{n}|_{K}
is tight for all compact sets
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. We may assume
K
contains
0
. First,
\mathcal{S}^{n}(0^{k},0^{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}^{n}_{i}(0)
, so
\mathcal{S}^{n}(0^{k},0^{k})
is tight by Theorem
2.13
. Tightness of
\mathcal{S}^{n}|_{K}
then follows from Lemma
3.5
and the Kolmorogov-Chentsov criterion, see Corollary 14.9 in
[
31
]
.
∎

3.2
Tightness of melon optimizers

Here we prove tightness and asymptotic results about melon optimizers.
For this, we extend the notions of geodesics and jump times from Section
2.5
to the case where the endpoints are not singletons.
For vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
x_{1}\geq 0
, we write

\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}=(\pi^{n}_{1}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\},\dots,\pi^{n}_{k}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\})

for the rightmost optimizer across
\mathcal{B}^{n}
from
(\mathbf{x}-n^{1/3}/2,n)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
.
We will write
\pi^{n}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]
for the rightmost optimizer across the original Brownian motions between the corresponding points
(2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n)
and
(1+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{y},1)
. We write

Z^{n}_{i,m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

for the jump time from line
m+1
to
m
for the path
\pi^{n}_{i}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
.

We start with a weak tightness result.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, and set
x^{k}=(x,\dots,x),y^{k}=(y,\dots,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then for every
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, the sequence of jump times

\{Z^{n}_{i,1}(x^{k},y^{k}):n\in\mathbb{N}\}

is tight.

Proof.

We write
\pi^{n}_{i}:=\pi^{n}_{i}\{x^{k},y^{k}\}
for the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
path in the disjoint optimizer
\pi^{n}\{x^{k},y^{k}\}
. By Lemma
2.7
and (
19
), we have

\|\pi_{i}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-n^{2/3}\geq\mathcal{S}^{n}(x^{k},y^{k})-\mathcal{S}^{n}(x^{k-1},y^{k-1}).

In particular, by Theorem
3.1
, the random variables

Y_{n}:=\|\pi_{i}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-n^{2/3}

are tight. Now suppose that
Z^{n}_{i,1}(x^{k},y^{k})<r
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\pi_{i}^{n}(z)=1
for all
z\in[r,y]
, so

\displaystyle\|\pi_{i}^{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\|\pi_{i}^{n}|_{[x-n^{1/3}/2,r]}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}+\mathcal{B}^{n}_{1}(y)-\mathcal{B}^{n}_{1}(r)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{B}^{n}[(x-n^{1/3}/2,n)\to(r,1)]+\mathcal{B}^{n}_{1}(y)-\mathcal{B}^{n}_{1}(r).

Therefore by (
19
) again, we have

Y_{n}\wedge 0\leq\mathbf{1}(Z^{n}_{i,1}(x^{k},y^{k})<r)[\mathcal{S}^{n}(x,r)+\mathcal{S}^{n}(0,y)-\mathcal{S}^{n}(0,r)]

(30)

The term multiplying the indicator on right-hand side of (
30
) converges to

X(x,y,r)=\mathcal{S}(x,r)+\mathcal{S}(0,y)-\mathcal{S}(0,r).

Since
\mathcal{S}(x,y)+(x-y)^{2}
has GUE Tracy-Widom distribution for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
(Lemma
2.23
), by a union bound, for all
x,y,r,m>0
we have

\mathbb{P}(X(x,y,r)>m-x^{2}-y^{2}+2xr)\leq ce^{-dm^{3/2}},

for some constants
c,d>0
.
In particular,
X(x,y,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}-\infty
as
r\to-\infty
for fixed
x,y
. Combining this, (
30
), and the tightness of
Y_{n}
gives that

\lim_{r\to-\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(Z^{n}_{i,1}(x^{k},y^{k})<r)=0.

Since all the random variables
Z^{n}_{i,1}(x^{k},y^{k})
are bounded above by
y
, this implies that the sequence
Z^{n}_{i,1}(x^{k},y^{k})
is tight.
∎

We can use Lemma
3.6
to prove a disjointness lemma for optimizers.

Lemma 3.7
.

Consider
\pi^{n}[x^{k},y^{k}]
, the (almost surely unique) optimizer from
(2xn^{-1/3},n)^{k}
to
(1+2yn^{-1/3},1)^{k}
across
n
independent standard Brownian motions
B^{n}
. Then for every
x,y\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
, we have that

\lim_{r\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n}[x^{k},y^{k}]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[x-\epsilon,y-r]\;\text{and}\;\pi^{n}[x+\epsilon,y+r]\right)=1.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we write
\pi^{n,k}[x,y]:=\pi^{n}[x^{k},y^{k}]
.
First, by a union bound it suffices to show that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n,k}[x,y]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[x-\epsilon,y-r]\right)=1,\qquad\text{ and}

(31)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n,k}[x,y]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[x+\epsilon,y+r]\right)=1.

(32)

We first simplify (
31
) and (
32
).
Translation invariance of Brownian increments and Brownian scaling gives that

\displaystyle B^{n}(t)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\alpha_{n}^{-1/2}\left(B^{n}(\alpha_{n}(t-2n^{-1/3}(x-\epsilon)))-B^{n}(2\alpha_{n}n^{-1/3}(x-\epsilon))\right),

\displaystyle\;\;\text{ where }\;\;\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+2(-x+\epsilon+y)n^{-1/3}},

and so (
31
) is equal to

\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n,k}[\epsilon+O(n^{-1/3}),0]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[0,-r+O(n^{-1/3})]\right).

Here the
O(n^{-1/3})
terms are small in the sense that for fixed
r,x,y
, there exists
c>0
such that
|O(n^{-1/3})|\leq cn^{-1/3}
.
In particular, for large enough
n
, monotonicity of optimizers (Lemma
2.3
) implies that this is bounded below by

\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n,k}[\epsilon/2,0]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[0,-r/2]\right).

(33)

By applying translation invariance and Brownian scaling, we can similarly show that (
32
) is equal to

\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n,k}[0,-r+O(n^{-1/3})]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[\epsilon+O(n^{-1/3}),0]\right),

which is again bounded below by

\mathbb{P}\left(\pi^{n,k}[0,-r/2]\text{ is essentially disjoint from }\pi^{n}[\epsilon/2,0]\right)

(34)

for large enough
n
. Next, by Lemma
2.11
and Lemma
2.15
, the probabilities (
33
) and (
34
) are the same as the corresponding probabilities with melon paths
\pi^{n}\{,\}
in place of the original Brownian paths
\pi^{n}[,]
.

Now, for any
0<b,a<c
and
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, since the melon path
\pi^{n}\{0^{k},a^{k}\}
only uses the top
k
lines by Lemma
2.12
,
\pi^{n}\{0^{k},a^{k}\}
is disjoint from
\pi^{n}\{b^{\ell},c^{\ell}\}
whenever the jump time

Z^{n}_{1,k}(b^{\ell},c^{\ell})>a.

Therefore to prove (
33
) and (
34
), we just need to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(Z^{n}_{1,1}((\epsilon/2)^{k},0^{k})>-r/2\right)=1,\qquad\text{ and}

(35)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(Z^{n}_{k}(\epsilon/2,0)>-r/2\right)=1.

(36)

Equation (
35
) follows from the tightness of
Z^{n}_{1,1}(x^{k},y^{k})
for fixed
x,y
in Lemma
3.6
.
Equation (
36
) follows from the tightness of
Z^{n}_{k}(\epsilon/2)
for fixed
k,\epsilon
in Lemma
2.20
.
∎

Lemma
3.7
can be combined with the asymptotics in Lemma
2.20
to give tightness and asymptotics for jump times on optimizers across the melon. For this next lemma, we set
(0,\infty)^{k}_{\leq}=(0,\infty)^{k}\cap\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any compact set
K\subset(0,\infty)_{\leq}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}
, we have that

\sup_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in K,i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket}\left|Z^{n}_{i,m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\sqrt{\frac{m}{2x_{i}}}\right|=\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{m}).

(37)

Moreover, for any fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},m,
and
i
, the sequence
Z^{n}_{i,m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is tight in
n
.

Proof.

We first prove this for a single
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. In the notation of Lemma
2.3
, for every
i
we have
(x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k})\leq_{1-i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq_{k-i}(x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k})
. Therefore by that lemma, we have

Z^{n}_{1,m}(x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k})\leq Z^{n}_{i,m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq Z^{n}_{k,m}(x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k}),

so it suffices to prove bounds when
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
consist only of repeated points. For this, observe that on the event
A_{\epsilon,r}
where the melon optimizer
\pi^{n}\{x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k}\}
is essentially disjoint from
\pi^{n}\{x_{i}-\epsilon,-r\}
and
\pi^{n}\{x_{i}+\epsilon,r\}
, that

Z^{n}_{j,m}(x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k})\in[Z^{n}_{m}(x_{i}-\epsilon,-r),Z^{n}_{m}(x_{i}+\epsilon,r)]

(38)

for all
m\in\mathbb{N},j\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
. By Lemma
2.11
, essential disjointness of
\pi^{n}\{x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k}\}
from
\pi^{n}\{x_{i}-\epsilon,-r\}
and
\pi^{n}\{x_{i}+\epsilon,r\}
is equivalent to essential disjointness of the original Brownian optimizers
\pi^{n}[x_{i}^{k},y_{i}^{k}]
from
\pi^{n}[x_{i}-\epsilon,-r]
and
\pi^{n}[x_{i}+\epsilon,r]
.
Therefore by Lemma
3.7
,

\lim_{r\to\infty}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}A_{\epsilon,r}=1.

Moreover, the asymptotics of the interval on the right-hand side of (
38
) are given by Lemma
2.20
. Putting these together proves (
37
) for a single point. The extension to the entire compact set follows again from monotonicity (Lemma
2.3
).

Finally, the tightness claim for fixed
k
follows from (
37
), the definition of the notation
\mathfrak{o}
, and the fact that the
Z^{n}_{i,m}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
are nonincreasing in
m
:
Z^{n}_{i,1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\geq Z^{n}_{i,2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\geq\ldots
.
∎

For this next corollary, we extend the definition of path space to include paths with noncompact domains. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the space of all nonincreasing cadlag functions from any closed interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{Z}
. For a sequence
\pi_{n}\in\mathcal{P}
, we say that
\pi^{n}\to\pi
if

\Gamma(\pi_{n})\cap[-n,n]\times\left\llbracket-n,n\right\rrbracket\to\Gamma(\pi)\cap[-n,n]\times\{-n,\dots,n\}

in the Hausdorff topology for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This is a Polish space, since the Hausdorff topology on paths whose zigzag graphs live in
[-n,n]\times\left\llbracket-n,n\right\rrbracket
is Polish for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Corollary 3.9
.

For any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in(0,\infty)_{\leq}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}
, the paths
\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
are tight in distribution in the product of
k
path spaces. Subsequential limits are
k
-tuples of nonincreasing paths
\pi_{i}:(-\infty,y_{i}]\to\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, any distributional subsequential limit
(\pi,\mathcal{B})
of
(\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\},\mathcal{B}^{n})
satisfies the following property:

For any set of times
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(z_{i},m)\in\Gamma(\pi_{i})
for all
i
, the restricted paths
\{\pi_{i}|_{[z_{i},y_{i}]}:i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
form a disjoint optimizer in
\mathcal{B}
from
(\mathbf{z},k)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
.

Proof.

Tightness is immediate from the tightness of each of the jump time sequences
Z^{n}_{i,k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
established in Lemma
3.8
, and the definition of the topology on path space. Now, consider a subsequential limit
(\pi,\mathcal{B})
of
(\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\},\mathcal{B}^{n})
, a coupling where
(\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\},\mathcal{B}^{n})\to(\pi,\mathcal{B})
almost surely, and a set of times
\mathbf{z}
as above. Since essential disjointness and path ordering are preversed under taking limits, on the almost sure set where this convergence holds,
\{\pi_{i}|_{[z_{i},y_{i}]}\}
is a disjoint
k
-tuple from
(\mathbf{z},m)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{B}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
uniformly on compact sets, we have

\mathcal{B}^{n}[(\mathbf{z},k)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|\pi_{i}^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}|_{[z_{i},y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}\to\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|\pi_{i}|_{[z_{i},y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}

(39)

as
n\to\infty
. Finally,
\mathcal{B}^{n}[(\mathbf{z},k)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]\to\mathcal{B}[(\mathbf{z},k)\to(\mathbf{y},1)]
by uniform-on-compact convergence, so (
39
) implies that
\{\pi_{i}|_{[z_{i},y_{i}]}\}
is a disjoint optimizer in
\mathcal{B}
from
(\mathbf{z},k)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
.
∎

4
Last passage percolation across the parabolic Airy line ensemble

Having established tightness of melon optimizers and prelimiting sheets, our next goal is to construct the limits of these objects. To do this, we introduce a notion of length and last passage percolation for infinite paths in
\mathcal{B}
.

4.1
Parabolic paths, length, and geodesics in
\mathcal{B}

A
parabolic path
across
\mathcal{B}
from
x\geq 0
to
z\in\mathbb{R}
is a nonincreasing cadlag function
\pi:(-\infty,z]\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\lim_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\pi(y)}{2y^{2}}=x.

(40)

For every
y<z
define the
discrepancy of
\pi
at
y
by

D_{\pi}(y)=\|\pi|_{[y,z]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\mathcal{B}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z,1)].

Note that
D_{\pi}(y)\leq 0
for all
y
.
We then define the length of
\pi
by

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{S}(x,z)+\liminf_{y\to-\infty}D_{\pi}(y),

(41)

where
\mathcal{S}
is the half-Airy sheet defined from
\mathcal{B}
as in Definition
2.17
.
A parabolic path
\pi
is a
geodesic
from
x
to
y
if the length
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is finite, and is maximal among all paths in
\mathcal{B}
from
x
to
z
. A parabolic path
\pi
is
locally geodesic
if
\pi|_{[a,b]}
is a geodesic for every compact interval
[a,b]
.
We first record some basic properties of lengths and geodesics in
\mathcal{B}
. The first lemma records useful deterministic facts.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a parabolic Airy line ensemble.

(i)

For any parabolic path
\pi
, the discrepancy
D_{\pi}(y)
is increasing in
y
. In particular, the liminf on the right-hand side of (
41
) is actually a limit.

(ii)

A parabolic path
\pi
from
x
to
z
is a geodesic if and only if
\pi
is locally geodesic, or equivalently
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{S}(x,z)
.

(iii)

If
\pi_{n}
is a sequence of parabolic paths from
x_{n}
to
z_{n}
converging to a parabolic path
\pi
from
x
to
z
, then
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\pi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\leq\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

For any parabolic path
\pi:(-\infty,z]\to\mathbb{N}
, for
y_{1}<y_{2}\leq z
we have

\|\pi|_{[y_{1},z]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\|\pi|_{[y_{1},y_{2}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\|\pi|_{[y_{2},z]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Combining this with the triangle inequality for last passage values in
\mathcal{B}
between the points
(\pi(y_{1}),y_{1}),(\pi(y_{2}),y_{2}),
and
(\pi(z),z)
, we get that

D_{\pi}(y_{2})-D_{\pi}(y_{1})\geq\mathcal{B}[(y_{1},\pi(y_{1}))\to(y_{2},\pi(y_{2}))]-\|\pi|_{[y_{1},y_{2}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\geq 0,

so
D_{\pi}
is increasing, giving (i).

For part (ii), note that
\pi
is locally geodesic if and only if
D_{\pi}(y)=0
for all
y
, or equivalently, if
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{S}(x,z)
. Noting that
D_{\pi}\leq 0
for any path
\pi
, if
D_{\pi}=0
, then
\pi
must be a geodesic. For the opposite direction, suppose that
\pi
is a path from
x
to
y
with

\lim_{y\to-\infty}D_{\pi}(y)=-c<0.

Then
D_{\pi}(y)=-a\in[-c,0)
for some
y<z
. We could modify the path
\pi
by replacing
\pi|_{[y,z]}
with a geodesic from
(y,\pi(y))
to
(z,1)
. The new path
\pi^{\prime}
is a also a parabolic path from
x
to
z
, and

D_{\pi^{\prime}}(y^{\prime})=D_{\pi}(y^{\prime})+a

for all
y^{\prime}<y
. Therefore
\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}>\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
, so
\pi
cannot be a geodesic.

For part (iii), observe that if
\pi_{n}\to\pi
, then the domains converge, and by continuity of
\mathcal{B}
, the last passage values on any compact interval
[x,y]
also converge. In particular,
D_{\pi_{n}}(y)\to D_{\pi}(y)
for all
y
. Combining this with the monotonicity from (i) and the continuity of the Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
(Definition
2.18
) gives (iii).
∎

Existence, uniqueness, and other basic structural results about geodesics across
\mathcal{B}
are guaranteed by limiting results for Brownian melons.

Lemma 4.2
.

(i)

(Uniqueness) For any fixed
(x,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique geodesic
\pi\{x,y\}
in
\mathcal{B}
from
x
to
y
almost surely.

(ii)

(Existence) Almost surely, for every
(x,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, there exists a geodesic
\pi
in
\mathcal{B}
from
x
to
y
. Moreover, almost surely for every
x,y\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, there are geodesics
\pi_{L}\{x,y\}
and
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
from
x
to
y
satisfying
\pi_{L}\{x,y\}(t)\leq\pi(t)\leq\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(t)
for any geodesic
\pi
from
x
to
y
and all
t\in(-\infty,y]
. We call
\pi_{L}\{x,y\}
and
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
the
leftmost and rightmost geodesics from
x
to
y
.

(iii)

(Overlap in the trunk) For a fixed
x\geq 0
and
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and any geodesics
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
from
x
to
y
and
x
to
y^{\prime}
, almost surely we have
\pi(z)=\pi^{\prime}(z)
for all sufficiently negative
z
.

(iv)

(Disjointness Structure) Let
x\geq 0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. For any fixed
r>0
, we have

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathbb{P}(\Gamma(\pi\{(x-\epsilon)\vee 0,y\})\cap\Gamma(\pi\{x+\epsilon,y+r\})=\emptyset)=0.

(42)

Also, for any fixed
0\leq x<x^{\prime}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\begin{split}\lim_{r\to\infty}&\mathbb{P}(\pi\{x,y\}\;\text{and}\;\pi\{x^{\prime},y+r\}\text{ are essentially disjoint})=1,\qquad\;\text{and}\;\\
\lim_{r\to\infty}&\mathbb{P}(\pi\{x,y-r\}\;\text{and}\;\pi\{x^{\prime},y\}\text{ are essentially disjoint})=1.\end{split}

(43)

(v)

(Monotonicity and tree structure) Let
\Omega
be the almost sure set where rightmost geodesics
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
from
x
to
y
exist for every
(x,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. On
\Omega
, for every
0\leq x_{1}\leq x_{2}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
, we have

\pi_{R}\{x_{1},y_{1}\}(t)\leq\pi_{R}\{x_{2},y_{2}\}(t)

for all
t\leq y_{1}
, and the overlap of zigzag graphs

\Gamma(\pi_{R}\{x_{1},y_{1}\})\cap\Gamma(\pi_{R}\{x_{2},y_{2}\})

is either empty, or else is the zigzag graph of a cadlag function
\pi
from a closed interval to
\mathbb{R}
.

While the proof of Lemma
4.2
is rather lengthy, the basic idea is just to use the limiting structure of Brownian melon geodesics in Theorem
2.19
. This theorem guarantees that limits of Brownian melon geodesics are geodesics across the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, the coalescence claims from this theorem pass to coalescence results for geodesics across
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

We will work with a subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
and a coupling of
\mathcal{B}^{n}
and
\mathcal{B}
so that the following conditions hold almost surely:

1.

\mathcal{B}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
.

2.

For all
(x,z)\in(\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty))\times\mathbb{Q}
, there exists a geodesic
\pi\{x,z\}
across
\mathcal{B}
from
x
to
z
such that
\pi^{n}\{x,z\}\to\pi\{x,z\}
.

3.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty),z<y\in\mathbb{Q}
, there exist
X_{1}<x<X_{2}
with
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
such that for all large enough
n
, there is a point
(W_{n},R_{n})
in the zigzag graph of both
\pi^{n}\{X_{1},z\}
and
\pi^{n}\{X_{2},y\}
. Moreover,
(W_{n},R_{n})\to(W,R)
for some
(W,R)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
.

4.

For any
w<y\in\mathbb{Q}
and
n>m\in\mathbb{N}
, there is almost surely a unique geodesic in
\mathcal{B}
from
(w,n)
to
(y,m)
.

The existence of a coupling satisfying conditions 1-3 follows from Theorem
2.19
. Condition 1 is immediate from Theorem
2.19
(i). Corollary
3.9
and the asymptotics in Theorem
2.19
(ii) guarantees convergence of the finite geodesics
\pi^{n}\{x,z\}
to a limiting parabolic path
\pi\{x,z\}
from
x
to
z
. The second part of Corollary
3.9
guarantees that each
\pi\{x,z\}
is locally geodesic, and hence is a geodesic by Lemma
4.1
(ii). This gives condition 2.

For condition 3, Theorem
2.19
(iii) guarantees that there exist
X_{1}<x<X_{2}
and
T\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all large enough
n
, the zigzag graphs of
\pi^{n}\{X_{1},z\}
and
\pi^{n}\{X_{2},y\}
overlap on the interval
[T,z]
. Since the paths
\pi^{n}\{X_{1},z\}|_{[T,z]}
and
\pi^{n}\{X_{2},y\}|_{[T,z]}
both converge, the region of overlap also converges. Therefore we can find
(W_{n},R_{n})\in\Gamma(\pi^{n}\{X_{1},z\})\cap\Gamma(\pi^{n}\{X_{2},y\})
that converges to some
(W,R)
. Condition 4 follows from Lemma
2.15
. For all proofs we work on the almost sure set where the four conditions above hold.

Proof of (i) for
x>0
:
Without loss of generality we can assume that
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
; the general case can be dealt with by working on a version of the above coupling where
(\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty))\times\mathbb{Q}
is replaced by
((\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty))\times\mathbb{Q})\cup\{(x,y)\}
. The existence of such a coupling still holds in this context, see the discussion after Theorem
2.19
. Suppose that
\pi^{\prime}
is another geodesic from
x
to
y
, and let
z<y,z\in\mathbb{Q}
. It is enough to show that
\pi^{\prime}=\pi\{x,y\}
on the interval
[z,y]
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be as in property
3
of the coupling for the triple
x,z<y
. The parabolic shape of the paths
\pi\{X_{1},z\},\pi\{x,y\},\pi^{\prime},
and
\pi\{X_{2},z\}
ensures that for large enough
m\in\mathbb{N}
we can find times
t_{1}<t_{2}<W
and
s,s^{\prime}\in(t_{1},t_{2})
such that

\pi\{X_{1},z\}(t_{1})=\pi\{X_{2},y\}(t_{2})=\pi\{x,y\}(s)=\pi^{\prime}(s^{\prime})=m

Also, let
r_{1},r_{2}
be rational times with
s,s^{\prime}\in(r_{1},r_{2})\subset(t_{1},t_{2})
. There are unique finite geodesics
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
from
(r_{1},m)
,
(r_{2},m)
to
(y,1)
.
Since the paths
\pi\{X_{1},z\},\pi\{x,y\},\pi^{\prime},
and
\pi\{X_{2},z\}
are locally geodesic by Lemma
4.1
(ii), we can apply the monotonicity in Lemma
2.3
(i) to get that

\pi\{X_{1},z\}|_{[t_{1},z]}\leq\tau_{1}\leq\pi\{x,y\}|_{[s,y]}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\pi\{X_{1},z\}|_{[t_{1},y]}.

The outer two inequalities imply that the point
(W,R)
is contained in the zigzag graphs of both
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
. Therefore by the tree structure of geodesics (Proposition
2.1
) and the uniqueness of
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
, the paths
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
coincide on the interval
[W,y]
. The inner two inequalities above then imply that
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
also coincide with
\pi\{x,y\}
on this interval. The same holds for
\pi^{\prime}
, and hence
\pi\{x,y\}=\pi^{\prime}
on
[W,y]
. Since
W\leq z
, this gives the desired claim.

Proof of (ii) for
x>0
:
By Lemma
2.3
, for any fixed
z
, the functions
(x,y)\mapsto\pi^{n}\{x,y\}(z)
are nondecreasing in
x
and
y
. This property passes to the limits
\pi\{x,y\}
.
Therefore for any
(x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, and any monotone decreasing sequences
x_{n}\downarrow x,y_{n}\downarrow y
with
(x_{n},y_{n})
rational, the paths
\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
have a limit in path space. This limit is a function
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}:(-\infty,y]\to\mathbb{N}
. Since
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}\leq\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
for all
n
, and each
\pi
is a parabolic path from
x_{n}
to
y_{n}
, we have

\limsup_{z\to-\infty}\frac{\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(z)}{2z^{2}}\leq x.

(44)

Again by monotonicity, for any rational points
x^{\prime}<x,y^{\prime}<y
and any
n
, we have
\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}\geq\pi\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\}
. Therefore
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}\geq\pi\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\}
as well, and so (
44
) is an equality with the
\limsup
replaced by a limit, and hence
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
is a parabolic path from
x
to
y
. The fact that
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
is a geodesic follows from Lemma
4.1
(iii) and continuity of the Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
(see Definition
2.18
). This proves existence of geodesics for
x>0
.

Next, we show that each
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
must be the rightmost geodesic from
x
to
y
. Suppose that there were another geodesic
\pi^{\prime}
with
\pi^{\prime}(t)>\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(t)
for some
t\in(-\infty,y)
. Since both
\pi,\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
are cadlag, there must exist
\epsilon>0
such that
\pi^{\prime}(s)>\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(s)
for all
s\in[t,t+\epsilon]
. Zigzag graph convergence of
\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
to
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
implies pointwise convergence at all continuity points of
\pi_{R}\{x,y\}
. In particular, pointwise convergence holds for some
s\in[t,t+\epsilon]
. Therefore for all large enough
n
, we have

\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}(s)=\pi_{R}\{x,y\}(s)<\pi^{\prime}(s).

(45)

Now define a new function
\pi^{*}
on
(-\infty,x_{n}]
by
\pi^{*}(t)=\max\{\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}(t),\pi^{\prime}(t)\}
for
t\leq y
and
\pi^{*}=\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
on
(y,y_{n}]
. The function
\pi^{*}
is a parabolic path from
x_{n}
to
y_{n}
. Also, since geodesics are locally geodesic,
\pi^{*}
must also be locally geodesic, and hence is a geodesic from
x_{n}
to
y_{n}
. Since
\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}\neq\pi^{*}
by (
45
), this contradicts the uniqueness of
\pi\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
shown in (i). The existence of leftmost geodesics is similar.

Proof of (iii) for
x>0
:
Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
be two geodesics from
x
to two points
y<y^{\prime}
. As in the proof of (i), for every
z<\min(y,y^{\prime})
, we can find a time
W_{z}\leq z
and a location
R_{z}
such that
(W_{z},R_{z})
lies on the zigzag graphs of both
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
. Let
W=\{W_{z}:z<\min(y,y^{\prime})\}
. Also, for any rational points
q<q^{\prime}<\min(y,y^{\prime})
, condition 4 of the coupling ensures that
\pi|_{[q,q^{\prime}]}
is the unique geodesic from
(q,\pi(q))
to
(q^{\prime},\pi(q^{\prime}))
, and
\pi^{\prime}|_{[q,q^{\prime}]}
is the unique geodesic from
(q,\pi^{\prime}(q))
to
(q^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}(q^{\prime}))
. Therefore
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
must agree on the half-open interval

[\inf(W\cap[q,q^{\prime}]),\sup(W\cap[q,q^{\prime}])).

Since
W
is nonempty and unbounded below, and
q,q^{\prime}
were arbitrary, this implies that
\pi(z)=\pi^{\prime}(z)
for all sufficiently negative
z
.

Proof of (iv) for
x>0
:
We start with (
42
). Since
x>0
, we may replace
(x-\epsilon)\vee 0
with
x-\epsilon
. Also, without loss of generality, we may assume that
x,y+r\in\mathbb{Q}
. By the monotonicity of the paths
\pi\{x,z\}
in
x
and
z
, to show the statement (
42
), it is enough to find random
X_{1}<x<X_{2}
with
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that the zigzag graphs of

\pi\{X_{1},y\},\pi\{X_{2},y+r\}

overlap. This follows from condition 3 of the coupling.

Equation (
43
) in the finite-
n
case follows from Lemma
3.7
with
k=1
, and the translation of essential disjointness of optimizers across the original Brownian motions to essential disjointness of optimizers across the melon, Lemma
2.11
. To pass to the limiting paths from the finite-
n
statement of Lemma
3.7
, we use that essential disjointness is a closed property in path space.

Proofs of (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv) for
x=0
:
The path
\pi_{R}\{0,y\}:=1
is locally geodesic, and hence is always a geodesic from
0
to
y
by Lemma
4.1
(ii).
We next prove (iv) when
x=0
when
\pi\{0,y\}
is replaced by the path
\pi_{R}\{0,y\}
. We will later show that
\pi_{R}\{0,y\}
is almost surely the unique geodesic from
0
to
y
, proving (i).

The path
\pi_{R}\{0,y\}
is the almost sure limit of the melon optimizers
\pi^{n}\{0,y\}
, which simply follow the top path by Lemma
2.12
. In particular, (
43
) then follows from the exact same argument as in the
x\neq 0
case.

For the first part of (iv), by Corollary
2.22
and the fact that
\pi^{n}\{0,y\}(z)=1
for all
n,y,z
we have
\pi\{\epsilon,y+r\}\to\pi_{R}\{0,y+r\}
almost surely in path space as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. Equation (
42
) follows since
\pi^{R}\{0,y\}=1
.

To prove (i), (ii) and (iii) for
x=0
, we just need to show that almost surely for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\pi_{R}\{0,y\}
is the only geodesic from
0
to
y
. By the first part of (
43
) for the paths
\pi_{R}\{0,y\}
, we can work on an almost sure set where for every
z\in\mathbb{Q}
we have
\pi\{\epsilon,z\}\to\pi_{R}\{0,z\}
almost surely in path space as
\epsilon\to 0^{+},\epsilon\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Now let
y\in\mathbb{R}
, and suppose that
\pi^{\prime}
is any geodesic from
0
to
y
. For any
\epsilon\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
and
z\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(y,\infty)
, monotonicity of geodesics (Proposition
2.1
) and the uniqueness of
\pi\{\epsilon,z\}
implies that
\pi^{\prime}(t)\leq\pi\{\epsilon,z\}(t)
for
t\in(-\infty,y]
. Since
\pi\{\epsilon,z\}\to\pi_{R}\{0,z\}
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, this implies
\pi^{\prime}(t)\leq\pi_{R}\{0,z\}(t)=1
for
t\in(-\infty,y]
and hence
\pi^{\prime}(t)=1=\pi_{R}\{0,y\}(t)
for
t\in(-\infty,y]
.

Proof of (v):
This follows from the fact that rightmost (and leftmost) geodesics are locally rightmost (and leftmost) geodesics, and the corresponding result in the finite case, Proposition
2.1
.
∎

For a parabolic path across
\mathcal{B}
, the definition of its length from (
41
) is not easy to work with, as it involves a
\liminf
of the discrepancy.
Thus we record the following lemma, which follows from (
41
) and Lemma
2.24
, and says that for two parabolic paths that agree off of a compact set, their difference in length can be computed locally.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following statement holds almost surely.
Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be any two parabolic paths across
\mathcal{B}
from any point
x
to any points
z_{1},z_{2}
respectively, such that for some
z_{0}<z_{1}\wedge z_{2}
, we have
\pi_{1}(y)=\pi_{2}(y)
for any
y\leq z_{0}
.
Then

\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi_{1}|_{[z_{0},z_{1}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi_{2}|_{[z_{0},z_{2}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

From this lemma we can deduce the following measurability result. Informally, this result
says that for any parabolic path
\pi
without any ‘jump points’ inside a compact set
[a,b]\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, the length of
\pi
is determined by the values of
\mathcal{B}
outside of
[a,b]\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
(see Figure
6
).

1
2
3
4
5
6
z_{I}
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
\pi^{\prime}
\pi

Figure 6:

An illustration of Lemma
4.4
and its proof: for a parabolic path
\pi
that does not have any jump point in the region
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, its length
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is determined by
\mathcal{B}
outside
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.
The proof compares
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
with the length of
\pi^{\prime}
, a parabolic path ending at
z_{I}
(the left endpoint of
I
).
The difference is determined by
\mathcal{B}
outside of
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
by Lemma
4.3
. Using the definition of path length (
41
),
\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is also determined by
\mathcal{B}
outside of
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Take any compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by all null sets, all
\mathcal{B}_{i}
for
i>k
, and
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}(x):x\not\in I\}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
Take any
x\geq 0
, and let
\Sigma_{x}
be the set of parabolic paths
\pi
from
x
to some
z\in\mathbb{R}
such that either
\pi(y)>k
for any
y\in(-\infty,z]\cap I
, or else
I\subset(-\infty,z]
and
\pi
is constant on
I
. Let
F:\Sigma_{x}\to\mathbb{R}
be the random function recording path length in
\mathcal{B}
:
F(\pi)=\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
. Then
F
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Before proceeding with the proof, let us briefly comment on the technical point that
\mathcal{F}
contains all null sets. This is required to ensure that certain almost sure limits are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. Adding null sets into
\mathcal{F}
does not give us very much extra information since the
\sigma
-algebra generated by null sets contains only the sets
A
with
\mathbb{P}(A)\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

Let
z_{I}
be the left endpoint of
I
.
We first show that the length of any parabolic path
\pi^{\prime}
from
x
to
z_{I}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
From the definition of path length (
41
), it suffices to show that
\mathcal{S}(x,z_{I})
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

By (
20
), for every
y<z_{I}
we have that
\mathcal{S}(x,z_{I})-\mathcal{S}(x,y)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
By (
21
) and translation invariance of
\mathcal{S}
(Lemma
2.23
), outside of a null set we have

\mathcal{S}(x,z_{I})=\lim_{a\to\infty}\frac{1}{a}\int_{z_{I}-a}^{z_{I}}\left(\mathcal{S}(x,z_{I})-\mathcal{S}(x,y)-(x-y)^{2}+\xi\right)dy,

where
\xi
is the expectation of a GUE Tracy-Widom random variable.
This implies that
\mathcal{S}(x,z_{I})
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, thus the length of any parabolic path
\pi^{\prime}
from
x
to
z_{I}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

For a general parabolic path
\pi\in\Sigma_{x}
(i.e.,
\pi
starts and
x
and does not jump in
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
), the idea is to compare the length of
\pi
with the length of some parabolic path ending at
z_{I}
using Lemma
4.3
, and show that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
Indeed, one can construct a parabolic path
\pi^{\prime}
from
x
to
z_{I}
such that
\pi(y)=\pi^{\prime}(y)
for all sufficiently negative
y
; and by Lemma
4.3
applied to the paths
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
we have that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
The conclusion follows.
∎

This result will be used in the proof of Lemma
7.3
below, where we use the Brownian Gibbs property (Theorem
2.14
) on sets of the form
I\times\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
to study distributional properties of parabolic path length.

4.2
Disjoint optimizers in
\mathcal{B}

Now that we have a notion of length of parabolic paths in
\mathcal{B}
, we can define multi-point last passage values. For
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
with
x_{1}\geq 0
, define

\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]=\sup_{\pi}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}:=\sup_{\pi_{1},\dots\pi_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{B}}

(46)

where the supremum is over
k
-tuples of ordered, essentially disjoint parabolic paths from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
. As in the finite case, we call such a collection
\pi
a
disjoint
k
-tuple
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
, we refer to any disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
that attains the above supremum as a
disjoint optimizer
, as long as
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is finite. We say that a
k
-tuple
\pi
is a
local optimizer
if for all
z\leq y_{1}
, the
k
-tuple consisting of the paths
\pi_{i}|_{[z,y_{i}]}
is a disjoint optimizer. Note that the notation (
46
) is similar to the notation for finite last passage values. The two notations are distinguished by the lack of start and end lines in (
46
).

We first focus on understanding the structure of disjoint optimizers in
\mathcal{B}
from distinct starting points
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{k})
with
x_{1}>0
, as such paths are more easily related to geodesics.

Proposition 4.5
.

Take any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{k})
with
x_{1}>0
.

(i)

Suppose that
\pi
is a disjoint optimizer from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
. Then
\pi
is locally optimal.

(ii)

Almost surely there is a unique optimizer
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
in
\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, letting
\pi\{x_{i},0\}
be the geodesic in
\mathcal{B}
from
x_{i}
to
0
, then for every
i
, there exists a (random)
Y\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi\{x_{i},0\}(t)=\pi_{i}(t)
for all
t\leq Y
.

(iii)

Almost surely, the only
k
-tuple
\pi
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
in
\mathcal{B}
which is locally optimal is the unique optimizer from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

The basic idea of the proof is to first look for pairs
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{-})
and
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{+})
with
y_{i}^{-}<y_{i}<y_{i}^{+}
where the optimizers from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\pm}
simply consists of the geodesics from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}^{\pm}
. Results about optimizers from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
can then be inferred using monotonicity and coalescence results.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that all
x_{i},y_{i}
are rational.
For (i), note that if any disjoint optimizer
\pi
were not locally optimal on some interval of lines
\left\llbracket 1,m\right\rrbracket
, then as in the
k=1
case of Lemma
4.1
(ii), we can increase its length
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
by replacing
\pi
on those lines with an optimizer
\pi^{\prime}
.

For (ii), we will work on the set where for all
x\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty)
and
y\in\mathbb{Q}
, there is a unique geodesic
\pi\{x,y\}
from
x
to
y
in
\mathcal{B}
. We also assume that there is a unique optimizer in
\mathcal{B}
from any rational starting location
\mathbf{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})\in(\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{N})^{k}
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
. We can do this by Lemma
2.16
. (In the more general case where
x_{i},y_{i}
are not all rational, we just consider the set with
\mathbb{Q}
replaced by
Q\cup\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}
.

First, by using both parts of (
43
) in Lemma
4.2
(iv), we can find rational
\epsilon>0
and rational points
y_{i}^{\pm}
with

y^{-}_{1}\ll y^{-}_{2}\ll\dots\ll y^{-}_{k}<y_{1}\leq y_{k}\ll y^{+}_{1}\ll y^{+}_{2}\dots\ll y^{+}_{k}

(47)

such that for any
i<j\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, the geodesics
\pi\{x_{i},y^{-}_{i}\}
and
\pi\{x_{j}-\epsilon,y^{-}_{j}\}
are essentially disjoint, as are the geodesics
\pi\{x_{i}+\epsilon,y^{+}_{i}\}
and
\pi\{x_{j},y^{+}_{j}\}
.

Now, by monotonicity of geodesics, Lemma
4.2
(v), for any rational
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
all of the geodesics
\gamma_{\delta}^{-}=\{\pi\{x_{i}-\delta,y_{i}^{-}\}:i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
are essentially disjoint from each other. Similarly, the geodesics
\gamma_{\delta}^{+}=\{\pi\{x_{i}+\delta,y_{i}^{+}\}:i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
are also essentially disjoint. In particular, the disjoint
k
-tuples
\gamma_{\delta}^{\pm}
are optimizers. They are also locally optimal by Lemma
4.1
(ii).

We use these geodesics to prove the existence of an optimizer from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
. We start with a disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi^{1}=(\pi^{1}_{1},\dots,\pi^{1}_{k})
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
. The
k
-tuple
\pi^{1}
can be obtained from the geodesics
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k}
from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
in the following way. Since these paths have different asymptotic directions
x_{i}
, there exists
y^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi_{i}(y)\neq\pi_{j}(y)
for all
y<y^{*}
, and
i\neq j
. Modifying these geodesics in any way for
y>y^{*}
to ensure essential disjointness and ordering gives a finite length
k
-tuple from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
. Each of the paths in this modification has finite length by Lemma
4.3
.

Therefore
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]>-\infty
. Let
\pi^{m}=(\pi^{m}_{1},\dots,\pi^{m}_{k})
be a sequence of
k
-tuples from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
whose lengths converge to the supremum in (
46
). The asymptotic growth rate of parabolic paths guarantees that there exists a sequence
z_{m}\to-\infty
such that

\pi\{x_{i}-1/m,y_{i}^{-}\}(z_{m})<\pi^{m}_{i}(z_{m})<\pi\{x_{i}+1/m,y_{i}^{+}\}(z_{m})

(48)

for all
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
. Next, for each
m
, modify
\pi^{m}
so that
\pi^{m}|_{(-\infty,z_{m}]}
is an optimizer. Doing this can only increase the length, so the new path lengths still converge to the supremal value
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
. Moreover, since the
k
-tuples
\gamma^{\pm}_{1/m}
are locally optimal, (
48
), (
47
), and monotonicity of optimizers (Lemma
2.3
) implies that for
t\in[z_{m},y_{i}^{-}]
, we have

\pi\{x_{i}-1/m,y_{i}^{-}\}(t)\leq\pi^{m}_{i}(t)\leq\pi\{x_{i}+1/m,y_{i}^{+}\}(t).

Now, as
m\to\infty
, each of the path collections
\pi\{x_{i}-1/m,y_{i}^{-}\}
converges to
\pi\{x_{i},y_{i}^{-}\}
in path space. Similarly each of the paths
\pi\{x_{i}+1/m,y_{i}^{+}\}
converges to
\pi\{x_{i},y_{i}^{+}\}
. This, and monotonicity of geodesics implies that the sequence of
k
-tuples
\pi^{m}
is precompact in the product of
k
path spaces, with subsequential limits
\pi
that satisfy

\pi\{x_{i},y_{i}^{-}\}\leq\pi_{i}\leq\pi\{x_{i},y_{i}^{+}\}

(49)

for all
i
, and are locally optimal. This implies that
\pi
is also a
k
-tuple from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
. Since essential disjointness and ordering are preserved under limits,
\pi
is a disjoint
k
-tuple from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
. Also, Lemma
4.1
(iii) implies that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}\geq\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
so
\pi
is a disjoint optimizer.

Next, we establish uniqueness of
\pi
by establishing (iii). This also completes the proof of (ii). Let
\gamma
be another
k
-tuple from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
which is locally optimal, and let
\gamma^{m}=\gamma
for all
m
. By a similar argument as above with
\gamma^{m}
used in place of
\pi^{m}
, the bounds (
49
) also hold with
\gamma_{i}
in place of
\pi_{i}
.
Next, Lemma
4.2
(iii) and (
49
) imply that there exists some
y^{*}
such that for all
i
and all
z<y^{*}
,
\pi_{i}(z)=\gamma_{i}(z)=\pi\{x_{i},0\}(z)
. Therefore
\gamma,\pi
are both locally optimal paths which are equal at their endpoints.

Also, we can find rational points
\{(z_{i},m_{i}):i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}\in(\mathbb{Q}\cap(-\infty,y^{*}))\times\mathbb{N}
such that
\gamma_{i}(z_{i})=\pi_{i}(z_{i})=m_{i}
for all
i
. Since we are working on an almost sure set where there are unique optimizers between all rational starting locations and
(\mathbf{y},1)
, this implies
\gamma=\pi
.
∎

Understanding the structure of optimizers in
\mathcal{B}
from general starting points is more difficult. We will wait until the construction of the extended Airy sheet to do this.

5
Limits of melon optimizers and the extended Airy sheet

We now have the tools to obtain both the scaling limit of
\mathcal{S}^{n}
, and the joint scaling limit of melon optimizers.
We will focus on first understanding the scaling limit of
\mathcal{S}^{n}
on the set

\hat{\mathfrak{X}}=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}:0\leq x_{1}\leq\dots\leq x_{k}\}

Also, let

\hat{\mathbb{Q}}=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathfrak{X}}:x_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty),y_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}\;\;\forall i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket,\text{ and }0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{k}\}.

Note that
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
is the closure of
\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
.
By Theorems
2.13
,
3.1
, and Corollary
3.9
, the functions
\mathcal{B}^{n},\mathcal{S}^{n}|_{\hat{\mathfrak{X}}}
and the paths
\{\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}:(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\}
are jointly tight. Here tightness is with respect to the following topologies:

•

For
\mathcal{B}^{n}
: uniform-on-compact convergence of functions from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

•

For
\mathcal{S}^{n}|_{\hat{\mathfrak{X}}}
: uniform-on-compact convergence of functions from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

•

For each of the paths
\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
: the path space topology defined at the beginning of Section
2
.

Let

\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S},\{\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}:(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\}

be any joint distributional subsequential limit along some subsequence
Y
. In this section, we will understand the joint structure of these limiting objects. We start with a lemma and a proposition.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists a subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
such that almost surely,

(\mathcal{B}^{n},\mathcal{S}^{n},\{\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}:(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\})\to(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S},\{\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}:(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\}),

and for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}
with
0<x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
, there exist rational points
z_{1}<z_{2}<\dots<z_{k}
such that for all large enough
n
, the paths
\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}
are essentially disjoint.

Proof.

For
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
, define the indicator

D^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{1}\left(\{\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}\}_{i=1}^{k}\text{ are essentially disjoint}\right).

We can find a subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
such that the random variables

\mathcal{B}^{n},\mathcal{S}^{n},\{\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}:(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\},\{D^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}):(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\}

converge jointly in distribution. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can couple the environments along
Y^{\prime}
so that this convergence takes place almost surely. Finally, by Lemma
3.7
and Lemma
2.11
, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k}
with
0<x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
and any
\epsilon>0
, we can find
\mathbf{z}
such that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}D_{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\geq 1-\epsilon.

Therefore on this coupling, the paths
\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}
are essentially disjoint for all large enough
n
with probability at least
1-\epsilon
. Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, this holds almost surely for some rational
\mathbf{z}
.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

With notation as above, almost surely the following statements hold.

1.

For all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}].

In particular, by continuity
\mathcal{S}|_{\hat{\mathfrak{X}}}
is a function of
\mathcal{B}
.

2.

For all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
, the
k
-tuple
\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
is the unique optimizer in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

Proof.

First, Corollary
3.9
and Lemma
3.8
ensure that each of the
\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
for
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
is a disjoint
k
-tuple in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
which is locally optimal. Proposition
4.5
(iii) then implies that
\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
is the unique optimizer in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
, yielding statement
2
.

For statement
1
, we first observe that by Theorem
2.19
(i), Lemma
4.1
(ii), and the definition (
46
), we have

\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathcal{B}[x\to y]

for
x>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore
to complete the proof it suffices to show that for every
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
we can find rational points
\mathbf{z}
such that

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}(x_{i},z_{i})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}[x_{i}\to z_{i}].

(50)

To prove (
50
), we work with the subsequence
Y^{\prime}
and the coupling in Lemma
5.1
.
On this coupling, there exists
\mathbf{z}
with
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}
are essentially disjoint for all large enough
n\in Y^{\prime}
. Since essential disjointness is a closed condition, the paths
\pi\{x_{i},z_{i}\}
are also essentially disjoint. Moreover, by Proposition
4.5
(ii), there exists some
T\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
t\leq T
, we have

\pi\{x_{i},z_{i}\}(t)=\pi_{i}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}(t).

(51)

In particular, by Lemma
4.3
,

\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}[x_{i}\to z_{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}|_{[T,y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi\{x_{i},z_{i}\}|_{[T,z_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}},

(52)

Also, (
51
) and the convergence of paths in this coupling implies that there exists
T_{n}\to T
such that for all large enough
n\in Y^{\prime}
, we have

\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}(T_{n})=\pi^{n}_{i}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}(T_{n}).

(53)

When the
\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}
are essentially disjoint, this equality also holds for all
y<T_{n}
. In particular, this holds for all large enough
n
, and so by (
19
),

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}^{n}(x_{i},z_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi^{n}_{i}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}|_{[Y_{n},y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}-\|\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\}|_{[Y_{n},z_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{n}}.

Since the paths
\pi^{n}\{x_{i},z_{i}\},\pi^{n}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
converge to
\pi\{x_{i},z_{i}\},\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
and
\mathcal{B}^{n}
converges uniformly to
\mathcal{B}
, the right-hand side above converges to the right-hand side of (
52
). The left-hand side above converges to the left-hand side of (
50
), yielding (
50
).
∎

Proposition
5.2
uniquely determines
\mathcal{S}
on
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
by continuity. This uniquely determines the distribution of
\mathcal{S}
by translation invariance.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
be the space of continuous functions from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
with the topology of uniform convergence. A random function
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
is an
extended Airy sheet
if

•

\mathcal{S}
can be coupled with a parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{B}
so that

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]

for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
.

•

For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k
, let
T_{c}\mathbf{x}
denote the shifted vector
(x_{1}+c,\dots,x_{k}+c)
. We can think of
T_{c}
as an operator acting on all of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{k}
. In particular,
T_{c}
acts on all of
\mathfrak{X}
and
T_{c}(\mathfrak{X})=\mathfrak{X}
. With this definition, for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}\circ T_{c}.

The above definition clearly yields a unique distribution on
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X},\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, we have the following theorem. This theorem encompasses Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 5.4
.

The prelimits
\mathcal{S}^{n}
converge in distribution to an extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Any subsequential limit
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{S}^{n}
satisfies the first property of Definition
5.3
by Proposition
5.2
. Moreover, for all
c
, translation invariance of Brownian increments guarantees that
\mathcal{S}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}^{n}\circ T_{c}
, and so
\mathcal{S}
also satisfies the second property of Definition
5.3
.
∎

5.1
Properties of the extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}

In this subsection we record a few basic properties of the extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
, and use these properties to better understand the structure of optimizers in
\mathcal{B}
. The culmination of this section will be a proof of (the remaining parts of) Theorem
1.3
. We start with basic symmetries. For this lemma recall that for
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with a slight abuse of notation we write
-\mathbf{x}=(-x_{k},\dots,-x_{1})
.

Lemma 5.5
.

The extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
satisfies

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(-\mathbf{y},-\mathbf{x}),

jointly in all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
.
Moreover, the parabolically shifted sheet

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}

is stationary in the sense that for any
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathcal{R}(T_{c_{1}}\mathbf{x},T_{c_{2}}\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

jointly in all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
. Here the shifts
T_{c_{i}}
are as in Definition
5.3
.

Proof.

The first distributional equality follows from the distributional equality

B(\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}B(1)-B(1-\cdot)

for Brownian motion. By the second part of Definition
5.3
, it is enough to prove the second equality when
c_{1}=0
. Let
\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}
and
\alpha_{n}=1+2c_{2}n^{-1/3}
. By Brownian scaling,

\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\alpha_{n}^{-1/2}\mathcal{R}^{n}(\alpha_{n}\mathbf{x},T_{c_{2}}\alpha_{n}\mathbf{y})+e_{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

jointly in
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, where the error term
e_{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
term is deterministic and converges to
0
uniformly on compact sets. Therefore since
\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{R}
in the uniform-on-compact topology,
\mathcal{R}
is continuous, and
\alpha_{n}\to 1
, we also have
\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathbf{x},T_{c_{2}}\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Using Theorem
5.4
to pass Lemma
3.5
to the limit, we get the following result for the parabolically shifted sheet
\mathcal{R}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Take any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}<1
, and
a>0
.
Then

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|>a\sqrt{\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}})<ce^{-da^{3/2}},

for some constants
c,d>0
depending only on
k
.

We will use this continuity bound to show that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
. First, we record an analogue of Lemma
2.4
from
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Take any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that
x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime}\geq 0
, and define
\mathbf{x}^{\ell},\mathbf{y}^{\ell},\mathbf{x}^{r},\mathbf{y}^{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
by
x^{\ell}_{i}=x_{i}\wedge x_{i}^{\prime}
,
y^{\ell}_{i}=y_{i}\wedge y_{i}^{\prime}
, and
x^{r}_{i}=x_{i}\vee x_{i}^{\prime}
,
y^{r}_{i}=y_{i}\vee y_{i}^{\prime}
, for each
1\leq i\leq k
.
Then

\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}^{\ell}\to\mathbf{y}^{\ell}]+\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}^{r}\to\mathbf{y}^{r}]\geq\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]+\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}].

Proof.

First, the inequality is trivial if either
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
or
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]
is
-\infty
, so we may assume both are finite. Let
\pi_{n},\pi_{n}^{\prime}
be sequences of disjoint
k
-tuples from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
whose weights converge to
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}],\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]
as in (
46
).
As in the proof of Lemma
2.4
, we define disjoint
k
-tuples
\tau_{n}^{\ell},\tau_{n}^{r}
from
\mathbf{x}^{\ell}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\ell}
,
\mathbf{x}^{r}
to
\mathbf{y}^{r}
,
by (for each
1\leq i\leq k
) setting
\tau_{n,i}^{\ell}=\pi_{n,i}\wedge\pi_{n,i}^{\prime}
,
\tau_{n,i}^{r}=\pi_{n,i}\vee\pi_{n,i}^{\prime}
on
(-\infty,y_{i}^{\ell}]
,
and setting
\tau^{r}_{n,i}
to be either
\pi_{n,i}
or
\pi_{n,i}^{\prime}
on
(y_{i}^{\ell},y_{i}^{r}]
, depending on whether
y^{r}_{i}
equals
y_{i}
or
y_{i}^{\prime}
.
Then as in the proof of Lemma
2.2
, one can check that
\tau_{n}^{\ell},\tau_{n}^{r}
are disjoint
k
-tuples in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}^{\ell}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\ell}
and from
\mathbf{x}^{r}
to
\mathbf{y}^{r}
, respectively.
To prove the lemma, we just need to show that

\|\tau_{n}^{\ell}\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\|\tau_{n}^{r}\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\|\pi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\|\pi^{\prime}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

(54)

Indeed, for any parabolic path
\pi
from some
x\geq 0
to
z\in\mathbb{R}
, and
y\leq z
, we denote

P(\pi,y)=\|\pi|_{[y,z]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\mathcal{B}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z,1)]+\mathcal{S}(x,z).

Then from the definition of the path length (
41
), we just need to verify that

\lim_{y\to-\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k}P(\tau_{n,i}^{\ell},y)+P(\tau_{n,i}^{r},y)-P(\pi_{n,i},y)-P(\pi_{n,i}^{\prime},y)=0.

This follows from Lemma
2.24
and the fact that for any
y\leq y^{\ell}_{1}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\tau_{n,i}^{\ell}|_{[y,y^{\ell}_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\|\tau_{n,i}^{r}|_{[y,y^{r}_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi_{n,i}|_{[y,y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\|\pi_{n,i}^{\prime}|_{[y,y_{i}^{\prime}]}\|_{\mathcal{B}}=0.\qed

We next study the extended Airy sheet and optimizers in
\mathcal{B}
for all endpoints in
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
(rather than just
\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
).

Proposition 5.8
.

The function
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
is continuous on
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
. In particular,

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]

for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
. Moreover, almost surely, for any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
there is an optimizer in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

Proposition
5.8
is the final piece of Theorem
1.3
. The proof is lengthy and a bit nuanced, so we give a sketch here.

We know that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
for
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\mathcal{S}
is continuous. Moreover, it is not difficult to show that
\mathcal{B}
is upper semicontinuous using Lemma
4.1
(iii). Therefore the main goal is to show that
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]\leq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for general
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. We first prove this for rational
\mathbf{x}
with
0<x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
. In this case, we can upper bound
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
by
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]+\epsilon
for a nearby rational
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
by making small modifications to a candidate optimizer from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

For general
\mathbf{x}
, by the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
5.7
) and an approximation of
\mathbf{x}
by a rational sequence, we have that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
is non-decreasing in
\mathbf{y}
.
Then it is enough to find a sequence
\mathbf{z}_{n}\to(-\infty,\dots,-\infty)
with

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}_{n})-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}_{n}]\geq 0.

(55)

The basic idea here is to choose the
\mathbf{z}_{n}
so that with probability tending to
1
with
n
, we have

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}(x_{i},z_{n,i})\geq\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}_{n}].

Note that there is a subtlety here because we need (
55
) to hold for all
\mathbf{x}
simultaneously. We work around this with an approximation argument, making use of the strong modulus of continuity on
\mathcal{S}
from Lemma
5.6
.

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Throughout the proof, we fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
as the size of the set of points
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
we work with. All points
\mathbf{x}
have
x_{1}\geq 0
.
By Proposition
5.2
,
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
almost surely coincides with the continuous function
