\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
at all points in
\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
, and there are unique optimizers
\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
in
\mathcal{B}
for all these points by Proposition
4.5
. Now consider an arbitrary point
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
. We can approximate
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
by a sequence of points
(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
x_{n,i}>x_{i}
and
y_{n,i}>y_{i}
for all
i
. Now, the collection of optimizers
\{\pi\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}:(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathbb{Q}}\}
is monotone in
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
; this is inherited from the prelimiting monotonicity, which follows from Lemma
2.3
.
Therefore as in the proof of Lemma
4.2
(ii), monotonicity of optimizers guarantees that the
k
-tuples
\pi\{\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}\}
have a limit, which is itself a disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi_{R}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.
Lemma
4.1
(iii) implies that

\|\pi_{R}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\geq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}_{n}\to\mathbf{y}_{n}].

Therefore
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]\geq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\hat{\mathfrak{X}}
. If we can show the opposite inequality, then the path
\pi_{R}\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\}
is an optimizer, and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is continuous, this will complete the proof of the proposition.

For this, we first prove that for a fixed rational
\mathbf{x}
with
0<x_{1}<\dots<x_{k}
, we have
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]=\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]>\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for some
\mathbf{y}
. Then by continuity of
\mathcal{S}
, there is an
\epsilon>0
and a disjoint
k
-tuple
\pi
in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
with

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}>\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})+\epsilon

(56)

for all
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
with
|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|<\epsilon
. Now, Lemma
4.3
and the continuity of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{S}
ensures that there exists a rational
k
-tuple
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}<\mathbf{y}
(coordinatewise) and
|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|<\epsilon
such that the path
\pi^{\prime}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
defined by
\pi^{\prime}_{i}=\pi_{i}|_{(-\infty,y_{i}^{\prime}]}
satisfies

\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}>\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}-\epsilon.

This is greater than
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
by (
56
).
On the other hand,
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]\geq\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]
, giving a contradiction.

Now consider general
\mathbf{x}
and let
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Consider a sequence of rational
\mathbf{x}_{n}
with
0<x_{n,1}<\dots<x_{n,k}
such that
x_{n,i}\downarrow x_{i}
. By Lemma
5.7
, we have

\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]\leq\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}_{n}\to\mathbf{y}]-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}_{n}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}].

Since the
\mathbf{x}_{n}
have distinct positive rational entries, the right-hand side above is equal to the same difference with
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\cdot)
in place of
\mathcal{B}[\cdot\to\cdot]
. Therefore by continuity of
\mathcal{S}
, we have

\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}^{\prime}]\leq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

(57)

for all
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{y}
. To complete the proof that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\geq\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]
it just suffices to show that we can find a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n}
such that
z_{n,i}^{-}\to-\infty
as
n\to\infty
for all
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, and

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n})-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n}]\geq 0

(58)

for every
\mathbf{x}
.
Indeed, for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, (
57
) gives that

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]\geq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}_{n}^{-})-\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}_{n}^{-}]

for all large enough
n
, so (
58
) gives that
\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}]\leq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, as desired.

Let
\mathbf{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
be any sequence of points with distinct positive rational entries, such that any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
satisfies
|\mathbf{x}_{n}-\mathbf{x}|<n^{-1/k}
for infinitely many
n
. The fact that we can find such a sequence is a consequence of the fact that the Lebesgue measure of the ball
B(\mathbf{x}_{n},n^{-1/k})
is
O(1/n)
as
n\to\infty
, and hence the sum over
n
is infinite.
By equation (
43
) in Lemma
4.2
, we can find a sequence of deterministic points
\mathbf{z}_{n}^{-}
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n})=\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}_{n}\to\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}[x_{n,i}\to z^{-}_{n,i}]\right)\to 1

(59)

as
n\to\infty
, and for every
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
as
n\to\infty
we have
z_{n,i}^{-}\to-\infty
. Moreover, the two-point estimate Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
3.3
gives that

\sup_{|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}_{n}|\leq n^{-1/k}}|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n})-\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n})|\leq C_{n}n^{-1/{2k}}

(60)

for a sequence of constants
C_{n}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(C_{n}>a)\leq ce^{-da^{3/2}}
for constants
c,d
that do not depend on
n
. This strong tail control on
C_{n}
ensures that the right-hand side of (
60
) converges to
0
almost surely as
n\to\infty
, and hence so does the left-hand side. Similarly,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}_{n}|\leq n^{-1/k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{R}(x_{n,i},z^{-}_{n,i})-\mathcal{R}(y_{i},z^{-}_{n,i})|=0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

(61)

Combining (
59
), the convergence of (
60
), and (
61
) with the fact that any point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
satisfies
|\mathbf{x}_{n}-\mathbf{x}|<n^{-1/k}
infinitely often implies that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{R}(x_{i},z^{-}_{n,i})=0,

and so after removing the parabolic correction,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}(x_{i},z^{-}_{n,i})\geq 0.

Now,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{S}(x_{i},z^{-}_{n,i})\geq\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}^{-}_{n}]
by the definition of parabolic path weight, yielding (
58
).
∎

The relationship between
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{B}
is particularly tractable when the start point
\mathbf{x}=0^{k}
. This proposition immediately gives the relationship (
8
).

Proposition 5.9
.

Almost surely the following holds. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we have

\mathcal{S}(0^{k},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{B}_{i}(y_{1})+\mathcal{B}[(y_{1}^{k},k)\to(\mathbf{y},1)].

(62)

Moreover, there is a disjoint optimizer
\pi
in
\mathcal{B}
from
0^{k}
to
\mathbf{y}
that only uses the top
k
lines.

Proof.

Equation (
62
) is true in the prelimit by Lemma
2.12
, and hence holds in the limit as well. The ‘Moreover’ claim follows by an explicit construction. Let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k})
be given by
\pi_{i}|_{(-\infty,y_{1})}=i
and
\pi_{i}|_{[y_{1},y_{i}]}=\tau_{i}
, where
\tau
is a disjoint optimizer from
(y_{1}^{k},k)
to
(\mathbf{y},1)
. We claim that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}
is equal to the right-hand side of (
62
). The result will then follow from Proposition
5.8
.

By (
41
) and the fact that
\mathcal{S}(0,y_{i})=\mathcal{B}_{1}(y_{i})
for all
i
, it is enough to show that

\mathcal{B}[(z,i)\to(y_{i},1)]-[\mathcal{B}_{1}(y_{i})-\mathcal{B}_{i}(z)]\to 0

(63)

as
z\to-\infty
. For any interval
[n,n+1]\subset[z,y_{i}]
, the left-hand side above is always bounded between
I_{n}:=\sup_{x\in[n,n+1]}\mathcal{B}_{i}(x)-\mathcal{B}_{1}(x)
and
0
.
This is because the left-hand side of (
63
) can be written as

\sup_{z\leq z_{i-1}\leq\cdots\leq z_{1}\leq y_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathcal{B}_{j+1}(z_{j})-\mathcal{B}_{j}(z_{j}).

The support of
I_{n}
contains
0
by the Brownian Gibbs property (Theorem
2.14
), and
I_{n}
is a stationary, ergodic process by the main result of
[
11
]
. Hence
\liminf_{n\to-\infty}I_{n}=0
, yielding (
63
).
∎

5.2
Metric composition law

To construct the full scaling limit of multi-point Brownian LPP from the extended Airy sheet, a key property is the following metric composition law. Recall from the introduction that if
\mathcal{S}
is an extended Airy sheet, then
s\mathcal{S}(s^{-2}\mathbf{x},s^{-2}\mathbf{y})
is an
extended Airy sheet of scale
s
.

Proposition 5.10
.

For
s_{1},s_{2}>0
, take independent extended Airy sheets
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2}
of scale
s_{1},s_{2}
, respectively. Then almost surely, for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
the maximum

\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}\mathcal{S}_{1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})+\mathcal{S}_{2}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y})

(64)

exists. Moreover,
\mathcal{S}
is an extended Airy sheet of scale
(s_{1}^{3}+s_{2}^{3})^{1/3}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
s_{1}^{3}+s_{2}^{3}=1
.
We set up multi-point Brownian LPP converging to an extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
as in Theorem
5.4
. Let
B^{n}
be a collection of
n
independent two-sided standard Brownian motions, and let
\mathcal{S}^{n}
be the prelimiting extended Airy sheet defined using last passage percolation across
B^{n}
(as in Theorem
1.2
) so that
\mathcal{S}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}
as
n\to\infty
.
We now let

\mathcal{S}^{n}_{1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=n^{1/6}\\
\times\left(B[(2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n)\to(s_{1}^{3}+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{y},n-\lfloor s_{1}^{3}n\rfloor)]-2ks_{1}^{3}\sqrt{n}-n^{1/6}\sum_{i=1}^{k}2(y_{i}-x_{i})\right),

and

\mathcal{S}^{n}_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=n^{1/6}\\
\times\left(B[(s_{1}^{3}+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n-\lfloor s_{1}^{3}n\rfloor-1)\to(1+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{y},1)]-2ks_{2}^{3}\sqrt{n}-n^{1/6}\sum_{i=1}^{k}2(y_{i}-x_{i})\right),

for any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Then
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{2}
are independent.
Using Theorem
5.4
, Brownian scaling, and continuity of
\mathcal{S}
, we have
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}_{2}
as
n\to\infty
.
On the other hand, by Lemma
2.9
we have

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}\mathcal{S}^{n}_{1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})+\mathcal{S}^{n}_{2}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y})

for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. This passes to the limit as a long as the
\argmax
for the right-hand side above is tight.
This tightness for a fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
follows from Lemma
5.11
below. Uniform tightness when
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
are allowed to range over a compact subset of
\mathfrak{X}
then follows from monotonicity of optimizers (Lemma
2.3
).
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist constants
c,d>0
such that the following is true.
Let
n,p,q\in\mathbb{N}
, with
p+q=n
, and denote
t=p/n
.
Take independent standard Brownian motions
B^{n}=(B^{n}_{1},\ldots,B^{n}_{n})
, and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/6}
.
For any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
define

A(\mathbf{z})=B^{n}[(2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n)\to(t+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{z},q+1)]+B^{n}[(t+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{z},q)\to(1+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{y},1)].

(65)

We set
A(\mathbf{z})=-\infty
if the right-hand side is not defined.
Then for any
a>0
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-dr^{3/2}}
the following is true:
for any
\mathbf{z}^{*}
where
A
achieves its maximum, we must have
\|\mathbf{z}^{*}-t\mathbf{y}-(1-t)\mathbf{x}\|_{2}<ca^{2}(t\wedge(1-t))^{1/3}
.

This lemma is an analogue of
[
15
, Lemma 9.3]
.
Its proof is also similar to the proof of
[
15
, Lemma 9.3]
, involving some technical estimates on the Brownian
n
-melon
W^{n}
. We leave it to Appendix
A
.

6
The scaling limit of multipoint Brownian LPP

6.1
Tightness of the prelimiting extended landscape

Recall from the introduction that

\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}=\{(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R})^{2}:s<t\}.

Let
B=(B_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an infinite sequence of independent two-sided standard Brownian motions. As in the introduction, let
(\mathbf{x},s)_{n}=(s+2\mathbf{x}n^{-1/3},-{\left\lfloor sn\right\rfloor})
, and define the prelimiting extended landscape

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=n^{1/6}\left(B[(\mathbf{x},s)_{n}\to(\mathbf{y},t)_{n}]-2k(t-s)\sqrt{n}-n^{1/6}\sum_{i=1}^{k}2(y_{i}-x_{i})\right).

(66)

This is a random function on
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
.
In this section we prove that
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is tight in an appropriate function space.
Given Lemma
3.5
, it remains to prove a two-point tail bound on the deviation of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
in the time direction. This is the analogue of
[
15
, Lemma 11.2]
.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the stationary version of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, defined as

\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}}{t-s}.

Lemma 6.1
.

Take any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and
t\in n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, such that
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/100}
,
1/2\leq t<1-n^{-1/100}
. Also take
0<a<n^{1/150}
.
Letting
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=t\mathbf{x}+(1-t)\mathbf{y}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)|>a(1-t)^{1/3}|\log(1-t)|)<ce^{-da^{9/8}},

for some constants
c,d
depending only on
k
.

We sketch the idea of the proof of this lemma here.
The complete proof is a technical computation and uses similar ideas to the proof of
[
15
, Lemma 11.2]
, so we leave it to Appendix
A
(with the proof of Lemma
5.11
).
For the upper tail of
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)
, via the triangle inequality it suffices to give a lower bound on
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t+n^{-1};\mathbf{y},1)
. This follows from Lemma
2.8
and tail bounds on points the Brownian melon, see Lemma
A.4
.
For the lower tail, by the metric composition law we need to upper bound

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}(\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t))+\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{z},t;\mathbf{y},1).

The term
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{z},t;\mathbf{y},1)
can be bounded with a curvature estimate on the Brownian melon. When
\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}
is large, such a curvature estimate also works to bound
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)
, and the aformentioned Lemma
A.4
can be used to bound
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t))
below.
When
\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}
is small we apply the more refined spatial continuity estimate on the prelimiting extended Airy sheets from Lemma
3.5
to bound the difference
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
.
Putting together these bounds gives the desired result.

We now move to tightness. Let
\mathfrak{F}
be the space of functions from
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
to
\mathbb{R}
that are either continuous, or of the form (
66
) for some
n
and some bi-infinite sequence of continuous functions
f
in place of
B
. This is a Polish space, and so all classical theorems about distributional convergence apply. All of the
\mathcal{L}_{n}
are random functions on this space.

Proposition 6.2
.

The functions
\mathcal{L}_{n}
are tight in
\mathfrak{F}
, and all subsequential limits are almost surely continuous.

Proof.

Fix a compact set
K\subset(\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R})^{2}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. It suffices to show tightness of
\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{K}
.
First, we replace
\mathcal{L}_{n}
by a continuous version
\mathcal{J}_{n}
on
K
.
For each
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
and
s
with
s\in n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, define the function
\mathcal{J}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},\cdot)
by setting
\mathcal{J}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)
whenever
t\in n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}

and by linear interpolation at times in between. Then for each
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we can define
\mathcal{J}_{n}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)
by linear interpolation between values when
s\in n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
. This procedure gives a well-defined continuous function on
K
for large enough
n
. By Theorem
2.13
,
\mathcal{J}_{n}(0^{k},0;0^{k},1)
is tight in
n
.
Moreover, by Lemma
3.5
, Lemma
6.1
, and translation and scale invariance properties of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
we get that for all
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in K
and large enough
n
,

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{J}_{n}(\mathbf{u})-\mathcal{J}_{n}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})|>a\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{1/3-\epsilon})\leq ce^{-da^{9/8}}

for any
a,\epsilon>0
. Here
c,d>0
are
K
-dependent constants. Using the Kolmogorov-Chentsov criterion, see
[
31
, Corollary 14.9]
, we get that the sequence
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is tight.
∎

6.2
The explicit construction of
\mathcal{L}^{*}

In this subsection, we construct the scaling limit
\mathcal{L}^{*}
of multipoint Brownian LPP axiomatically and prove Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
. We call this object an extended
∗
directed landscape, or extended
∗
landscape for brevity. Later we will show that this object coincides with the extended directed landscape as defined in Definition
1.1
.

Definition 6.3
.

An
extended
∗
directed landscape
is a random continuous function
\mathcal{L}^{*}
taking values in the space
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow},\mathbb{R})\subset\mathfrak{F}
of continuous functions from
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
to
\mathbb{R}
with the uniform-on-compact topology. It satisfies the following properties.

I.

(Indepedendent extended Airy sheet marginals)
For any disjoint time intervals
\{(s_{i},t_{i}):i\in\{1,\dots k\}\}
, the random functions

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s_{i};\mathbf{y},t_{i}),\quad i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket

are independent extended Airy sheets of scale
(t_{i}-s_{i})^{1/3}
.

II.

(Metric composition law) For any
r<s<t
, almost surely we have that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t),

for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}
.

Note that
\mathcal{L}^{*}|_{\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}}
is the usual directed landscape, since extended Airy sheets are simply Airy sheets when restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

While
\mathcal{L}^{*}
can be constructed directly similarly to how the directed landscape was constructed in
[
15
, Section 10]
, we will instead show its existence by proving that it is the scaling limit of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
.
The next result encompasses Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
.

Theorem 6.4
.

The extended
∗
landscape
\mathcal{L}^{*}
exists and is unique in law. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}^{*}
as random functions in
\mathfrak{F}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
follows since conditions I and II specify all finite dimensional distributions. Indeed, let
\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{k}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
be any collection of points with time indices
S=\{s_{i}<t_{i}:i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
. Let
r_{1}<\dots<r_{\ell}
denote the order statistics of the set
S
, for some
2\leq\ell\leq 2k
. Then the marginals
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,r_{i};\cdot,r_{i+1}),i\in\left\llbracket 1,\ell-1\right\rrbracket
are independent Airy sheets of scale
(r_{i+1}-r_{i})^{1/3}
by I. All the random variables
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{u}_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{u}_{k})
are measurable functions of
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,r_{i};\cdot,r_{i+1}),i\in\left\llbracket 1,\ell-1\right\rrbracket
by repeated applications of II.

Next, we know
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is tight in
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow},\mathbb{R})
by Proposition
6.2
. Let
\mathcal{M}:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
be any subsequential limit of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The function
\mathcal{M}
has independent increments by the independence of the Brownian motions that give rise to
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. These increments must be rescaled extended Airy sheets by Theorem
5.4
, and satisfy metric composition since
\mathcal{L}_{n}
does, and maximizer locations are tight (Lemma
5.11
). Therefore
\mathcal{M}
is an extended
∗
landscape.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we gather continuity estimates for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.
Let
\mathcal{K}
be the stationary extended landscape, defined as

\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-y_{i})^{2}}{t-s}.

By passing Lemma
6.1
to the limit, we have the following two-point bound on
\mathcal{K}
(and hence on
\mathcal{L}
) in the time direction. Note that a two-point bound in the spatial direction follows from Lemma
5.6
and rescaling.

Lemma 6.5
.

Take any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and
0<t^{\prime}<t
with
2t^{\prime}\geq t
.
Letting
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=(t^{\prime}/t)\mathbf{x}+(1-t^{\prime}/t)\mathbf{y}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t^{\prime})-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},t)|>a(t-t^{\prime})^{1/3}|\log(1-t^{\prime}/t)|)<ce^{-da^{9/8}},

for all
a>0
. Here
c,d>0
are constants depending only on
k
.

By Lemmas
5.6
and
6.5
, and using Lemma
3.3
, we have that in any compact subset of
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, the function
\mathcal{L}
is
(1/2-\epsilon)
-Hölder in the spatial coordinate and
(1/3-\epsilon)
-Hölder in the time coordinate, for any
\epsilon>0
.

We also need uniform upper and lower bounds on
\mathcal{K}
on
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
.
We first give a one-point bound. This is obtained from passing the bound Lemma
A.4
on Brownian last passage values to the limit.

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},t)|>at^{1/3})<ce^{-da^{3/2}}

for all
a>0
. Here
c,d>0
are constants depending only on
k
.

Next we use Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
6.5
to upgrade Lemma
6.6
to a uniform bound that will be sufficient for our purposes.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
\eta>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a random constant
R>1
, such that for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
, we have

|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)|<RG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}

where

G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)=\left(1+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{y}\|_{1}}{(t-s)^{2/3}}\right)\left(1+\frac{|s|}{t-s}\right)(1+|\log(t-s)|).

Also
\mathbb{P}(R>a)<ce^{-da}
for any
a>0
. Here
c,d>0
are constants depending on
k,\eta
.

Proof.

Fix
\eta>0,k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Throughout this proof we let
c,d
be constants depending on
k,\eta
, whose values can vary from line to line.
For each
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
L_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}
consist of all
(\mathbf{x},s)
, where each coordinate of
\mathbf{x}
is in
2^{2\ell}\mathbb{Z}
and
s\in 2^{3\ell}\mathbb{Z}
.
For any
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t)\in L_{\ell}
, denote

F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)=(1+2^{-2\ell}(\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{y}\|_{1}))(1+2^{-3\ell}(|s|+|t|))(1+|\ell|).

Take any
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t),(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)\in L_{\ell}
with
s<t
, such that
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}
differ at exactly one coordinate, and by exactly
2^{2\ell}
.
By Lemma
5.6
we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)|>aF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell})<ce^{-da^{3/2}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{3\eta/2}}.

(67)

We then consider
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t),(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in L_{\ell}
,
such that
s<t^{\prime}
,
t=t^{\prime}+2^{3\ell}
, and so that
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
satisfy the bound
|y_{i}^{\prime}-((t-t^{\prime})x_{i}+(t^{\prime}-s)y_{i})/(t-s)|\leq 2^{2\ell}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
By Lemmas
5.6
and
6.5
we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t^{\prime})|>aF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell}|\log(2^{3\ell}(t-s)^{-1})|)<ce^{-da^{9/8}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{9\eta/8}}.

(68)

We next consider any
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t)\in L_{\ell}
with
t-s=2^{3\ell}
.
By Lemma
6.6
we have

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)|>aF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell})<ce^{-da^{3/2}F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{3\eta/2}}.

(69)

The right-hand sides of (
67
), (
68
), and (
69
) are summable over all allowable
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t
and
\ell
with sums that decrease at least exponentially in
a
.
In other words, we conclude that there exists a random number
R
, such that
\mathbb{P}(R>a)<ce^{-da}
and the following is true.

1.

For any
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s),(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)\in L_{\ell}
with
s<t
, such that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{x}
differ at exactly one coordinate by
2^{2\ell}
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{y}
differ at exactly one coordinate by
2^{2\ell}
, we have

|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)|<RF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell},

|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)|<RF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell}.

The second bound follows by a symmetric analogue of (
67
) where we vary
\mathbf{x}
rather than
\mathbf{y}
.

2.

For any
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t),(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in L_{\ell}
,
such that
s<t^{\prime}
,
t=t^{\prime}+2^{3\ell}
, and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
satisfy the bound
|y_{i}^{\prime}-((t-t^{\prime})x_{i}+(t^{\prime}-s)y_{i})/(t-s)|\leq 2^{2\ell}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, we have

|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t^{\prime})|<RF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell}|\log(2^{3\ell}(t-s)^{-1})|.

3.

For any
(\mathbf{x},s),(\mathbf{y},t)\in L_{\ell}
with
t-s=2^{3\ell}
, we have

|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)|<RF(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t,\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell}.

Now consider any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
and let
\ell_{0}=\lfloor\log_{8}(t-s)\rfloor-1
. For each
\ell\leq\ell_{0}
, let
s_{\ell}=2^{3\ell}\lceil 2^{-3\ell}s\rceil
,
t_{\ell}=2^{3\ell}\lfloor 2^{-3\ell}t\rfloor
,
and let
\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
be chosen such that
(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell}),(\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)},t_{\ell})\in L_{\ell}
and

|x^{(\ell)}_{i}-((t-s_{\ell})x_{i}+(s_{\ell}-s)y_{i})/(t-s)|\leq 2^{2\ell},\qquad|y^{(\ell)}_{i}-((t-t_{\ell})x_{i}+(t_{\ell}-s)y_{i})/(t-s)|\leq 2^{2\ell}

(70)

for each
1\leq i\leq k
.
As
\ell\to-\infty
we have
(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell},t_{\ell})\to(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)
.
Therefore by the continuity of
\mathcal{K}
and the triangle inequality we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)|\leq&\;|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell_{0})},s_{\ell_{0}};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell_{0})},t_{\ell_{0}})|\\
&+\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{-\infty}|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)},t_{\ell})-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)},t_{\ell-1})|\\
&+|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)},t_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell-1)},s_{\ell-1};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)},t_{\ell-1})|.\end{split}

(71)

Using the above bounds, we have

|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell_{0})},s_{\ell_{0}};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell_{0})},t_{\ell_{0}})|<RF(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell_{0})},\mathbf{y}^{(\ell_{0})},s_{\ell_{0}},t_{\ell_{0}},\ell_{0})^{\eta}2^{\ell_{0}}<cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3},

(72)

where the last inequality is by the fact that
2^{-3\ell_{0}}(t-s)
and
2^{-3\ell_{0}}(t_{\ell_{0}}-s_{\ell_{0}})
are upper and lower bounded by constants, and (
70
).
For each
\ell\leq\ell_{0}
, we can find a sequence

t_{\ell}=t_{\ell,1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{\ell,m}=t_{\ell-1}
, and

\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)}=\mathbf{y}^{(\ell,1)},\cdots,\mathbf{y}^{(\ell,m)}=\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k}
for some
m\leq c
, such that for each
1\leq j<m
, one of the following two events happens:

1.

t_{\ell,j}=t_{\ell,j+1}+2^{3\ell}
, and
|y_{i}^{(\ell,j+1)}-((t_{\ell,j}-t_{\ell,j+1})x_{i}^{(\ell)}+(t_{\ell,j+1}-s_{\ell})y_{i}^{(\ell,j)})/(t_{\ell,j}-s_{\ell})|\leq 2^{2\ell}
for each
1\leq i\leq k
.

2.

t_{\ell,j}=t_{\ell,j+1}
and
\mathbf{y}^{(\ell,j)}
differ from
\mathbf{y}^{(\ell,j+1)}
at exactly one coordinate by
2^{2\ell}
.

Thus we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)},t_{\ell})&-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)},t_{\ell-1})|\\
<&\;\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}RF(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell,j)},\mathbf{y}^{(\ell,j)},s_{\ell},t_{\ell,j},\ell)^{\eta}2^{\ell}|\log(2^{3\ell}(t-s)^{-1})|\\
<&\;cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},s,t)^{\eta}\left(\frac{1+|\ell|}{1+|\ell_{0}|}\right)^{\eta}2^{\ell}(1+\ell_{0}-\ell).\end{split}

For
|\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)},s_{\ell};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)},t_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell-1)},s_{\ell-1};\mathbf{y}^{(\ell-1)},t_{\ell-1})|
, arguing similarly we get that the same bound holds. Combining these bounds, summed over all
\ell\leq\ell_{0}
, with the bound (
72
) and the triangle inequality (
71
), gives the result.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
6.7
, we can estimate the location of the maximizer in the metric composition law for the extended landscape.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any small enough
\eta>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, take the random variable
R>1
and the function
G
from Lemma
6.7
.
For any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
r<s<t
, if

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)
, then

\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2}<cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}(s-r)^{1/3},

where
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}=((t-s)\mathbf{x}+(s-r)\mathbf{y})/(t-r)
and
c
is a constant depending only on
\eta,k
.

Proof.

In this proof we let
c
denote a large constant depending on
k,\eta
, whose value may change from line to line.
By Lemma
6.7
we have

0=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)<\frac{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}}{t-r}-\frac{\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}}{s-r}-\frac{\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}}{t-s}\\
+RG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},r,s)^{\eta}(s-r)^{1/3}+RG(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y},s,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}+RG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-r)^{1/3}\\
=-\frac{(t-r)\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2}^{2}}{(t-s)(s-r)}+R(G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},r,s)^{\eta}(s-r)^{1/3}+G(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y},s,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}+G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-r)^{1/3}).

(73)

Now,
\frac{1+|\log(s-r)|}{1+|\log(t-r)|}\leq\frac{t-r}{s-r}
, and
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{y}\|_{1}\geq\frac{s-r}{t-r}(\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{1})
,
so from the definition of
G
we have

\begin{split}\frac{G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},r,s)}{G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)}&=\;\frac{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{z}\|_{1}}{(s-r)^{2/3}}}{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{y}\|_{1}}{(t-r)^{2/3}}}\times\frac{1+\frac{|r|}{s-r}}{1+\frac{|r|}{t-r}}\times\frac{1+|\log(s-r)|}{1+|\log(t-r)|}\\
&\leq\;\frac{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{z}\|_{1}}{(t-r)^{2/3}}}{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}+\|\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{1}}{(t-r)^{2/3}}}\left(\frac{t-r}{s-r}\right)^{5/3}\times\left(\frac{t-r}{s-r}\right)\times\left(\frac{t-r}{s-r}\right)\\
&\leq\;\left(1+\left|\frac{\|\mathbf{z}\|_{1}-\|\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{1}}{(t-r)^{2/3}}\right|\right)\left(\frac{t-r}{s-r}\right)^{11/3}.\end{split}

Thus we have

\frac{G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},r,s)}{G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)}<c(1+\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2}(t-r)^{-2/3})\left(\frac{t-r}{s-r}\right)^{10},

and similarly

\frac{G(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y},s,t)}{G(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)}<c(1+\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2}(t-r)^{-2/3})\left(\frac{t-r}{t-s}\right)^{10}.

Without loss of generality we assume that
t-s\geq s-r
; thus
(t-r)/2\leq t-s\leq t-r
.
We plug these two estimates into the inequality (
73
).
By taking
\eta<1/30
we have

\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2}^{2}(s-r)^{-1}<cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(1+\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2}(t-r)^{-2/3})^{\eta}(t-r)^{1/3}.

(74)

Now consider the function

f:Z\mapsto Z^{2}(s-r)^{-1}-cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(1+Z(t-r)^{-2/3})^{\eta}(t-r)^{1/3}.

We have that
f(0)<0
, and on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
this function first decreases then increases.
From (
74
), we have that
f(\|\mathbf{z}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\|_{2})<0
.
Also,
f(cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}(s-r)^{1/3})>0
, since

\displaystyle(cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}(s-r)^{1/3})^{2}(s-r)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta})^{2}(t-s)^{2/3}(s-r)^{-1/3}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-r)^{1/3}+(cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta})^{2}(t-s)^{1/3}(s-r)^{1/3}(t-r)^{-1/3}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(1+cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}(t-s)^{1/3}(s-r)^{1/3}(t-r)^{-2/3})^{\eta}(t-r)^{1/3},

where the first inequality is by
cRG(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},r,t)^{\eta}\geq c
and taking
c
large enough, and the second inequality is by taking
\eta<1
.
These imply the conclusion.
∎

We can now show that metric composition holds everywhere in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Almost surely, for every
r<s<t
and
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
we have

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t).

Also, almost surely we have the triangle inequality

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)\geq\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t)

for every
r<s<t,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
.

Proof.

By condition II in Definition
6.3
, we can ensure that almost surely, metric composition holds at all rational times
r<s<t
. The triangle inequality then holds at all rational times. This extends to all times by continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.

Now, let
r<s<t
and
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
. Consider rational sequences
r_{n}\to r,s_{n}\to s,t_{n}\to t
. By the metric composition law at rational times, for every
n
we can find
\mathbf{z}_{n}
such that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r_{n};\mathbf{y},t_{n})=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r_{n};\mathbf{z}_{n},s_{n})+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z}_{n},s_{n};\mathbf{y},t_{n}).

(75)

Lemma
6.8
ensures that all the points
\mathbf{z}_{n}
are contained in a common compact set, and hence we can find a subsequential limit
\mathbf{z}
. Continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
ensures that Equation (
75
) then holds with the
n
’s removed. Combining this with the triangle inequality yields the metric composition law at
r<s<t
and
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.
∎

We finish this section by recording some symmetries of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Take
q>0
,
r,c\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
T_{c}\mathbf{x}
denote the shifted vector
(x_{1}+c,\dots,x_{k}+c)
.
We have the following equalities in distribution for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
as functions in
\mathfrak{F}
.

1.

Stationarity:
\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(T_{c}\mathbf{x},s+r,T_{c}\mathbf{y},t+r).

2.

Flip symmetry:
\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(-\mathbf{y},-t,-\mathbf{x},-s).

3.

Rescaling:
\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}q\mathcal{L}^{*}(q^{-2}\mathbf{x},q^{-3}s,q^{-2}\mathbf{y},q^{-3}t).

4.

Skew symmetry:

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)+(t-s)^{-1}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s,T_{c}\mathbf{y},t)+(t-s)^{-1}\|\mathbf{x}-T_{c}\mathbf{y}\|_{2}^{2}.

Proof.

The first three symmetries of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
can be deduced by the convergence from
\mathcal{L}_{n}
(Theorem
6.4
), since finite versions hold for
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The final symmetry follows from the corresponding symmetry in Lemma
5.5
and the characterization of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
in Definition
6.3
.
∎

7
Paths in the extended landscape

Having constructed
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, our next goal is to understand its optimizers. In this section we introduce both paths and optimizers in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, and prove a selection of basic properties.

7.1
Path weights

We call a continuous function
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
for some interval
[s,t]
a
multi-path of size
k
.
For any multi-path
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, define its
length
in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
by

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\inf_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}=t}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};\pi(t_{i}),t_{i}).

This is the
\mathcal{L}^{*}
-analogue of the formula (
3
).
For any
\pi:[r,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and a sequence
\pi^{(i)}:[r_{i},t_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
\pi^{(i)}\to\pi
in the
dyadic pointwise topology
, if
r_{i}\to r
,
t_{i}\to t
, and
\pi^{(i)}(s)\to\pi(s)
for each
s\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}\cap[r,t]
, where
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is the set of dyadic rational numbers. This is a Polish topology, making it easy to work with probabilistically. Note that the length above can also be defined for discontinuous functions
\pi
. However, almost surely all discontinuous functions will have length
-\infty
by Lemma
6.7
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For a sequence of multi-paths
\{\pi^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and a multi-path
\pi
, such that
\pi^{(i)}\to\pi
in the dyadic pointwise topology, we have
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\|\pi^{(i)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\leq\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\pi
is on
[r,t]
and each
\pi^{(i)}
is on
[r_{i},t_{i}]
.
Take any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and any sequence
r=s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{m}=t
, such that
s_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
for each
0<j<m
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<j<m
we denote
s_{i,j}=s_{j}
, and
s_{i,0}=r_{i}
,
s_{i,m}=t_{i}
.
By the definition of
\|\pi^{(i)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, for all
i
large enough so that
r_{i}<s_{1},s_{m-1}<t_{i}
, we have that

\|\pi^{(i)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{(i)}(s_{i,j-1}),s_{i,j-1};\pi^{(i)}(s_{i,j}),s_{i,j}).

As
i\to\infty
the right-hand side converges to
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s_{j-1}),s_{j-1};\pi(s_{j}),s_{j})
, by the convergence of
\pi^{(i)}
to
\pi
in the dyadic pointwise topology and the continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.
Therefore

\limsup_{i\to\infty}\|\pi^{(i)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s_{j-1}),s_{j-1};\pi(s_{j}),s_{j}).

By the continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
and of
\pi
, this inequality holds even when the points
s_{i}
are not in
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
.
The conclusion then follows from the definition of
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
.
∎

7.2
Optimizers and transversal fluctuation

From the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, and the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
(Proposition
6.9
), for any
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we have that

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\leq\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s),s;\pi(t),t).

(76)

We call a multi-path
\pi
an
optimizer in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
from
(\pi(s),s)
to
(\pi(t),t)
, if equality holds in (
76
). If
\pi
is an optimizer, then

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s),s;\pi(t),t)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};\pi(t_{i}),t_{i})

for any partition
s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}=t
of
[s,t]
. In the case where
k=1
, this defines a geodesic in the directed landscape, since
\mathcal{L}^{*}|_{\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}}=\mathcal{L}
.
We next address the existence and uniqueness of optimizers. We start with a fixed pair of endpoints.

Lemma 7.2
.

Given
(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, almost surely there is a unique optimizer in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.

We need the following result on the uniqueness of the maximum in the metric composition law.

Lemma 7.3
.

Given
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
r<s<t
, almost surely the function
A_{s}(\mathbf{z})=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t)
has a unique maximum.

We leave the proof of this lemma to the end of this subsection, and continue our discussion of existence and uniqueness of optimizers.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

By Lemma
7.3
, almost surely for each rational
s\in(r,t)
, the function
A_{s}(\mathbf{z})=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},s;\mathbf{y},t)
has a unique maximum. Therefore the value of any optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
is uniquely determined at all rational times, and hence the optimizer itself is uniquely determined by continuity.

For existence, we can construct an optimizer
\pi
as follows. Let
\pi(r)=\mathbf{x},\pi(t)=\mathbf{y}
, and for any rational
s\in(r,t)
let
\pi(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
be the unique maximum of
A_{s}
.
Then for any triple
s_{1}<s_{2}<s_{3}\in((r,t)\cap\mathbb{Q})\cup\{r,t\}
we claim that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s_{1}),s_{1};\pi(s_{2}),s_{2})+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s_{2}),s_{2};\pi(s_{3}),s_{3})=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s_{1}),s_{1};\pi(s_{3}),s_{3}).

(77)

Indeed, by the metric composition law there exists
\mathbf{z}^{(1)},\mathbf{z}^{(2)},\mathbf{z}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, such that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{z}^{(1)},s_{1})+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)},s_{1};\mathbf{z}^{(2)},s_{2})+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)},s_{2};\mathbf{z}^{(3)},s_{3})\\
+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z}^{(3)},s_{3};\mathbf{y},t).

The triangle inequality for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
(Proposition
6.9
) and the uniqueness of maxima for the functions
A_{s}
ensures that
\mathbf{z}^{(i)}=\pi(s_{i})
for
i=1,2,3
and enforces equation (
77
).
By Lemma
6.8
,
\pi
is continuous at rational points and at
r,t
. Therefore we can extend
\pi
to a continuous function on
[r,t]
.

Finally we check that for any
r=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}=t
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};\pi(t_{i}),t_{i})=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t).

If for all
0<i<m
,
t_{i}
is rational, this follows by (
77
). This extends to general times
t_{i}
by the continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
. We conclude that
\pi
is an optimizer.
∎

We can upgrade the existence of optimizers to hold simultaneously for all pairs of endpoints, although the same cannot be achieved for uniqueness.

Lemma 7.4
.

Almost surely, for any
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, there is an optimizer in the extended landscape
\mathcal{L}^{*}
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2
, almost surely there is a unique optimizer between any pair of rational endpoints.
For any
\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, we can take a sequence of rational points
(\mathbf{x}^{(i)},s_{i};\mathbf{y}^{(i)},t_{i})\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
converging to
\mathbf{u}
, and let
\pi^{(i)}
be the unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}^{(i)},s_{i})
to
(\mathbf{y}^{(i)},t_{i})
. All these optimizers are Hölder-
(2/3)^{-}
with a common Hölder constant
c
by Lemma
6.8
. Therefore the sequence
\pi^{(i)}
has a subsequential limit
\pi
in the dyadic pointwise topology which is itself Hölder-
(2/3)^{-}
continuous. By Lemma
7.1
and the continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, we have that
\pi
is an optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.
∎

We finish this subsection with the proof of Lemma
7.3
.
We first reduce the problem to understanding the sum of two multi-point last passage values across two independent parabolic Airy line ensembles
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. This is an optimization problem involving parabolic paths across
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
The remainder of the proof is essentially a resampling argument for parabolic paths, similar in spirit to the proof of Lemma
2.15
.

The general idea here is to fix a rational interval
I
, and consider two restricted versions of the optimization problem: one which forces exactly one of the parabolic paths to jump in the interval
I
either on or off of the line
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, and one which does not allow any jumps on or off of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
in the interval
I
. Using the Brownian Gibbs property for
\mathcal{B}
(Theorem
2.14
), we conclude that almost surely these two restricted optimization problems have different maxima.
Now if the function
A_{s}
has two different maxima
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
, then we show that almost surely we can find a rational interval
I
where the two optimizations problems discussed above have the same maximum, yielding a contradiction.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

We now implement the arguments summarized above.

Step 1: Reducing to multi-point last passage values across
\mathcal{B}
.

By symmetries of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
(Lemma
6.10
), we can assume that
r=-1,s=0,t>0
and
x_{1}>0>y_{k}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}^{*}
has independent extended sheet marginals, using symmetries of extended sheets (Lemma
5.5
) we have

(\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},-1;\mathbf{z},0),\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},0;\mathbf{y},t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}),\mathcal{S}_{t}(-\mathbf{y},-\mathbf{z})),

where
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}_{t}
are independent extended sheets of scale
1
and
t^{1/3}
.
Therefore by Proposition
5.8
, we have

A_{s}(\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\cdot]+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[-\mathbf{y}\to-\;\cdot],

(78)

where
\mathcal{B}=\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is independent of
\mathcal{B}
, and

\{x\mapsto t^{-1/3}\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}(t^{2/3}x)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble. We will show that the right-hand side of (
78
) has a unique maximum
\mathbf{z}
. While the argument is similar in spirit to the one used in Lemma
2.15
, there are extra complexities coming from the definition of length for parabolic paths.

Step 2: Setup of paths intersecting/avoiding an interval.

We next define certain collections of parabolic paths that do or not jump in the given interval. We will later show that almost surely, these collections have different maximum weights.

We start by setting up notation for jump times. For a disjoint
k
-tuple of parabolic paths
\pi=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{z}
, let

z[\pi]_{i,m}=\sup\{w\leq z_{i}:\pi_{i}(w)\geq m+1\},

(79)

for any
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and non-negative integer
m
.
In words,
z[\pi]_{i,m}
is the jump time from line
m+1
to line
m
for
\pi_{i}
(when
m\geq 1
), and
z[\pi]_{i,0}=z_{i}
.

For any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, and
j\in\{0,1\}
,
let
\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(j)}
be the collection of all
k
-tuples of essentially disjoint parabolic paths
\pi
in
\mathcal{B}
such that
z[\pi]_{i,j}\in I
, and
z[\pi]_{i^{\prime},m}\not\in I
for any
(i^{\prime},m)\neq(i,j)
.
Also let
\mathcal{P}_{I}^{c}
be the collection of all
k
-tuples of essentially disjoint parabolic paths
\pi
in
\mathcal{B}
such that
z[\pi]_{i^{\prime},m}\not\in I
for any
(i^{\prime},m)
.

In other words,
\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(0)}
,
\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{c}
contain
k
-tuples of essentially disjoint parabolic paths
\pi
, satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(0)}
: the endpoint of
\pi_{i}
is in
I
, and no other endpoint or jump point from line
2
to line
1
is in
I
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(1)}
: the jump point of
\pi_{i}
from line
2
to line
1
is in
I
, and no endpoint or other jump point from line
2
to line
1
is in
I
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{I}^{c}
: no endpoint or jump point from line
2
to line
1
is in
I
.

Now we fix a compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq i\leq k
.
Define
M_{0},M_{1},M_{c}
using the same expression

\sup\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}},

where the supremums are over different sets of pairs of disjoint
k
-tuples
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{z}
and
-\mathbf{y}
to
-\mathbf{z}
for some
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
.
For
M_{0}
, we require that
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(0)}
. For
M_{1}
, we require
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(1)}
. For
M_{c}
, we require
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I}^{c}
. We have no additional restriction on
\pi^{\prime}
.
See Figure
7
for an illustration of the paths.

\mathcal{B}_{1}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{3}
\mathcal{B}_{4}
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{4}^{\prime}
z_{I}
z_{I}^{\prime}
z[\pi]_{2,0}
\pi_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{1}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{3}
\mathcal{B}_{4}
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{4}^{\prime}
z[\pi]_{2,1}
\pi_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{1}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{3}
\mathcal{B}_{4}
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{4}^{\prime}

Figure 7:

An illustration of the paths used in defining
M_{0}
,
M_{1}
, and
M_{c}
(from left to right, respectively). The parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is rotated by
180
degrees for the picture.
The green regions indicate the interval
I
and the lines where there are constraints on jumps.
For
M_{0}
, here we have
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I,2}^{(0)}
; for
M_{1}
, we have
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I,2}^{(1)}
; for
M^{c}
, we have
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I}^{c}
.

Step 3: Almost surely
M_{0}\neq M_{c}
and
M_{1}\neq M_{c}
.

The general idea for this step is to show that conditional on
M_{c}
, the random variable
M_{0}
(or
M_{1}
) has a continuous distribution, using the Brownian Gibbs property for the interval
I
.

We let
\mathcal{F}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by null sets,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, all
\mathcal{B}_{m}
for
m\geq 2
, and
\{\mathcal{B}_{1}(x):x\not\in I\}
.
Then
M_{c}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, since the function recording all lengths of paths
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{c}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable by Lemma
4.4
.

We then deduce that, given
\mathcal{F}
, the random variable
M_{0}
has continuous distribution.
For this, we investigate how
M_{0}
depends on
\mathcal{B}_{1}
in
I
(see the left panel of Figure
7
).
By Lemma
4.3
we can write

M_{0}=\sup_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}+\sup_{x\in I}(\mathcal{B}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(x))-\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{I})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(-z_{I}^{\prime}).

Here
z_{I},z_{I}^{\prime}
are the left and right endpoints of
I
, i.e.,
I=[z_{I},z_{I}^{\prime}]
.
The first supremum above is taken over all
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{I}^{c}
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{-I}^{c}
, where
\pi
is from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{z}
and
\pi^{\prime}
is from
-\mathbf{y}
to
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
, and such that
z_{i}=z_{I},z_{k+1-i}^{\prime}=-z_{I}^{\prime}
, and
z_{j}=-z_{k+1-j}^{\prime}
for any
j\neq i
.
Therefore
M_{0}-\sup_{x\in I}(\mathcal{B}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(x))
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable by Lemma
4.4
. On the other hand,
the Brownian Gibbs property for
\mathcal{B}
(Theorem
2.14
) implies that conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
the law of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
on
I
is absolutely continuous to a Brownian bridge (with diffusion parameter
2
). Therefore conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
, the random variable
\sup_{x\in I}(\mathcal{B}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(x))
almost surely has a continuous distribution, and hence so does
M_{0}
. Since
M_{c}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable,
M_{0}\neq M_{c}
almost surely. The argument to show that
M_{1}\neq M_{c}
almost surely is similar.
Moreover, these inequalities hold almost surely simultaneously for all compact rational intervals
I
and all
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
. Below we assume this probability one event.

Step 4: Contradiction with two maximums.

Now we consider the function
\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}]+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[-\mathbf{y}\to-\mathbf{z}]
. Note that by the metric composition law (Proposition
5.10
) and symmetry of the extended Airy sheet (Lemma
5.5
) this function attains its maximum.
Suppose that the maximum is attained at two points
\mathbf{z}^{(1)}\neq\mathbf{z}^{(2)}
.
We take any disjoint optimizers
\pi^{(1)}
and
\pi^{(2)}
in
\mathcal{B}
, from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{z}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{z}^{(2)}
;
and
\pi^{\prime(1)}
and
\pi^{\prime(2)}
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, from
-\mathbf{y}
to
-\mathbf{z}^{(1)}
and
-\mathbf{z}^{(2)}
. Such optimizers exist by Proposition
5.8
.
Consider the jump times
z[\pi^{(1)}]_{i,0}=z^{(1)}_{i}
,
z[\pi^{(1)}]_{i,1}
, and
z[\pi^{(2)}]_{i,0}=z^{(2)}_{i}
,
z[\pi^{(2)}]_{i,1}
.
By Lemma
7.5
below (and see Figure
8
), almost surely we have

z[\pi^{(1)}]_{1,1}<z[\pi^{(1)}]_{1,0}<z[\pi^{(1)}]_{2,1}<\cdots<z[\pi^{(1)}]_{k,1}<z[\pi^{(1)}]_{k,0},

and

z[\pi^{(2)}]_{1,1}<z[\pi^{(2)}]_{1,0}<z[\pi^{(2)}]_{2,1}<\cdots<z[\pi^{(2)}]_{k,1}<z[\pi^{(2)}]_{k,0}.

Since
\mathbf{z}^{(1)}\neq\mathbf{z}^{(2)}
, we can find a number in the first sequence which does not appear in the second sequence. There are two cases:

1.

We can find
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, such that
z[\pi^{(1)}]_{i,0}=z^{(1)}_{i}
is not in the second sequence.
Then we can find a rational interval
I
that contains
z^{(1)}_{i}
and does not contain any other number in the two sequences.
Thus
\pi^{(1)}\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(0)}
and
\pi^{(2)}\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{c}
.
Then we have that
M_{0}=M_{c}
for such
I
and
i
.

2.

We can find
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, such that
z[\pi^{(1)}]_{i,1}
is not in the second sequence.
Then we can find a rational interval
I
that contains
z[\pi^{(1)}]_{i,1}
and does not contain any other number in the two sequences.
Thus
\pi^{(1)}\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{(1)}
and
\pi^{(2)}\in\mathcal{P}_{I,i}^{c}
.
Then we have that
M_{1}=M_{c}
for such
I
and
i
.

In either case, we get a contradiction with the assumption that
M_{0}\neq M_{c}
,
M_{1}\neq M_{c}
for all compact rational intervals
I
and all
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
.
This means that
A_{s}
cannot have two different maximums.
∎

It remains to prove the following lemma, which says that almost surely, consecutive jumps happen at different locations.

Lemma 7.5
.

Almost surely the following statement is true. Fix
t>0
, and as in the proof of Lemma
7.3
, let
\mathcal{B}
be a parabolic Airy line ensemble,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be independent of
\mathcal{B}
such that

\{x\mapsto t^{-1/3}\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}(t^{2/3}x)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble.

Take any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that
x_{1}>0>y_{k}
.
Let
\mathbf{z}^{*}
be any maximum of

\mathbf{z}\mapsto\mathcal{B}[\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{z}]+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[-\mathbf{y}\to-\mathbf{z}],

and let
\pi^{*},\pi^{\prime*}
be optimizers in
\mathcal{B}
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{z}^{*}
, and in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
from
-\mathbf{y}
to
-\mathbf{z}^{*}
, respectively.
Recall the notation from (
79
), and
let
z^{*}_{i,m}=z[\pi^{*}]_{i,m}
and
-z^{*}_{k+1-i,-m}=z[\pi^{\prime*}]_{i,m}
for any
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
and non-negative integer
m
(see Figure
8
).
Then for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
z^{*}_{i,m}\neq z^{*}_{i,m+1}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, and
z^{*}_{i-1,m}\neq z^{*}_{i,m+1}
for
2\leq i\leq k
.

\mathcal{B}_{1}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{3}
\mathcal{B}_{4}
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{3}^{\prime}
\mathcal{B}_{4}^{\prime}
z^{*}_{3,0}
z^{*}_{3,1}
z^{*}_{3,2}
z^{*}_{3,3}
z^{*}_{2,0}
z^{*}_{2,1}
z^{*}_{2,2}
z^{*}_{2,3}
z^{*}_{1,0}
z^{*}_{1,1}
z^{*}_{1,2}
z^{*}_{1,3}
z^{*}_{1,-1}
z^{*}_{1,-2}
z^{*}_{1,-3}
z^{*}_{2,-1}
z^{*}_{2,-2}
z^{*}_{2,-3}
z^{*}_{3,-1}

Figure 8:

An illustration of the jump times in the statement of Lemma
7.5
.

Since
\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
is countable, it suffices to show that for fixed
(i,m)
, almost surely
z^{*}_{i,m}\neq z^{*}_{i,m+1}
and
z^{*}_{i-1,m}\neq z^{*}_{i,m+1}
(if
i\geq 2
).
The idea is again to use the Brownian Gibbs property, reducing this to the following statement: on a compact interval, two Brownian motions that are correlated (but not identical or trivially related) almost surely attain their maxima at different points.

Proof.

We note that there are interlacing relations
z^{*}_{i,m+1}\leq z^{*}_{i,m}
and
z^{*}_{i-1,m}\leq z^{*}_{i,m+1}
, from the definition and essential disjointness of the parabolic paths.

Now suppose that there is some
\hat{z}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that
\hat{z}=z^{*}_{i,m}=z^{*}_{i,m+1}
or
\hat{z}=z^{*}_{i-1,m}=z^{*}_{i,m+1}
for some
i,m
.
The plan is to use the Brownian Gibbs property to reduce this problem to studying the
\argmax
of Brownian motion.
The interlacing relations impose constraints on the domain of the
\argmax
, so we need to consider all jump times that equal
\hat{z}
.

By looking at all
z^{*}_{i,m}
that equal
\hat{z}
, we can find some integers
m_{-}
and
m_{+}
with
m_{+}>m_{-}
, such that for each
m\in\left\llbracket m_{-},m_{+}\right\rrbracket
, there is at least one jump time equal to
\hat{z}
on line
m
, and there is no jump time equal
\hat{z}
on the lines
m_{-}-1
and
m_{+}+1
.
We then let
\Phi\subset\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
be the set of indices for all jump times equal to
\hat{z}
amongst the lines
\left\llbracket m_{-},m_{+}\right\rrbracket
.
In other words, we define

\Phi=\{(i,m):z^{*}_{i,m}=\hat{z},\;\;m_{-}\leq m\leq m_{+}\},

such that
z^{*}_{i,m_{-}-1}\neq\hat{z}
,
z^{*}_{i,m_{+}+1}\neq\hat{z}
for any
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
.

We note that the number of
\Phi
of this form is countable. It now suffices to prove the following claim.

Claim:
For any fixed
\Phi\subset\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
such that
\Phi\cap(\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times\{m\})
is nonempty if and only if
m\in\left\llbracket m_{-},m_{+}\right\rrbracket
for some
m_{-}<m_{+}
,
almost surely we cannot find a number
\hat{z}
so that
\Phi=\{(i,m):z^{*}_{i,m}=\hat{z},\;\;m_{-}-1\leq m\leq m_{+}+1\}
.

To prove this claim, the general strategy is as follows:

1.

translate this event to properties of the
\argmax
of some linear combination of lines of
\mathcal{B}
;

2.

replace
\mathcal{B}
by independent Brownian motions (with diffusion parameter
2
);

3.

analyze the probability of certain events involving Brownian motions.

Claim Proof.

It suffices to show that, for any such
\Phi
and any rational interval
I
, almost surely the following event does not happen: we can find
\hat{z}\in I^{\prime}
, where
I^{\prime}
is the middle
1/3
of
I
, such that

•

for any
(i,m)\in\Phi
, we have
z^{*}_{i,m}=\hat{z}
,

•

for any
(i,m)\in\left\llbracket m_{-}-1,m_{+}+1\right\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}\setminus\Phi
, we have
z^{*}_{i,m}\not\in I
.

By comparing path lengths using Lemma
4.3
,
this implies that
(\mathcal{B}_{m^{-}},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{+}+1})\in A
, where
A
is the following event defined on sequences of continuous functions
f_{m^{-}},\dots,f_{m^{+}+1}:I\to\mathbb{R}
. The function

g_{f}(\{z_{i,m}\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi})=\sum_{(i,m)\in\Phi}f_{m+1}(z_{i,m})-f_{m}(z_{i,m})

defined on the domain where
z_{i,m}\in I
,
z_{i,m+1}\leq z_{i,m}
and
z_{i-1,m}\leq z_{i,m+1}
for all
i
and
m
attains its maximum when
\{z_{i,m}\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi}=\{\hat{z}\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi}
for some
\hat{z}\in I^{\prime}
. Here when defining
(\mathcal{B}_{m^{-}},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{+}+1})
we let
\mathcal{B}_{m}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1-m}
for
m\leq 0
.

We next apply the Brownian Gibbs property to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, which enables us to replace
(\mathcal{B}_{m^{-}},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{+}+1})
by independent Brownian motions (with diffusion parameter
2
).
Namely, it now suffices to prove that almost surely
B=(B_{m^{-}},\dots,B_{m^{+}+1})\in A
, where the
B_{i}
are independent Brownian motions (with diffusion parameter
2
).

The last step is to prove
\mathbb{P}(B\in A)=0
, by studying the Brownian motions and using inequalities given by the maximum condition.
Consider the functions

S_{1}=\sum_{(i,m)\in\Phi}B_{m+1}-B_{m},\quad S_{2}=B_{m_{0}+1}-B_{m_{0}},

where
(i_{0},m_{0})\in\Phi
is chosen so that
(i_{0},m_{0}-1),(i_{0}+1,m_{0}+1)\not\in\Phi
.
Under the event
A
, for any
z\in I
we have

g_{B}\big(\{\hat{z}\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi}\big)\geq g_{B}\big(\{z\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi}\big),

so
S_{1}(\hat{z})\geq S_{1}(z)
.
We can also just deviate the
(i_{0},m_{0})
coordinate, but now only in one direction since we must preserve the interlacing conditions. Namely, for any
z\in I
,
z\geq\hat{z}
, under the event
A
we have

g_{B}\big(\{\hat{z}\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi}\big)\geq g_{B}\big(\{\hat{z}+\mathbf{1}((i,m)=(i_{0},m_{0}))(z-\hat{z})\}_{(i,m)\in\Phi}\big),

so
S_{2}(\hat{z})\geq S_{2}(z)
.
It remains to show that, for any
\hat{z}\in I^{\prime}
,

\mathbb{P}(S_{2}(\hat{z})\geq S_{2}(z),\forall z\geq\hat{z},z\in I\mid\hat{z}=\argmax_{I}S_{1})=0.

(80)

Note that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are Brownian motions, and we can write
S_{2}=\alpha S_{1}+\beta S_{3}
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta>0
, where
S_{3}
is a Brownian motion independent of
S_{1}
.
However, for any
\hat{z}\in I^{\prime}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\limsup_{z\searrow\hat{z}}\frac{S_{3}(z)-S_{3}(\hat{z})}{S_{1}(\hat{z})-S_{1}(z)}>\alpha\beta^{-1}\mid\hat{z}=\argmax_{I}S_{1}\big)=1.

(81)

This is because, conditional on
\hat{z}=\argmax_{I}S_{1}
and as
z\searrow\hat{z}
, a rescaling of
S_{1}(\hat{z})-S_{1}(z)
converges in distribution to a Bessel process of order
3
. By Blumenthal’s zero–one law, the left-hand side of (
81
) is either zero or one. It cannot be zero since the distribution of the ratio of a Brownian motion over an independent Bessel process of order
3
is unbounded.

From (
81
) we get (
80
), which means that
\mathbb{P}(B\in A)=0
and the conclusion follows.
∎

Given the above claim, since the number of possible
\Phi
is countable, the conclusion follows.
∎

7.3
Monotonicity of optimizers

In this subsection we aim to establish monotonicity for optimizers in the extended landscape.
Some arguments are in parallel to those in Section
2.2
for last passage across lines.
We first establish that leftmost and rightmost optimizers are well-defined. For this lemma we write
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
for two multi-paths
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
if the weak inequality holds pointwise and coordinatewise.

Lemma 7.6
.

The following statement holds almost surely for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.
For any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
,
there are optimizers
\pi^{\ell},\pi^{r}
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, such that
\pi^{\ell}\leq\pi\leq\pi^{r}
for any other optimizer
\pi
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.
We call
\pi^{\ell}
and
\pi^{r}
the leftmost and rightmost optimizers from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
\pi^{(1)}
and
\pi^{(2)}
be two optimizers from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.
Let
\pi^{(3)}=\pi^{(1)}\wedge\pi^{(2)}
and
\pi^{(4)}=\pi^{(1)}\vee\pi^{(2)}
, where
\wedge
and
\vee
are defined pointwise and coordinatewise. Since all optimizers are continuous by Lemma
6.8
,
\pi^{(3)}
and
\pi^{(4)}
are continuous functions from
[t,s]
to
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
.
By the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, the fact that
\mathcal{L}^{*}
has extended Airy sheet marginals, and Lemma
5.7
,
we have that

\|\pi^{(3)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{(4)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\|\pi^{(1)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}.

Since
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)}
are optimizers, this must an equality.
Thus
\pi^{(3)},\pi^{(4)}
are also optimizers.

Now consider any monotone sequence of optimizers
\pi^{(1)}\leq\pi^{(2)},\dots
. By Lemma
6.8
, this sequence has a bounded pointwise limit
\pi^{\prime}
on dyadic rationals, and
\pi^{\prime}
is continuous. This limit is also an optimizer by Lemma
7.1
. Thus by Zorn’s lemma, there is an optimizer
\pi^{\ell}
, such that for any optimizer
\pi
, the condition
\pi\leq\pi^{\ell}
implies
\pi=\pi^{\ell}
. Thus for any optimizer
\pi
, since the multi-path
\pi\wedge\pi^{\ell}
is an optimizer satisfying
\pi\wedge\pi^{\ell}\leq\pi^{\ell}
, we must have
\pi\wedge\pi^{\ell}=\pi^{\ell}
, implying that
\pi^{\ell}\leq\pi
. Therefore
\pi^{\ell}
is the leftmost optimizer.
The existence of the rightmost optimizer follows similarly.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

The following statements hold almost surely.
For any
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
s<t
,
let
\pi
be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
and
\pi^{\prime}
be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
.
Then
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove for the case where
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
are the leftmost optimizers. The rightmost case follows similarly.
Define
\pi^{\ell}=\pi\wedge\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{r}=\pi\vee\pi^{\prime}
.
Then
\pi^{\ell}
and
\pi^{r}
are both continuous multi-paths from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, and from
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
, respectively.

We claim that
\|\pi^{\ell}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
.
Indeed, this follows by the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
and Lemma
5.7
.
However, we also have
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\|\pi^{\ell}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
and
\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\|\pi^{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, by the definition of optimizers.
Therefore
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\|\pi^{\ell}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
and
\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\|\pi^{r}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, and hence
\pi^{\ell},\pi^{r}
are also optimizers.
As
\pi
is the leftmost optimizer, we have
\pi\leq\pi^{\ell}
. On the other hand,
\pi^{\ell}\leq\pi,\pi^{\prime}
from the definition of
\pi^{\ell}
.
Thus
\pi=\pi^{\ell}\leq\pi^{\prime}
.
∎

7.4
Sums of disjoint paths

The goal of this section is to show the following proposition.

Proposition 7.8
.

Almost surely the following statement is true.
Take any
s<t
and any multi-paths
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
\pi^{\prime}:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k^{\prime}}_{\leq}
, such that for any
r\in(s,t)
, we have
\pi_{k}(r)\leq\pi^{\prime}_{1}(r)
.
Let
\pi^{\prime\prime}:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}_{\leq}^{k+k^{\prime}}
be such that
\pi^{\prime\prime}(r)=(\pi(r),\pi^{\prime}(r))
for all
r\in(s,t)
. Then

\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\leq\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}.

Moreover, if
\pi_{k}(r)<\pi^{\prime}_{1}(r)
for all
r\in(s,t)
, then
\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
.

The inequality in Proposition
7.8
is immediate from the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, and the fact that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s,\mathbf{y},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s,\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)\geq\mathcal{L}^{*}((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),s,(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),t)

for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},s,t
for which both sides above make sense. This inequality is inherited from the prelimit
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, where it is clear. To prove the claimed equality in Proposition
7.8
, we require a few lemmas.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
h,t>0
.
Then

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{*}(0^{k},0;0^{k},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(h^{\ell},0;h^{\ell},t)>\mathcal{L}^{*}((0^{k},h^{\ell}),0;(0^{k},h^{\ell}),t))<ce^{-dh^{3}t^{-2}},

for
c,d
depending only on
k,\ell
.

Proof.

By rescaling we can assume that
t=1
. Since
\mathcal{L}^{*}
has extended Airy sheet marginals, by Proposition
5.8
the probability in question is the same as the probability of the event

A=\{\mathcal{B}[0^{k}\to 0^{k}]+\mathcal{B}[h^{\ell}\to h^{\ell}]>\mathcal{B}[(0^{k},h^{\ell})\to(0^{k},h^{\ell})]\}.

By Proposition
5.9
, the event
A
implies that every optimizer
\pi
from
h^{\ell}
to
h^{\ell}
in
\mathcal{B}
intersects the first
k
lines of
\mathcal{B}
in the interval
(-\infty,0]
. That is,
\pi_{1}(0)\leq k
, and so

\mathcal{B}[h^{\ell}\to h^{\ell}]\leq\mathcal{B}[h^{\ell-1}\to h^{\ell-1}]+\mathcal{B}[h\to 0]+\mathcal{B}_{1}(h)-\mathcal{B}_{k}(0).

(82)

Now by translation invariance of the extended Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{B}[h^{\ell}\to h^{\ell}]-\mathcal{B}[h^{\ell-1}\to h^{\ell-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}_{\ell}(0)
and
\mathcal{B}[h\to 0]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}_{1}(-h).
Finally, since
\mathcal{B}(x)+x^{2}
is stationary, (
82
) is equivalent to an inequality of the form

X_{1}+X_{2}+2h^{2}\leq X_{3}+X_{4},

(83)

where each of the random variables
X_{i}
are equal in distribution to
\mathcal{B}_{i}(0)
for some
i\leq k\wedge\ell
. The points
\mathcal{B}_{i}(0)
are points in the Airy point process, which are known to have well-controlled tails. For example, we can pass Theorem
A.1
to the limit to get that
\mathbb{P}(|X_{i}|>a)\leq ce^{-da^{3/2}}
for all
1\leq i\leq 4
and constants
c,d
that depend only on
k,\ell
. Therefore by a union bound, the probability of (
83
) is bounded above by
ce^{-dh^{3}t^{-2}}
, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

For any
s<t
, almost surely the following statement holds.
For any
\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\leq\mathbf{x}^{(2)}\leq\mathbf{x}^{(3)}\leq\mathbf{x}^{(4)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and
\mathbf{y}^{(1)}\leq\mathbf{y}^{(2)}\leq\mathbf{y}^{(3)}\leq\mathbf{y}^{(4)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, if

\mathcal{L}^{*}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),s;(\mathbf{y}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)}),t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},s;\mathbf{y}^{(2)},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},s;\mathbf{y}^{(3)},t),

we then have

\mathcal{L}^{*}((\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(4)}),s;(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)}),t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},s;\mathbf{y}^{(1)},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{(4)},s;\mathbf{y}^{(4)},t).

Proof.

Without loss of generality we assume that
s=0
,
t=1
. With these choices, the lemma is a fact about an extended Airy sheet of scale
1
.
We claim that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(2)})+\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)})-\mathcal{S}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)}))\\
\geq\;&\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(1)})+\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(4)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})-\mathcal{S}((\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(4)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)}))\\
\geq\;&0.\end{split}

(84)

For this we study the prelimiting sheet
\mathcal{S}^{n}
.
By Lemma
2.5
, for
n
large enough we have

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(2)})+\mathcal{S}^{n}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)}))\geq\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(1)})+\mathcal{S}^{n}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)})),

and

\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)})+\mathcal{S}^{n}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)}))\geq\mathcal{S}^{n}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})+\mathcal{S}^{n}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)})).

Adding up these two inequalities, and passing to the limit via Theorem
5.4
, we get

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(2)})+\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)})-\mathcal{S}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(3)}))\\
\geq\;&\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(1)})+\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})-\mathcal{S}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})).\end{split}

Similarly, we also have

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{y}^{(1)})+\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(3)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})-\mathcal{S}((\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(3)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)}))\\
\geq\;&\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(1)})+\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x}^{(4)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})-\mathcal{S}((\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(4)}),(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\mathbf{y}^{(4)})).\end{split}

Thus adding up the above two inequalities we get the first inequality in (
84
).
The second inequality in (
84
) is obvious for
\mathcal{S}^{n}
, so by passing to the limit via Theorem
5.4
it also holds for
\mathcal{S}
.
Finally, when the first line in (
84
) equals zero, so does the second line. The conclusion follows.
∎

For this next lemma and vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we write

\min(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\min\{x_{i}\wedge y_{i}:i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}.

We similarly define
\max(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For each
M,h>0
and
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a random number
P>0
such that the following is true.
For any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{\prime}}_{\leq}
and
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
with
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2},|s|,|t|<M
,

s<t
,
t-s<P
, and

\min(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\max(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})>h
,
we have

\mathcal{L}^{*}((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),s;(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t).

Proof.

For each
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

J_{\ell}=\{(x,x^{\prime},s,t):|x|,|s|<M,x\in 2^{\ell}\mathbb{Z},x^{\prime}=x+2^{\ell},s\in 2^{2\ell}\mathbb{Z},t=s+2^{2\ell-1}\}.

By Lemma
7.9
,
for any
\ell<0
,
with probability at least

1-cM^{2}2^{-3\ell}e^{-d2^{-\ell}}
(for some constants
c,d
depending on
k,k^{\prime}
),
for any

(x,x^{\prime},s,t)\in J_{\ell}
, we have

\mathcal{L}^{*}(x^{k},s;x^{k},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(x^{\prime k^{\prime}},s;x^{\prime k^{\prime}},t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}((x^{k},x^{\prime k^{\prime}}),s;(x^{k},x^{\prime k^{\prime}}),t).

Then almost surely, there is a random
L_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{-}
, such that this event happens for all
\ell\leq L_{0}
. Now, we can choose
P
small enough such that for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{\prime}}_{\leq}
and
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying the conditions of the lemma, we can find
\ell\leq L_{0}
and
(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime},\tilde{s},\tilde{t})\in J_{\ell}
such that the following holds:

•

\tilde{s}<s<t<\tilde{t}
and
\min(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})>\tilde{x}+h/3
,
\max(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})<\tilde{x}^{\prime}-h/3

•

There exist optimizers
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
from
(\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{s})
to
(\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{t})
and from
(\tilde{x}^{\prime k^{\prime}},\tilde{s})
to
(\tilde{x}^{\prime k^{\prime}},\tilde{t})
, such that
\mathbf{x}\leq\pi(s)\leq\pi^{\prime}(s)\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}\leq\pi(t)\leq\pi^{\prime}(t)\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
.

To ensure the second condition, we have used the transversal fluctuation bound on optimizers from Lemma
6.8
. The fact that
\ell\leq L_{0}
ensures that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{s};\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{t})+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{x}^{\prime k^{\prime}},\tilde{s};\tilde{x}^{\prime k^{\prime}},\tilde{t})=\mathcal{L}^{*}((\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{x}^{\prime k^{\prime}}),s;(\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{x}^{\prime k^{\prime}}),t),

which implies that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s),s;\pi(t),t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime}(s),s;\pi^{\prime}(t),t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}((\pi(s),\pi^{\prime}(s)),s;(\pi(t),\pi^{\prime}(t)),t).

Finally, assuming that for any
s<t\in\mathbb{Q}
the event in Lemma
7.10
holds, we have that
\mathcal{L}^{*}((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),s;(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),t)=\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},s;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
, if
s,t\in\mathbb{Q}
. By continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of the equality in Proposition
7.8
.

First assume that
\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}>-\infty
. Let
\delta>0
.
Take
s<t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}<t
, such that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime\prime}(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};\pi^{\prime\prime}(t_{i}),t_{i})<\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\delta
, and
t_{0}-t,s-t_{m}<\delta
.
We next choose parameters to apply Lemma
7.11
. Let

h=\min_{r\in[t_{0},t_{m}]}\{\min\pi^{\prime}(r)-\max\pi(t)\},\qquad M=\max\{|s|,|t|,\max_{r\in[t,s]}\|\pi^{\prime\prime}(r)\|_{2}\}.

Observe that
h>0
by the assumptions of the proposition. Let
P
be as in Lemma
7.11
for this
M,h
.
Then we choose
\overline{m}\geq m
, and
t_{0}=\overline{t}_{0}<\overline{t}_{1}<\cdots<\overline{t}_{\overline{m}}=t_{m}

such that
\{t_{0},t_{1},\cdots,t_{m}\}\subset\{\overline{t}_{0},\overline{t}_{1},\cdots,\overline{t}_{\overline{m}}\}
,
and
\overline{t}_{i}-\overline{t}_{i-1}<P
for each
1\leq i\leq\overline{m}
.
Then we have

\begin{split}&\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}-\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s),s;\pi(t_{0}),t_{0})-\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(t_{{m}}),t_{{m}};\pi(t),t)\\
&+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}-\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime}(s),s;\pi^{\prime}(t_{0}),t_{0})-\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime}(t_{{m}}),t_{{m}};\pi^{\prime}(t),t)\\
\leq&\;\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(\overline{t}_{i-1}),\overline{t}_{i-1};\pi(\overline{t}_{i}),\overline{t}_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime}(\overline{t}_{i-1}),\overline{t}_{i-1};\pi^{\prime}(\overline{t}_{i}),\overline{t}_{i})\\
=&\;\sum_{i=1}^{\overline{m}}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime\prime}(\overline{t}_{i-1}),\overline{t}_{i-1};\pi^{\prime\prime}(\overline{t}_{i}),\overline{t}_{i})\\
<&\;\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\delta.\end{split}

Now we send
\delta\to 0
.
By Lemma
6.7
we have

\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(s),s;\pi(t_{0}),t_{0})=\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi(t_{{m}}),t_{{m}};\pi(t),t)\leq 0,

\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime}(s),s;\pi^{\prime}(t_{0}),t_{0})=\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{*}(\pi^{\prime}(t_{{m}}),t_{{m}};\pi^{\prime}(t),t)\leq 0.

Therefore
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\|\pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\leq\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
, and our conclusion follows. In the case when
\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=-\infty
, we can apply the same argument with an arbitrary
b\in\mathbb{R}
in place of
\|\pi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}+\delta
to get the result.
∎

8
Disjointness of optimizers

The main goal of this section is to prove the following disjointness result.

Proposition 8.1
.

Almost surely, for any
(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
,
there exists an optimizer
\pi
in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
such that
\pi_{i}(s)<\pi_{j}(s)
for all
i<j
and
s\in(r,t)
.

Proposition
8.1
essentially says that disjoint optimizers in Brownian LPP remain disjoint even as we pass to the limit. In other words, it implies that constituent paths in disjoint optimizers in Brownian LPP are separated from each other by an amount that remains visible in the limiting scaling. This is not an obvious fact, and we will prove it in stages. Note also that Proposition
8.1
still leaves open the possibility that at exceptional points
(\mathbf{x},r;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
where there are multiple
\mathcal{L}^{*}
-optimizers, some of these optimizers may have overlapping constituent paths; we do not believe believe that such optimizers exist but we do not attempt to resolve this issue here.
