By using Proposition
8.1
and checking the definition of the extended directed landscape from Definition
1.1
, we will show that
\mathcal{L}^{*}=\mathcal{L}
. This is the content of Theorem
1.6
.
We will also use Proposition
8.1
to prove Theorem
1.7
and Corollaries
1.9
and
1.11
.

8.1
Convergence in the overlap topology

We start with the following weaker result, which says that all optimizers are disjoint at fixed time.

Lemma 8.2
.

For any fixed
s
the following holds almost surely for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.
For any
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathfrak{X}
,
r<s<t
, and any optimizer
\pi
from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, we have that
\pi_{1}(s)<\pi_{2}(s)<\dots<\pi_{k}(s)
.

Proof.

As any optimizer restricted to a smaller interval of time is also an optimizer, it suffices to prove the result for fixed
r=s-\delta
and
t=s+\delta
with a fixed small
\delta
, and for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with each coordinate in a compact interval.
Since
\mathcal{L}^{*}
has extended Airy sheet marginals, the conclusion follows from Lemma
7.5
for compact sets
K\subset\mathfrak{X}
such that every point
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in K
satisfies
x_{1}>0>y_{k}
.
For more general compact sets, the conclusion follows by skew symmetry of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
(Lemma
6.10
).
∎

For any continuous paths
\pi_{n}:[r_{n},t_{n}]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\pi:[r,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, we say that
\pi_{n}\to\pi

in the overlap topology
, if for all large enough
n
,
O_{n}=\{s\in[r,t]\cap[r_{n},t_{n}]:\pi_{n}(s)=\pi(s)\}
is an interval, and the endpoints of
O_{n}
converge to
r
and
t
. Overlap was first introduced in
[
16
, Section 3]
, and is particularly useful for studying geodesics or optimizers in the directed or extended landscape.

We aim to prove an overlap convergence result for optimizers.
We will require two closely related results for
\mathcal{L}
-geodesics from
[
16
]
.
To state them, for any path
\pi:[r,t]\to\mathbb{R}
, define the
graph
of
\pi
by

\mathfrak{g}\pi:=\{(\pi(s),s):s\in[r,t]\}.

This is the usual graph of a function with coordinates reversed.

Lemma 8.3

(
[
16
, Lemma 3.1]
)
.

Almost surely the following is true.
Let
(p_{n};q_{n})\to(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and let
\pi_{n}
be any sequence of geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
.
Then the sequence
\mathfrak{g}\pi_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff metric, and any subsequential limit is the graph of a geodesic from
p
to
q
.

Lemma 8.4

(
[
16
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

Almost surely the following is true. Let
(p_{n};q_{n})\to(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and let
\pi_{n}
be any sequence of geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
.
Suppose that
(p_{n};q_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{4}
, and
\mathfrak{g}\pi_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\pi
in the Hausdorff metric, for some geodesic
\pi
from
p
to
q
.
Then
\pi_{n}\to\pi
in the overlap topology.

From these we can deduce the following result.

Lemma 8.5
.

Almost surely the following is true.
Let
(p_{n};q_{n})=(x_{n},s;y_{n},t)\to(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and suppose that
x_{n}\geq x,y_{n}\geq y
for all
n
. Let
\pi_{n}
be the sequence of rightmost geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
, and let
\pi
be the rightmost geodesic from
p
to
q
.
Then
\pi_{n}\to\pi
in the overlap topology.

The existence of rightmost and leftmost geodesics follows from
[
15
, Lemma 13.2]
; alternately, it follows from Lemma
7.6
.

Proof of Lemma
8.5
.

First, by Lemma
8.3
the sequence
\mathfrak{g}\pi_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff metric and any subsequential limit is the graph of a geodesic from
p
to
q
. Consider such a subsequential limit
\mathfrak{g}\pi^{\prime}
. Since the
\pi_{n}
are rightmost geodesics, by Lemma
7.7
we have
\pi_{n}\geq\pi
for all
n
, and hence
\pi^{\prime}\geq\pi
. Since
\pi
is a rightmost geodesic, this implies
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
, and therefore
\mathfrak{g}\pi_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\pi
. Lemma
8.4
then completes the proof.
∎

We can now upgrade the above lemma to optimizers in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Almost surely the following statement is true.
Take any
r<t
,
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, and two sequences
\mathbf{x}^{(i)},\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that
x^{(i)}_{j}>x_{j}
,
y^{(i)}_{j}>y_{i}
for
j\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, and that
\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\to\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\to\mathbf{y}
as
i\to\infty
.
Let
\pi^{(i)}
be the rightmost optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}^{(i)},r)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{(i)},t)
, and
\pi
be the rightmost optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.
Then
\pi^{(i)}\to\pi
in the overlap topology.

Proof.

First, the graphs of all the optimizer paths
\pi_{j}^{(i)}
are Hölder-
(2/3)^{-}
with a common Hölder constant by Lemma
6.8
. Therefore along any subsequence we can take a further subsequence so that
\pi^{(i)}
converges to a continuous limit in the dyadic pointwise topology.
This limit must be an optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, by Lemma
7.1
and the continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
. Thus the limit must be
\pi
since
\pi\leq\pi^{(i)}
for each
i
, by Lemma
7.7
.
We conclude that
\pi^{(i)}\to\pi
in the dyadic pointwise topology.

We take any
s\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
with
r<s<t
(recall that
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is the set of dyadic rational numbers).
By Lemma
8.2
we can assume that
\pi_{1}(s),\cdots,\pi_{k}(s)
are pairwise distinct.
Then in a small neighborhood of
s
, the paths
\pi_{1},\cdots,\pi_{k}
are mutually disjoint.
By Lemma
6.8
, we can find a (random)
\delta>0
, such that
\delta\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, and for any
0<\delta_{1}^{-},\cdots,\delta_{k}^{-},\delta_{1}^{+},\cdots,\delta_{k}^{+}<\delta
, and any geodesics from
(\pi_{j}(s-\delta)+\delta_{j}^{-},s-\delta)
to
(\pi_{j}(s+\delta)+\delta_{j}^{+},s+\delta)
,
1\leq j\leq k
, these geodesics are disjoint. By the dyadic convergence established above and Lemma
7.7
, for all large enough
i
we have

\pi_{j}(s\pm\delta)\leq\pi^{(i)}_{j}(s\pm\delta)<\pi_{j}(s\pm\delta)+\delta

(85)

for all
j\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
. From now on, we work with
i
such that (
85
) holds.
For each
1\leq j\leq k
, let
\tau^{(i)}_{j}
be the rightmost geodesic from
(\pi^{(i)}_{j}(s-\delta),s-\delta)
to
(\pi^{(i)}_{j}(s+\delta),s+\delta)
. We claim that
\pi^{(i)}_{j}=\tau^{(i)}_{j}
on the interval
I_{\delta}=[s-\delta,s+\delta]
as long as
i
is sufficiently large. Indeed, letting
\tau^{(i)}=(\tau_{1}^{(i)},\dots,\tau_{k}^{(i)})
, we have

\|\tau^{(i)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|\tau^{(i)}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|\pi_{j}^{(i)}|_{I_{\delta}}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\|\pi^{(i)}|_{I_{\delta}}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}.

Here the equality follows from Proposition
7.8
and (
85
), the first inequality uses that each
\tau^{(i)}_{j}
is a geodesic, and the second inequality uses
Proposition
7.8
again. Since
\pi^{(i)}
is an optimizer, all inequalities above must be equalities, so
\tau^{(i)}
must also be an optimizer, and all the paths
\pi_{j}^{(i)}|_{I_{\delta}}
must be geodesics. Since
\pi^{(i)}
is a rightmost optimizer, we have
\pi^{(i)}|_{I_{\delta}}\geq\tau^{(i)}
. Since each of the
\pi_{j}^{(i)}|_{I_{\delta}}
are geodesics and each of the
\tau_{j}^{(i)}
are rightmost geodesics, this implies that
\tau_{j}^{(i)}=\pi_{j}^{(i)}|_{I_{\delta}}
for all
i
large enough.

The same argument shows that each
\pi_{j}|_{I_{\delta}}
is also a rightmost geodesic. Therefore by Lemma
8.5
, and the fact that for any
\delta>0
, (
85
) holds for all large enough
i
shows that for
i
large enough we have
\pi_{j}^{(i)}(s)=\pi_{j}(s)
for all
j\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
.

Next we take
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
with
r<s_{1}<s_{2}<t
.
For
i
large enough we have
\pi^{(i)}(s_{1})=\pi(s_{1})
and
\pi^{(i)}(s_{2})=\pi(s_{2})
. Thus
\pi^{(i)}(s)=\pi(s)
for any
s_{1}<s<s_{2}
since on
[s_{1},s_{2}]
both
\pi^{(i)}
and
\pi
are the rightmost optimizer from
(\pi(s_{1}),s_{1})
to
(\pi(s_{2}),s_{2})
.
By sending
s_{1}\to r
and
s_{2}\to t
we get the conclusion.
∎

Remark 8.7
.

A similar statement to Lemma
8.6
holds for convergence to leftmost optimizers.

8.2
Two paths

To prove Proposition
8.1
, we start with the two-path case with fixed endpoints.

Lemma 8.8
.

Fix
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
and
s<t
. Then almost surely, the unique optimizer in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
consists of two paths that are disjoint, except possibly at the endpoints.

The proof of Lemma
8.8
consists of two steps. First, we will handle the common endpoint case when
x_{1}=x_{2},y_{1}=y_{2}
. This can be dealt with by proving a quantitative estimate on the location of optimizers from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
at a fixed time, Lemma
8.9
below, and then appealing to Hölder continuity of optimizers. We will then compare the case of general
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1}<x_{2}),\mathbf{y}=(y_{1}<y_{2})
to the common endpoint case by applying a resampling argument to show that optimizers from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
can be approximated in overlap by optimizers from
((0,0),s-1)
to
((0,0),t+1)
in a sequence of extended landscapes defined with different, independent noise on
[s-1,s]
and
[t,t+1]
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}):[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
be the almost surely unique optimizer from
((0,0),0)
to
((0,0),1)
in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
.
Take any
\delta,\eta,d>0
.
There exists
c>0
depending on
\delta,d,\eta
, such that for any
t\in[\delta,1-\delta]
, and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\pi_{1}(t)-\pi_{2}(t)|<\epsilon,|\pi_{1}(t)|,|\pi_{2}(t)|<d)<c\epsilon^{2-\eta}.

The basic strategy for the proof of Lemma
8.9
is to use Proposition
5.8
to relate the joint distribution of
\pi_{1}(t),\pi_{2}(t)
to a certain optimization problem across the first two lines of two parabolic Airy line ensembles. This optimization problem is amenable to analysis since the parabolic Airy line ensemble withstands a strong comparison to independent Brownian motions, and is further reduced to the same optimization problem for several independent Brownian motions (see Lemma
8.11
below).

It is worth mentioning that exponents for disjointness of
geodesics
in the directed landscape have previously been analyzed by Hammond
[
27
]
by similarly appealing to a particular statistic in the parabolic Airy line ensemble. Hammond showed that the probability that
k
geodesics from time
0
to time
1
that all start and end within
\epsilon
of each other are mutually disjoint is bounded above by
\epsilon^{(k^{2}-1)/2+o(1)}
. This exponent is expected to be sharp. One thing that is new in the analysis in Lemma
8.9
is the use of the precise relationship between the Airy sheet and the parabolic Airy line ensemble, which was not yet known when
[
27
]
was written.

The optimization problem (Lemma
8.11
) that arises in Lemma
8.9
is not straightforward to analyze, even heuristically. Indeed, we do not expect that the exponent
2
in Lemma
8.9
is sharp and do not have a prediction for the true exponent. To set up the proof, we need the following result giving a strong comparison between the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{B}
on a compact set and a sequence of independent Brownian motions.

Theorem 8.10
.

For
d>0
, let
\mathcal{C}_{d}
be the space of continuous functions on
[-d,d]
which vanish at
-d
. Let
\mu_{d}
denote the law of a standard Brownian motion on
[-d,d]
, and for
k\in\mathbb{N}
let
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}
denote the law of
k
-tuples of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{d}
given by the product of
k
copies of
\mu_{d}
. For any measurable set
A\subset\mathcal{C}^{k}_{d},k\in\mathbb{N}
and
d\geq 1
we have

\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}\in A)\leq\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)\exp\left(bkd^{6}+de^{bk}\left(\log[\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)]^{-1}\right)^{5/6}\right),

where
b>0
is a universal constant,
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}=(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{k})
, and each
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{i}
is given by

\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{i}(x)=2^{-1/2}\left(\mathcal{B}^{k}_{i}(x)-\mathcal{B}^{k}_{i}(-d)\right).

The main result in
[
9
]
(Theorem 3.11 therein) shows that each of the marginals
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{k}
satisfy the above Radon-Nikodym derivative bound with
\mu_{d}
in place of
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}
. While Theorem
8.10
is stronger than
[
9
, Theorem 3.11]
, it can nonetheless be proven by combining the same key technical ingredients developed in Sections 4 and 5 of
[
9
]
. We do this in Appendix
B
.

Proof of Lemma
8.9
.

We define

\tilde{\mathbf{z}}=\argmax_{-2d\leq z_{1}\leq z_{2}\leq 2d}\mathcal{L}^{*}((0,0),0;\mathbf{z},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{z},t;(0,0),1).

Since
\mathcal{L}^{*}
has extended Airy sheet marginals, by Proposition
5.8
and the symmetry
\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{S}(-\mathbf{y},-\mathbf{x})
(Lemma
5.5
), we could alternatively define
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}
as

\tilde{\mathbf{z}}=\argmax_{-2d\leq z_{1}\leq z_{2}\leq 2d}\mathcal{B}[(0,0)\to(z_{1},z_{2})]+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[(0,0)\to(-z_{2},-z_{1})],

(86)

where
t^{1/3}\mathcal{B}(t^{-2/3}\;\cdot)
and
(1-t)^{1/3}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}((1-t)^{-2/3}\;\cdot)
are independent parabolic Airy line ensembles. Uniqueness of the
\argmax
follows the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
7.3
.
It suffices to prove

\mathbb{P}(|\tilde{z}_{1}-\tilde{z}_{2}|<\epsilon,|\tilde{z}_{1}|,|\tilde{z}_{2}|<d)<c\epsilon^{2-\eta},

since if
|\pi_{1}(t)|,|\pi_{2}(t)|<d
, we must have that
\pi_{1}(t)=\tilde{z}_{1}
and
\pi_{2}(t)=\tilde{z}_{2}
. By Proposition
5.9
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[(0,0)\to(z_{1},z_{2})]

\displaystyle=\max_{z_{1}\leq w\leq z_{2}}\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{1})+\mathcal{B}_{1}(z_{2})-\mathcal{B}_{1}(w)+\mathcal{B}_{2}(w),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[(0,0)\to(-z_{2},-z_{1})]

\displaystyle=\max_{-z_{2}\leq w\leq-z_{1}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(-z_{2})+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(-z_{1})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(w)+\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2}(w).

Therefore by Theorem
8.10
applied to the interval
[-2\delta^{-2/3}d,2\delta^{-2/3}d]
and Brownian scaling and time-reversal symmetry of Brownian motion, it suffices to study the same problem when
\mathcal{B}_{1}(\cdot),\mathcal{B}_{2}(\cdot)
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}(-\;\cdot),\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}(-\;\cdot)
are replaced by independent Brownian motions. This is done in Lemma
8.11
, implying the desired result.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Take four independent two-sided Brownian motions
B_{1},B_{2},B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, with diffusion parameter
2
.
Let
(\tilde{z}_{1},\tilde{z}_{2},\tilde{w},\tilde{w}^{\prime})
be

\argmax_{-2\leq z_{1}\leq w,w^{\prime}\leq z_{2}\leq 2}(B_{1}(z_{1})+B_{1}(z_{2})-B_{1}(w)+B_{2}(w))+(B_{1}^{\prime}(z_{1})+B_{1}^{\prime}(z_{2})-B_{1}^{\prime}(w^{\prime})+B_{2}^{\prime}(w^{\prime})).

Note that a priori we do not assume the
\argmax
is unique, and just take an arbitrary one.
Then given any small
\eta>0
, for any small enough
\epsilon>0
we have
\mathbb{P}(|\tilde{z}_{1}|,|\tilde{z}_{2}|<1,|\tilde{z}_{1}-\tilde{z}_{2}|<\epsilon)<\epsilon^{2-\eta}
.

While now we only work with Brownian motions, analyzing the
\argmax
formula is still involved. Our general strategy is to simplify the problem by restricting the choice of parameters (for example, we will usually take
w=w^{\prime}
). We expect that these simplifications do not capture the full picture and so we do not expect the exponent of
2-\eta
to be sharp.

Proof of Lemma
8.11
.

Throughout the proof we assume that
\eta>0
is small and that
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small given
\eta
.

Step 1: Conditioning on the location of the
\argmax
.
We first split
[-1,1]
into
\lceil\epsilon^{-1}\rceil
intervals, each of length at most
2\epsilon
.
We just need to show that, for each interval
I
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{z}_{1},\tilde{z}_{2}\in I)<\epsilon^{3-\eta}
.

Let
F(z_{1},z_{2},w,w^{\prime})
denote the function inside the
\argmax
.
Denote the center of
I
by
z_{I}
, and
F_{I}=F(z_{I},z_{I},z_{I},z_{I})
.
If
\tilde{z}_{1},\tilde{z}_{2}\in I
, then one of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
happens, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}:

\displaystyle\quad\max_{z_{1}\leq w,w^{\prime}\leq z_{2},z_{1},z_{2}\in I}F(z_{1},z_{2},w,w^{\prime})\geq F_{I}+\epsilon^{1/2-\eta/10},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}:

\displaystyle\quad\max_{z_{I}\leq w\leq z_{2}\leq 2}F(z_{I},z_{2},w,w)<F_{I}+\epsilon^{1/2-\eta/10},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}:

\displaystyle\quad\max_{-2\leq z_{1}\leq w\leq z_{I}}F(z_{1},z_{I},w,w)<F_{I}+\epsilon^{1/2-\eta/10}.

We have taken
w^{\prime}=w
in defining
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
to simplify arguments below.
From the tail of Brownian motions in an interval of length
2\epsilon
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})<e^{-\epsilon^{-\eta/6}}
.
Also note that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
are independent, since they depend only on
B_{1}+B_{1}^{\prime}-B_{1}(z_{I})-B_{1}^{\prime}(z_{I})
and
B_{2}+B_{2}^{\prime}-B_{2}(z_{I})-B_{2}^{\prime}(z_{I})
, to the right and left of
z_{I}
, respectively.
Thus
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})
.
It remains to show that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})<\epsilon^{3/2-\eta/3}
, since similarly we will also have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})<\epsilon^{3/2-\eta/3}
.

Step 2: Reducing to two Brownian motions.
To bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})
, we rewrite
F(z_{I},z_{2},w,w)
using two independent Brownian motions.
Consider two processes on
[0,3]
, defined as
\tilde{B}_{1}(z)=2^{-1}(B_{1}(z_{I}+z)+B_{1}^{\prime}(z_{I}+z)-B_{1}(z_{I})-B_{1}^{\prime}(z_{I}))
and
\tilde{B}_{2}(z)=2^{-1}(B_{2}(z_{I}+z)+B_{2}^{\prime}(z_{I}+z)-B_{2}(z_{I})-B_{2}^{\prime}(z_{I}))
, respectively.
These are two independent standard Brownian motions.
Letting
h=2^{-1}\epsilon^{1/2-\eta/10}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leq w\leq z\leq 1}\tilde{B}_{1}(z)-\tilde{B}_{1}(w)+\tilde{B}_{2}(w)<h\right).

(87)

Now let
\hat{B}(w)=\max\{\tilde{B}_{1}(z)-\tilde{B}_{1}(1-w):1-w\leq z\leq 1\}
. By
[
36
, Theorem 2.34]
and the independence of
\tilde{B}_{1}
and
\tilde{B}_{2}
, we have
(\hat{B},\tilde{B}_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(|B|,\tilde{B}_{2})
on
[0,1]
, where
B
is another Brownian motion, also independent of
\tilde{B}_{2}
. Therefore the right-hand side of (
87
) equals

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{w\in[0,1]}|B(1-w)|+\tilde{B}_{2}(w)<h\right).

(88)

Our goal is to show that (
88
) is
O(h^{3})
.

Step 3: Computation using the reflection principle.

Up to a constant factor, (
88
) further equals

\iint\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{w\in[0,1]}|B(1-w)|+\tilde{B}_{2}(w)<h\;\Big\mid\;B(1)=a,\tilde{B}_{2}(1)=b\right)e^{-(a^{2}+b^{2})/2}dadb.

(89)

Conditioned on
B(1)=a,\tilde{B}_{2}(1)=b
, the processes
B(1-w)-a(1-w)
and
\tilde{B}_{2}(w)-bw
are independent Brownian bridges.
Thus we can write the probability in (
89
) as

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{w\in[0,1]}\max\{G_{1}(w)+G_{2}(w)+a(1-w)+bw,-G_{1}(w)+G_{2}(w)-a(1-w)+bw\}<h\right),

where
G_{1},G_{2}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
are two independent Brownian bridges.
Using that
H_{1}:=2^{-1/2}(G_{1}+G_{2})
and
H_{2}:=2^{-1/2}(G_{1}-G_{2})
are independent Brownian bridges, this probability can be further written as

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{w\in[0,1]}\sqrt{2}H_{1}(w)+a(1-w)+bw<h\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{w\in[0,1]}\sqrt{2}H_{2}(w)-a(1-w)+bw<h\right).

These two probabilities can be computed using the reflection principle (see e.g.
[
36
, Theorem 2.19]
).
The first one equals (for
B
being a standard Brownian motion on
[0,1]
)

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{w\in[0,1]}B(w)<2^{-1/2}(h-a)&\mid B(1)=2^{-1/2}(b-a)\Big)\\
=\;&\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(B(1)=2^{-1/2}(b-a)\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(B(1)=2^{-1/2}(2h-a-b)\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(B(1)=2^{-1/2}(b-a)\right)}\\
=\;&e^{(a-b)^{2}/4}(e^{-(a-b)^{2}/4}-e^{-(2h-a-b)^{2}/4}),\end{split}

and similarly for the second one.
Therefore we can write (
89
) as

\begin{split}&\iint_{|a|,b\leq h}e^{-(a^{2}+b^{2})/2}e^{(a-b)^{2}/4}(e^{-(a-b)^{2}/4}-e^{-(2h-a-b)^{2}/4})e^{(-a-b)^{2}/4}(e^{-(-a-b)^{2}/4}-e^{-(2h+a-b)^{2}/4})dadb\\
=\;&\iint_{|a|,b\leq h}e^{-(a^{2}+b^{2})/2}(1-e^{-(h-a)(h-b)})(1-e^{-(h+a)(h-b)})dadb\\
<\;&\iint_{|a|,b\leq h}e^{-(a^{2}+b^{2})/2}(h-a)(h+a)(h-b)^{2}dadb\\
<\;&2h^{3}\int_{b\leq h}e^{-b^{2}/2}(h-b)^{2}db.\end{split}

We note that the integral in the last line is uniformly bounded for
h<1
.
Thus we conclude that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})<\epsilon^{3/2-\eta/3}
, and our conclusion follows.
∎

Before moving to the proof of Lemma
8.8
, we need one more result.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
s<t
, and let
\mathcal{F}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{L}^{*}
restricted to time increments
[r,r^{\prime}]\subset[s,t]
. Let
\pi
denote the almost surely unique optimizer from
((0,0),s-1)
to
((0,0),t+1)
. Then the conditional law of
(\pi(s),\pi(t))
given
\mathcal{F}
almost surely has full support
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{x}_{*},\mathbf{y}_{*})=\argmax_{(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}}F(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}}),
where

F(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})=\mathcal{L}^{*}((0,0),s-1;\tilde{\mathbf{x}},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},s;\tilde{\mathbf{y}},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}},t;(0,0),t+1).

Then
(\pi(s),\pi(t))=(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*})
and by Lemma
7.2
, the argmax is almost surely unique. Now, the outer two functions are independent of
\mathcal{F}
.
Moreover, by Proposition
5.9
, we have

\mathcal{L}^{*}((0,0),s-1;\tilde{\mathbf{x}},s)=\max_{\tilde{x}_{1}\leq w\leq\tilde{x}_{2}}\mathcal{B}_{1}(\tilde{x}_{1})+\mathcal{B}_{1}(\tilde{x}_{2})-\mathcal{B}_{1}(w)+\mathcal{B}_{2}(w),

(90)

where
\mathcal{B}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble.
A similar decomposition exists for
\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}},t;(0,0),t+1)
in terms of an independent parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Now let
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
. Conditionally on
\mathcal{F}
, we can apply the Brownian Gibbs property to resample the first two lines of
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
on an interval
[-m,m]
containing
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}
. Let
F^{\prime}
denote the analogue of the original function
F
after resampling. By (
90
), for any
M,\delta>0
, with positive probability we have

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-F(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})>M,\qquad|F(\mathbf{u})-F^{\prime}(\mathbf{u})|\leq\delta\text{ for all }\mathbf{u}\text{ such that }\|\mathbf{u}-(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\|_{2}>\delta.

Since
F
achieves its argmax, this implies that
F^{\prime}
can achieve its argmax arbitrarily close to
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Since
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}F^{\prime}
, this gives the result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

Let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
be the optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
. By Lemma
7.2
we assume that it is the unique one.

Step 1.

We first prove the case where
x_{1}=x_{2},y_{1}=y_{2}
.
By the symmetries of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
(Lemma
6.10
) we may assume
x_{1}=x_{2}=y_{1}=y_{2}=0
, and
s=0
,
t=1
.

Fix some small
\delta
with
0<\delta<1
.
Take a large
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
t_{i}=\delta+(1-2\delta)i/N
for
i=0,\ldots,N
.
By Lemma
8.9
,
for any fixed
d
and
\eta
there is some constant
c>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(\exists i,|\pi_{1}(t_{i})-\pi_{2}(t_{i})|<N^{\eta-2/3},|\pi_{1}(t_{i})|,|\pi_{2}(t_{i})|<d)<cN^{3\eta-1/3}.

By Lemma
6.8
, each
\pi_{i}
is Hölder
2/3^{-}
. Therefore taking
N\to\infty
we have

\mathbb{P}(\exists t^{\prime}\in[\delta,1-\delta],\pi_{1}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{2}(t^{\prime}),|\pi_{1}(t^{\prime})|<d)=0.

Since
d
and
\delta
are arbitrary, we have
\pi_{1}(t^{\prime})\neq\pi_{2}(t^{\prime})
,
\forall t^{\prime}\in(0,1)
.

Step 2.
Now we prove the general case by a resampling argument.

Let
\pi^{\prime}
be the optimizer from
((0,0),s-1)
to
((0,0),t+1)
, which is assumed to be unique by Lemma
7.2
.
Setting

(\mathbf{x}_{*},\mathbf{y}_{*})=\argmax_{(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\tilde{\mathbf{y}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}}\mathcal{L}^{*}((0,0),s-1;\tilde{\mathbf{x}},s)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}},s;\tilde{\mathbf{y}},t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}(\tilde{\mathbf{y}},t;(0,0),t+1),

(91)

then
\pi^{\prime}
is the concatenation of the optimizers from
((0,0),t-1)
to
(\mathbf{x}_{*},t)
, from
(\mathbf{x}_{*},t)
to
(\mathbf{y}_{*},s)
, and from
(\mathbf{y}_{*},s)
to
((0,0),s+1)
.
Each of these three optimizers must be unique, otherwise
\pi^{\prime}
is not unique.

Now we take a series of independent samples of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
,
denoted as
\mathcal{L}^{*,i}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Using these samples, we can define landscapes
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{*,i}
by setting
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{*,i}(\cdot,r;\cdot,r^{\prime})
equal to
\mathcal{L}^{*,i}
when
[r,r^{\prime}]\subset(s,t)^{c}
and equal to
\mathcal{L}^{*}
when
[r,r^{\prime}]\subset[s,t]
. Defining
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{*,i}
at all other time increments via metric composition yields an extended landscape.

We denote by
\pi^{(i)}
the optimizer from
((0,0),s-1)
to
((0,0),t+1)
in
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{*,i}
, and define
(\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i)},\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i)})
as in (
91
) with
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{*,i}
in place of
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, so that arguing as before,
\pi^{(i)}
is a concatenation of the unique optimizer from
((0,0),s-1)
to
(\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i)},s)
in
\mathcal{L}^{*,i}
, the unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i)},s)
to
(\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i)},t)
in
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, and the unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i)},t)
to
((0,0),t+1)
in
\mathcal{L}^{*,i}
. In addition, from the first step, we have that each
\pi^{(i)}
consists of disjoint paths, except for the endpoints.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, for any fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
and any
\epsilon>0
, by Lemma
8.12
there is a positive probability that
x_{*,j}^{(i)}>x_{j}
and
y_{*,j}^{(i)}>y_{j}
for all
j\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
, and that
\|\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i)}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}<\epsilon
and
\|\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i)}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<\epsilon
.
Thus almost surely, we can find a sequence
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots
, such that
\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i_{\ell})}\to\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i_{\ell})}\to\mathbf{y}
as
\ell\to\infty
, and
x_{*,j}^{(i_{\ell})}>x_{j}
and
y_{*,j}^{(i_{\ell})}>y_{j}
for all
\ell
.
Then by Lemma
8.6
,
the optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i_{k})},s)
to

(\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i_{k})},t)
converges to the rightmost optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, in the overlap topology.
Since for each
i
, the optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}_{*}^{(i)},s)
to

(\mathbf{y}_{*}^{(i)},t)
consists of disjoint paths, the optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
must also consists of disjoint paths, except possibly at the endpoints.
∎

We upgrade Lemma
8.8
to all endpoints simultaneously.

Lemma 8.13
.

Almost surely the following statement is true.
For any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
and
s<t
,
there exists an optimizer in the extended landscape from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, that consists of two paths that are disjoint, except possibly at the endpoints.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
8.8
, almost surely for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{2}
and
s<t\in\mathbb{Q}
the above statement is true, and there is a unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
.
For any general
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
and
s<t
, we take a sequence
\mathbf{x}^{(i)},\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\leq}
and
s^{(i)}<t^{(i)}
consisting of rational numbers, and satisfying the following conditions:

•

s^{(i)}<s<t<t^{(i)}
, and
s^{(i)}\to s
,
t^{(i)}\to t
as
i\to\infty
;

•

for each
1\leq j\leq k
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

x^{(i)}_{j}-x_{j}>(s-s^{(i)})^{1/5}
,
y^{(i)}_{j}-y_{j}>(t^{(i)}-t)^{1/5}
;

•

\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\to\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}^{(i)}\to\mathbf{y}
as
i\to\infty
.

Let
\pi^{(i)}
be the unique optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}^{(i)},s^{(i)})
to
(\mathbf{y}^{(i)},t^{(i)})
.
By Lemma
6.8
, for
i
large enough we have
\pi^{(i)}(s)>\mathbf{x}
and
\pi^{(i)}(t)>\mathbf{y}
, while
\pi^{(i)}(s)\to\mathbf{x}
and
\pi^{(i)}(t)\to\mathbf{y}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then by Lemma
8.6
, as
i\to\infty

the (unique) optimizer from
(\pi^{(i)}(s),s)
to
(\pi^{(i)}(t),t)
converges to the rightmost optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
in the overlap topology.
This means that the rightmost optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
consists of two paths that are disjoint, except possibly at the endpoints.
∎

8.3
Multiple paths

We now extend from the two-path case to the general
k
-path case. The basic idea is that if two adjacent paths in
k
-path optimizer overlap at a point
(z,r)
, then a similar situation must occur with two paths in a two-path optimizer whose endpoints are chosen close to
(z,r)
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

We show that for each
k\geq 2
, almost surely, for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
r<t
,
there exists an optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
consisting of paths that are disjoint, except possibly at the endpoints.
We prove this by induction on
k
.
The
k=2
case is Lemma
8.13
.
Now suppose that
k>2
and that the statement is true for
k-1
.

We first prove the fixed endpoint version, i.e., for any fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
and
r<t
, almost surely the unique optimizer
\pi
from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
is disjoint, except possibly at the endpoints.

Take
\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}_{\leq}
consisting of the first
k-1
coordinates of
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, respectively.
We assume that the optimizer from
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},r)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
is unique, and denote it as
\pi^{\prime}
.
We then have that almost surely, these optimizers interlace; i.e.
for each
1\leq i\leq k-1
and
r\leq s\leq t
we have

\pi_{i}(s)\leq\pi^{\prime}_{i}(s)\leq\pi_{i+1}(s).

This follows from Lemma
7.7
.
Indeed, by Lemma
6.8
one can find large enough
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that the geodesic from
(x^{\prime\prime},r)
to
(y^{\prime\prime},t)
(denoted as
\pi^{\prime\prime}
) is disjoint from
\pi^{\prime}
.
Then
(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
is a optimizer from
((\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}),r)
to
((\mathbf{y}^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}),t)
,
by Proposition
7.8
;
and by Lemma
7.7
applied to
\pi
and
(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
the first inequality is obtained. The second inequality follows similarly by taking
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}
sufficiently negative.

By the inductive hypothesis,
\pi^{\prime}
consists of paths that are disjoint, except possibly at the endpoints.
This implies that
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{i+2}
are disjoint except possibly at the endpoints, for each
1\leq i\leq k-2
.

Now suppose that
\pi_{i}(s)=\pi_{i+1}(s)
for some
r<s<t
and
1\leq i\leq k-1
.
Then
\pi_{i-1}(s)
(if
i>1
) and
\pi_{i+2}(s)
(if
i+1<k
) are different from
\pi_{i}(s)=\pi_{i+1}(s)
.
Then there exists some
\epsilon>0
, such that

\max_{s^{\prime}\in[s-\epsilon,s+\epsilon]}\pi_{i-1}(s^{\prime})+\epsilon<\min_{s^{\prime}\in[s-\epsilon,s+\epsilon]}\pi_{i}(s^{\prime}),

\min_{s^{\prime}\in[s-\epsilon,s+\epsilon]}\pi_{i+2}(s^{\prime})-\epsilon>\max_{s^{\prime}\in[s-\epsilon,s+\epsilon]}\pi_{i+1}(s^{\prime}).

Now for small enough
\delta>0
, let
\mathbf{x}^{\delta}=(\pi_{i}(s-\delta),\pi_{i+1}(s-\delta))
and let
\mathbf{y}^{\delta}=(\pi_{i}(s+\delta),\pi_{i+1}(s+\delta))
. By Lemma
8.13
, we can find an optimizer
\pi^{\delta}
from
(\mathbf{x}^{\delta},s-\delta)
to
(\mathbf{y}^{\delta},s+\delta)
with
\pi^{\delta}_{1}(s)<\pi^{\delta}_{2}(s)
; in particular,
\pi^{\delta}\neq(\pi_{i},\pi_{i+1})
.
By Lemma
6.8
, for small enough
\delta>0
, the optimizer
\pi^{\delta}
is disjoint from
\pi_{i-1}
(if
i>1
) and
\pi_{i+2}
(if
i+1<k
). Therefore letting
\tau^{\delta}
denote
\pi
with
\pi^{\delta}
in place of
(\pi_{i},\pi_{i+1})
on the interval
[s-\delta,s+\delta]
, Proposition
7.8
ensures that
\|\tau^{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\geq\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
.
Thus this new path is also an optimizer from
(\mathbf{x},r)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, contradicting the uniqueness assumption.

To upgrade this to hold for all endpoints simultaneously, we use the arguments in the proof of Lemma
8.13
, essentially verbatim.
∎

Proof of Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
and Corollaries
1.9
and
1.11
.

First, we can couple
\mathcal{L}^{*},\mathcal{L}
so that
\mathcal{L}^{*}|_{\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}}=\mathcal{L}|_{\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}}
.
By Proposition
7.8
, for any multi-path
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\pi_{i}(r)<\pi_{i+1}(r)
for all
r\in(s,t)
, we have

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}}.

(92)

Moreover, almost surely for all
(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, Proposition
8.1
guarantees that

\mathcal{L}^{*}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\sup_{\pi}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}^{*}},

where the supremum is over all multi-paths
\pi
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
that are disjoint away from the endpoints. Comparing this with Definition
1.1
gives that
\mathcal{L}^{*}=\mathcal{L}
, proving Theorem
1.6
. Theorem
1.7
then follows from (
92
), Proposition
8.1
, and Lemma
7.2
. Corollary
1.9
follows from (
8
) and Theorem
1.6
. For Corollary
1.11
, (
12
) follows from the existence of disjoint geodesics by definition. The opposite direction uses Theorem
1.7
.
∎

9
Convergence of optimizers

In this section we prove Theorem
1.8
, which shows that disjoint optimizers in Brownian LPP converge to disjoint optimizers in
\mathcal{L}
. The convergence for geodesics was shown in
[
15
]
. The argument in
[
15
]
is purely deterministic, relying only the metric composition law for
\mathcal{L}
and a few basic regularity properties. We will adopt a similar strategy here.
In this section, we will work in a coupling where the following conditions hold on some set
\Omega
of probability
1
.

(i)

\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
.

(ii)

For every bounded set
K=[-b,b]^{4}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, there exists some finite
C_{b}
such that for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{(x,s;y;t)\in K}\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,s;y;t)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s+\epsilon}\leq C_{b}.

(iii)

For any
\eta>0
, there is a constant
R>0
such that

\left|\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}\right|\leq R(t-s)^{1/3}G(x,s;y,t)^{\eta}.

Here the function
G
is as in Lemma
6.7
.

The fact that such a coupling exists follows from Theorem
1.5
for the first statement,
[
15
, Lemma 13.3]
for the second statement, and Lemma
6.7
for the third statement (or alternately,
[
15
, Corollary 10.7]
). We let
B^{n}=(B^{n}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z})
denote the collection of standard Brownian motions that give rise to
\mathcal{L}_{n}
in this coupling. We work on
\Omega
for all statements and proofs in this section.

Most of this section is focused on proving Hausdorff convergence of rescaled zigzag graphs; we translate to the language of Theorem
1.8
at the end.
For a path
\pi:[a,b]\to\mathbb{Z}
, recall from Section
2
that its zigzag graph is

\Gamma(\pi)=\{(c,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}:c\in[a,b],\pi(r)\leq y\leq\pi(r^{-})\}.

Note that we write
\pi(r^{-})
for the left-hand limit at
r
, and that
\pi(r^{-})
is always defined, see Section
2
.
Also let
A_{n}
be the linear transformation of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by the matrix

A_{n}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}n^{1/3}/2&n^{-2/3}/2\\
0&-n^{-1}\end{array}\right].

(93)

For any path
\pi
, its transformed zigzag graph
A_{n}\Gamma(\pi)
is contained in
\mathbb{R}\times n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, the restriction
A_{n}|_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}\times n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
is the inverse of the map
(x,s)\mapsto(x,s)_{n}
used in the construction of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
in Theorem
1.5
. Therefore for any path
\pi
from
(a,m)
to
(b,\ell)
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, after tracing through the definitions we get that

\begin{split}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}&=\inf\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{n}(p_{i-1};p_{i}),\qquad\qquad\text{ where }\\
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}:&=\|\pi\|_{B^{n}}+2\sqrt{n}(b-a)+n^{1/6}(A_{n}(b,\ell)_{1}-A_{n}(a,m)_{1}).\end{split}

(94)

Here the infimum is over all finite sequences
p_{0},\dots,p_{k}\subset A_{n}\Gamma(\pi)
such that

a=(A_{n}^{-1}p_{0})_{1}<(A_{n}^{-1}p_{1})_{1}<\dots<(A_{n}^{-1}p_{k})_{1}=b.

Here and in (
94
),
(A^{-1}_{n}p)_{1}
denotes the first coordinate of
A_{n}^{-1}p
. We begin with a tightness statement for zigzag graphs.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\pi_{n}
be a sequence of paths from
(a_{n},m_{n})
to
(b_{n},\ell_{n})
such that

A_{n}(a_{n},m_{n})\to(x,r)\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad A_{n}(b_{n},\ell_{n})\to(y,t)

(95)

as
n\to\infty
. Suppose also that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\pi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}>-\infty

(96)

almost surely. Then on
\Omega
, the sequence
A_{n}\Gamma(\pi_{n})
is precompact in the Hausdorff metric. Moreover, any subsequential limit of
A_{n}\Gamma(\pi_{n})
is equal to
\mathfrak{g}\pi=\{(\pi(s),s):s\in[r,t]\}
for some continuous function
\pi:[r,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(r)=x
and
\pi(t)=y
.

Proof.

First, let

\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})=\{x\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}:d(x,A_{n}\Gamma(\pi))\leq n^{-2/3}\}.

Here
d(x,A)
denotes the Euclidean distance between a point and a set. The definitions of
A_{n}
and
\Gamma_{n}(\pi)
ensure that the sets
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})
are all connected. Moreover, the Hausdorff distance
d_{H}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n}),\Gamma_{n}(\pi_{n}))
is at most
n^{-2/3}
, so it suffices to prove all statements in the lemma for
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})
. Next, fix an interval
[-b,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
. The definition of the scaling matrix
A_{n}
and the limiting statements (
95
) guarantee that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})\cap([-b,b]\times\mathbb{R})
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology, with subsequential limits contained in
[-b,b]\times[r,t]
.

Take
b
large enough so that
x,y\in(-b,b)
. Connectedness of the sets
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})
implies either there is a subsequential limit of
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})\cap([-b,b]\times\mathbb{R})
that intersects the boundary
\{-b,b\}\times\mathbb{R}
, or else the sequence
\Gamma_{n}^{\prime}(\pi_{n})
is precompact, and all subsequential limits are contained in
(-b,b)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Suppose that some subsequential limit of
\Gamma^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{n})\cap[-b,b]\times\mathbb{R}
intersects the boundary
\{-b,b\}\times\mathbb{R}
at a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then there exists a sequence of points
p_{n}\in A_{n}\Gamma(\pi_{n})
that converge to
p
. By the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{L}_{n}
and (
94
) we have

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}\leq\mathcal{L}_{n}(A_{n}(a_{n},m_{n}),p_{n})+\mathcal{L}_{n}(p_{n},A_{n}(b_{n},\ell_{n})).

If
p_{n}\to(z,s)
for some
(z,s)\in\{-b,b\}\times\{r,t\}
, then the right-hand side above converges to
-\infty
by condition (ii) above, contradicting (
96
).
If
p_{n}\to(z,s)
for some
s\in(r,t)
and
z=\pm b
, then uniform-on-compact convergence of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}
guarantees that the right-hand side above converges to

\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t).

For
b
large enough, condition (iii) above guarantees that this quantity can become arbitrarily large and negative, contradicting (
96
). Therefore the sequence
\Gamma_{n}^{\prime}(\pi_{n})
is precompact, and all subsequential limits are contained in
(-B,B)\times[r,t]
for some random
B>0
. Since all subsequential limits of
\Gamma_{n}^{\prime}(\pi_{n})
are connected and contain the points
(x,r)
and
(y,t)
, to show that any subsequential limit
\Gamma
is of the form
\{(\pi(s),s):s\in[r,t]\}
for some continuous function
\pi:[r,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(r)=x
and
\pi(t)=y
, we just need to show that
\Gamma
intersects each horizontal line at most once.

Suppose that this is not the case, and that
p=(z,s),p^{\prime}=(z^{\prime},s)\in\Gamma
for some
z\neq z^{\prime}
. Then there are sequences
p_{n}\in A_{n}\Gamma(\pi_{n})
and
p_{n}^{\prime}\in A_{n}\Gamma(\pi_{n})
converging to
p,p^{\prime}
, respectively.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
(A_{n}^{-1}p_{n})_{1}<(A_{n}^{-1}p_{n}^{\prime})_{1}
infinitely often, so that by (
94
) we have

\|\pi_{n}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}\leq\mathcal{L}_{n}(A_{n}(a_{n},m_{n});p_{n})+\mathcal{L}_{n}(p_{n};p_{n}^{\prime})+\mathcal{L}_{n}(p_{n}^{\prime};A_{n}(b_{n},\ell_{n}))

for infinitely many
n
. Condition (ii) guarantees that almost surely, the middle term on the right-hand side above converges to
-\infty
, whereas the first and third terms are bounded above. Again, this contradicts (
96
).
∎

Theorem 9.2
.

Fix
\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)\in\mathfrak{X}_{\uparrow}
, and let
C_{\mathbf{u}}
be the almost sure set where there is a unique disjoint optimizer
\pi
in
\mathcal{L}
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
. Let
\pi^{(n)}
be any sequence of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
-optimizers from
(\mathbf{a}_{n},m_{n})
to
(\mathbf{b}_{n},\ell_{n})
where

A_{n}(a_{n,i},m_{n})\to(x_{i},s)
and
A_{n}(b_{n,i},\ell_{n})\to(y_{i},t)
.

Then on
\Omega\cap C_{\mathbf{u}}
,
A_{n}\Gamma(\pi^{(n)}_{i})\to\mathfrak{g}\pi_{i}=\{(\pi_{i}(r),r):r\in[s,t]\}
in the Hausdorff metric for all
i
. Moreover, letting
h_{n,i}:[s,t]\to[a_{n,i},b_{n,i}]
be the linear function satisfying
h_{n,i}(s)=a_{n,i},h_{n,i}(t)=b_{n,i}
, on
\Omega\cap C_{\mathbf{u}}
we have the uniform convergence

\tilde{\pi}^{(n)}_{i}:=\frac{\pi^{(n)}_{i}\circ h_{n,i}+nh_{n,i}}{2n^{2/3}}\to\pi_{i},

as functions from
[s,t]
to
\mathbb{R}
.

The ‘Moreover’ in Theorem
9.2
is Theorem
1.8
.

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

In the proof, we work on the set
C_{\mathbf{u}}\cap\Omega
.
Let
k
be such that
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
. Since the
\pi^{(n)}
are optimizers, and
\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
uniformly on compact sets, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}^{(n)}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}=\mathcal{L}_{n}(A_{n}(\mathbf{a}_{n},m_{n}),A_{n}(\mathbf{b}_{n},\ell_{n}))\to\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t).

(97)

Also, for each
i
, we have

\|\pi^{(n)}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}\leq\mathcal{L}_{n}(A_{n}(a_{n,i},m_{n}),A_{n}(a_{n,i},\ell_{n})).

(98)

The right-hand side above converges to
\mathcal{L}(x_{i},s;y_{i},t)
, so for all
i
, by (
97
) and (
98
), we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|\pi^{(n)}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{n}}\geq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)-\sum_{1\leq j\leq k,j\neq i}\mathcal{L}(x_{j},s;y_{j},t)>-\infty.

Hence by Lemma
9.1
, each of the sequences
\{A_{n}\Gamma(\pi^{(n)}_{i}):n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is precompact, with subsequential limits that are of the form
(\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{k})
for some continuous multi-path
\gamma
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
. Now, let
P_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}\times\mathbb{Z}
be the set of all points
(\mathbf{z},j)
such that

B^{n}[(\mathbf{a}_{n},m_{n})\to(\mathbf{b}_{n},\ell_{n})]=B^{n}[(\mathbf{b}_{n},m_{n})\to(\mathbf{z},j)]+B^{n}[(\mathbf{z},j-1)\to(\mathbf{b}_{n},\ell_{n})],

(99)

and such that
(z_{i},j),(z_{i},j-1)\in\Gamma(\pi^{(n)}_{i})
for all
i
.
Metric composition (Lemma
2.9
) and the fact that
\pi^{(n)}
is an optimizer guarantees that for every
j\in\{\ell_{n}+1,\dots,m_{n}\}
, there exists
(\mathbf{z},j)\in P_{n}
. In particular, this implies that along a subsequence where
A_{n}\Gamma(\pi^{(n)}_{i})\to\mathfrak{g}\gamma_{i}
for all
i
, we have

A_{n}P_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\gamma=\{(\gamma(r),r):r\in[s,t]\}

and so (
99
) passes to the limit to give that

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\mathbf{y},t)=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x},s;\gamma(r),r)+\mathcal{L}(\gamma(r),r;\mathbf{y},t)

for all
r\in(s,t)
.
This can only occur if
\gamma
is the unique optimizer in
\mathcal{L}
from
(\mathbf{x},s)
to
(\mathbf{y},t)
, yielding the first part of the theorem.

For the ‘Moreover’, it is enough to show that
\mathfrak{g}\tilde{\pi}^{(n)}_{i}\to\mathfrak{g}\pi_{i}
for all
i
in the Hausdorff metric, since Hausdorff convergence of graphs implies uniform convergence of functions when the limit is continuous. For this, by the first part of the theorem we just need to show that the Hausdorff distance
d_{H}(\mathfrak{g}\tilde{\pi}_{i}^{(n)},A_{n}\Gamma(\pi_{i}^{(n)}))
converges to
0
with
n
.

Since
A_{n}(a_{n,i},m_{n})\to(x_{i},s)
and
A_{n}(b_{n,i},\ell_{n})\to(y_{i},t)
we have
a_{n,i}\to s
and
b_{n,i}\to t
.
Then the function
h_{n,i}
converges to the identity, so
d_{H}(\mathfrak{g}\tilde{\pi}_{i}^{(n)},\mathfrak{g}\hat{\pi}^{(n)}_{i})\to 0
, where

\hat{\pi}^{(n)}_{i}(x)=\frac{\pi^{n}_{i}(x)+nx}{2n^{2/3}}.

Moreover, letting
\Lambda\pi^{(n)}_{i}=\{(c,\pi^{(n)}_{i}(c)):c\in[a,b]\}
denote the graph of
\pi^{(n)}_{i}
, the first part of the theorem guarantees that
d_{H}(A_{n}\Lambda\pi_{i}^{(n)},A_{n}\Gamma\pi_{i}^{(n)})\to 0
. Therefore it suffices to show that
d_{H}(A_{n}\Lambda\pi_{i}^{(n)},\mathfrak{g}\hat{\pi}_{i}^{(n)})\to 0
with
n
. This boils down to a matrix computation. We have
\mathfrak{g}\hat{\pi}_{i}^{(n)}=D_{n}\mathfrak{g}\pi_{i}^{(n)}
and
\mathfrak{g}\pi_{i}^{(n)}=R\Lambda\pi^{(n)}_{i}
, where

D_{n}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}n^{-2/3}/2&n^{1/3}/2\\
0&1\end{array}\right],\qquad R=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right].

(100)

Therefore
D_{n}RA^{-1}_{n}(A_{n}\Lambda\pi^{(n)}_{i})=\mathfrak{g}\hat{\pi}_{i}^{(n)}
. A quick computation shows that
D_{n}RA_{n}^{-1}\to I
, yielding the result.
∎

Appendix A
Brownian melon estimates

In this appendix we prove Lemma
5.11
and
6.1
, using some Brownian melon estimates from the literature.
We start by quoting these results.

Theorem A.1

(
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

There exist positive constants
c_{k},d_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds. For all
m\in(0,5n^{2/3})
and
n\geq 1
we have

\mathbb{P}(W^{n}_{k}(1)-2\sqrt{n}\geq mn^{-1/6})\leq c_{1}e^{-d_{1}m^{3/2}},

\mathbb{P}(W^{n}_{k}(1)-2\sqrt{n}\leq-mn^{-1/6})\leq c_{k}e^{-d_{k}m^{3}}.

Also, for all
m\geq 5n^{2/3}
and
n\geq 1
we have

\mathbb{P}(|W^{n}_{k}(1)-2\sqrt{n}|\geq mn^{-1/6})\leq c_{1}e^{-d_{1}n^{-1/3}m^{2}}.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x,a,b,w>0
, denote

\mathcal{N}_{b,w}(n,x,a)=2\sqrt{nx}+\sqrt{x}n^{-1/6}(a+b\log^{2/3}(n^{1/3}|\log(x/w)|+1)).

Note that for any
\alpha>0
, we have

\mathcal{N}_{b,\alpha w}(n,\alpha x,a)=\sqrt{\alpha}\mathcal{N}_{b,w}(n,x,a).

(101)

Proposition A.2

(
[
17
, Proposition 4.3]
)
.

There exist positive constants
b,c
and
d
such that for all
w,a>0
and
n\geq 1
, the probability that

W^{n}_{1}(x)\leq\mathcal{N}_{b,w}(n,x,a),\;\;\forall x\in(0,\infty)

is greater than or equal to
1-ce^{-da^{3/2}}
.

The following estimate is also necessary. This estimate is simply a deterministic inequality and does not involve any probabilistic objects.

Lemma A.3

(
[
15
, Lemma 9.4]
)
.

Let
b>0
be a fixed constant. Then there exists a constant
c
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N},t\in\{1/n,2/n,\dots,(n-1)/n\},a>1
and

z\in[0,t-c(t\wedge(1-t))^{1/3}a^{2}n^{-1/3}]\cup[t+c(t\wedge(1-t))^{1/3}a^{2}n^{-1/3},1],

we have that

\mathcal{N}_{b,t}(nt,z,a)+\mathcal{N}_{b,1-t}(n(1-t),1-z,a)\leq 2\sqrt{n}-an^{-1/6}.

We first use Theorem
A.1
to deduce an estimate on last passage values across Brownian motions. To clean up the notation in this lemma, its proof, and in the subsequent proof of Lemma
5.11
, for a vector
\mathbf{x}
, we let

\hat{\mathbf{x}}=(2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},n)\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=(1+2n^{-1/3}\mathbf{x},1).

The dependence on
n
in the notation is implicit.

Lemma A.4
.

Take independent standard Brownian motions
B^{n}=(B^{n}_{1},\ldots,B^{n}_{n})
, and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/6}
.
For any
a>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|B^{n}[\hat{\mathbf{x}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{y}}]-\sum_{i=1}^{k}2\sqrt{n(1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i}))}\right|>an^{-1/6}\right)<ce^{-da^{3/2}},

where
c,d
are constants depending only on
k
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.8
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}(B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}^{k}\to\tilde{y}_{i}^{k}]-B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}^{k-1}\to\tilde{y}_{i}^{k-1}])\leq B^{n}[\hat{\mathbf{x}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{y}}]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}\to\tilde{y}_{i}].

If
W^{n}
is the
n
dimensional Brownian melon, then by Theorem
2.10
,

\displaystyle B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}\to\tilde{y}_{i}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}W_{1}^{n}(1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i}))\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad

\displaystyle B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}^{k}\to\tilde{y}_{i}^{k}]-B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}^{k-1}\to\tilde{y}_{i}^{k-1}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}W_{k}^{n}(1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i})).

Then the conclusion follows from Theorem
A.1
, using that
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/6}
, and scale invariance of the Brownian melon:
\sqrt{\alpha}W^{n}(\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}W^{n}(\alpha\;\cdot)
for any
\alpha>0
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.11
.

Throughout this proof we let
c,d
denote constants depending on
k
, whose values may change from line to line. We also assume that
n
is large enough, since otherwise the conclusion follows by taking
c
large and
d
small.

By Lemma
A.4
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}A(\mathbf{z})<\sum_{i=1}^{k}2\sqrt{n(1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i}))}-an^{-1/6}\right)<ce^{-da^{3/2}}.

(102)

For each
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
for which
A(\mathbf{z})
is not equal to
-\infty
(i.e. when it is defined by (
65
)), by Lemma
2.8
we also have

A(\mathbf{z})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(B^{n}[\hat{x}_{i}\to(t+2n^{-1/3}z_{i},q+1)]+B^{n}[(t+2n^{-1/3}z_{i},q)\to\tilde{y}_{i}]\big).

(103)

By Proposition
A.2
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-da^{3/2}}
, the
i
th summand on the right-hand side of (
103
) is bounded above by

\mathcal{N}_{b,t(1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i}))}(p,t+2n^{-1/3}(z_{i}-x_{i}),a)+\mathcal{N}_{b,(1-t)(1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i}))}(q,1-t+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-z_{i}),a),

where
b
is a universal constant.
By (
101
) this equals

\sqrt{1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i})}\\
\times\left(\mathcal{N}_{b,t}\left(nt,\frac{t+2n^{-1/3}(z_{i}-x_{i})}{1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i})},a\right)+\mathcal{N}_{b,(1-t)}\left(n(1-t),\frac{1-t+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-z_{i})}{1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i})},a\right)\right).

Recall that we require
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/6}
.
By Lemma
A.3
, for any
a>1
the above can be bounded by
\sqrt{1+2n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i})}(2\sqrt{n}-an^{-1/6})
,
when
|z_{i}-ty_{i}-(1-t)x_{i}|>ca^{2}(t\wedge(1-t))^{1/3}
.
Thus we conclude that, for any
a>0
, with probability at least
1-ce^{-da^{3/2}}
we have

A(\mathbf{z})<\sum_{i=1}^{k}2\sqrt{n(1+n^{-1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i}))}-an^{-1/6}

for any
\mathbf{z}
with
\|\mathbf{z}-t\mathbf{y}-(1-t)\mathbf{x}\|_{2}>ca^{2}(t\wedge(1-t))^{1/3}
.
This with (
102
) finishes the proof.
∎

Now we complete proving Lemma
6.1
, following the outline in Section
6.1
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

In this proof we let
c,d
denote large and small constants depending on
k
, whose values may change from line to line.

We first upper bound
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)
.
By the triangle inequality we have

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)\leq-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t+n^{-1};\mathbf{y},1).

Thus we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)>a(1-t)^{1/3})\\
\leq&\;\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t+n^{-1};\mathbf{y},1)<-a(1-t)^{1/3})\leq ce^{-da^{3/2}}.\end{split}

Here the last inequality follows by applying Lemma
A.4
to
(1-t)n-1
Brownian motions, and elementary calculations.
We next lower bound
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)
. For any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
we denote
A(\mathbf{z})=(\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t))+\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{z},t;\mathbf{y},1)
.
It remains to bound the probability of this event

\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}A(\mathbf{z})>-\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}(1-t)+a(1-t)^{1/3}|\log(1-t)|.

To bound
A(\mathbf{z})
, we collect some estimates on
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{z},t;\mathbf{y},1)
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
.
For this, take any
1<\hat{a}<n^{1/100}
.

Estimate 1.

By Lemma
2.8
we have

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{z},t;\mathbf{y},1)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{n}(z_{i},t;y_{i},1)
.
By Proposition
A.2
and using the notation there, for some constant
b>0
, with probability
>1-ce^{-d\hat{a}^{3/2}}
we have

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{z},t;\mathbf{y},1)<\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big[n^{1/6}\mathcal{N}_{b,1-t+2(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})n^{-1/3}}((1-t)n,1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3},\hat{a})\\
-2(1-t)n^{2/3}-2n^{1/3}(y_{i}-z_{i})\Big],

(104)

for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that
1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3}>0
for each
i
.
We now give a more explicit bound for the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
summand in the right-hand side of (
104
), when
|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|<cn^{1/20}
.
Note that
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/100}
,
1-t>n^{-1/100}
, so in this case we would have
(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3},(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})n^{-1/3}<d(1-t)
.
Further, recall that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{N}_{b,1-t+2(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})n^{-1/3}}((1-t)n,1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3},\hat{a})\\
=&\;2\sqrt{(1-t)n(1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3})}+\sqrt{1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3}}\\
&\times((1-t)n)^{-1/6}\left(\hat{a}+b\log^{2/3}\left(((1-t)n)^{1/3}\left|\log\left(\frac{1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3}}{1-t+2(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})n^{-1/3}}\right)\right|+1\right)\right).\end{split}

By Taylor expansion of
y_{i}-z_{i}
, we can bound the first term in the right-hand side by

2(1-t)\sqrt{n}+2n^{1/6}(y_{i}-z_{i})-n^{-1/6}\frac{(y_{i}-z_{i})^{2}}{1-t}+cn^{-1/2}\frac{(y_{i}-z_{i})^{3}}{(1-t)^{2}}.

For the second term, we use that
\sqrt{1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3}}<c\sqrt{1-t}
, and that
\left|\log\left(\frac{1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3}}{1-t+2(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})n^{-1/3}}\right)\right|<cn^{-1/3}\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{1-t}
, to bound it by

cn^{-1/6}\hat{a}(1-t)^{1/3}+cn^{-1/6}\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}}.

Thus when
|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|<cn^{1/20}
we can bound the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
summand in the right-hand side of (
104
) by

-\frac{(y_{i}-z_{i})^{2}}{1-t}+c\hat{a}(1-t)^{1/3}+c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}}.

(105)

Estimate 2.

For
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
we give two different bounds.
The first of these bounds
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
separately.

By Lemma
2.8
we have

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{n}(x_{i},0;z_{i},t)
.
By Proposition
A.2
(for
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)
) and Lemma
A.4
(for
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
), with probability
>1-ce^{-d\hat{a}^{3/2}}
we have

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)<\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big[n^{1/6}\mathcal{N}_{b,t+2(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}(tn,t+2(z_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3},\hat{a})\\
-2n^{2/3}\sqrt{t(t+2(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})n^{-1/3})}+\hat{a}\sqrt{t+2(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}t^{-1/6}-2n^{1/3}(z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})\Big],

(106)

for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
such that
t+2(z_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}>0
for each
i
.
Similar to Estimate 1, when
|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|<cn^{1/20}
, we can bound the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
summand in the right-hand side of (
106
) by

-\frac{(z_{i}-x_{i})^{2}}{t}+c\hat{a}{t}^{1/3}+c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{{t}^{1/3}}+\frac{({y_{i}}^{\prime}-x_{i})^{2}}{t}.

(107)

Estimate 3.

The second bound for
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)
is from the continuity of the prelimiting extended Airy sheet (Lemma
3.5
). It is more refined when
\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}
is small.

By Lemma
3.5
and using Lemma
3.3
, we also have that with probability
>1-ce^{-d\hat{a}^{3/2}}
,

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{z},t)-\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)<\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big[\hat{a}\log^{2/3}(2|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|^{-1})\sqrt{|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|}\Big]\\
-\frac{\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}}{t},

(108)

for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{z}\|_{2}<1
.

Below we shall bound
A(\mathbf{z})
assuming that the above three estimates (
104
), (
106
), (
108
) hold.

Upper bound
A(\mathbf{z})
for
\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{z}\|_{2}<1
.

In this case, by (
105
) and (
108
)
we have

\begin{split}A(\mathbf{z})<&\;\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big[-\frac{(y_{i}-z_{i})^{2}}{1-t}+c\hat{a}(1-t)^{1/3}+c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}}\\
&+\hat{a}\log^{2/3}(2|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|^{-1})\sqrt{|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|}-\frac{(z_{i}-x_{i})^{2}-(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})^{2}}{t}\Big]\\
=&\;-(1-t)\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big[-\frac{(y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i})^{2}}{t(1-t)}+\hat{a}\log^{2/3}(2|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|^{-1})\sqrt{|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|}\\
&+c\hat{a}(1-t)^{1/3}+c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}}\Big]\\
<&\;-(1-t)\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}+c\hat{a}^{4/3}(1-t)^{1/3}|\log(1-t)|,\end{split}

where the last inequality uses that

\frac{(y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i})^{2}}{2t(1-t)}+c^{2}(1-t)^{1/3}>c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}},

\frac{(y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i})^{2}}{2t(1-t)}+c\hat{a}^{4/3}(1-t)^{1/3}|\log(1-t)|>\hat{a}\log^{2/3}(2|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|^{-1})\sqrt{|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|}.

Upper bound
A(\mathbf{z})
for
\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{z}\|_{2}\geq 1
.

In this case we use (
104
) and (
106
).
Letting
A_{i}
be the sum of the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
term in the right-hand side of (
104
) and (
106
), we have
A(\mathbf{z})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}A_{i}
.
By (
101
) and Lemma
A.3
, for each
1\leq i\leq k
such that
|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|>c\left(\frac{\hat{a}}{1-t}\right)^{2}(1-t)^{1/3}
we have

\begin{split}&\mathcal{N}_{b,1-t+2(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})n^{-1/3}}((1-t)n,1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3},\hat{a})\\
&+\mathcal{N}_{b,t+2(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}(tn,t+2(z_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3},\hat{a})\\
\leq&\;\sqrt{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}\Bigg(\mathcal{N}_{b,1-t}\left((1-t)n,\frac{1-t+2(y_{i}-z_{i})n^{-1/3}}{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}},\frac{\hat{a}}{1-t}\right)\\
&+\mathcal{N}_{b,t}\left(tn,\frac{t+2(z_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}},\frac{\hat{a}}{1-t}\right)\Bigg)\\
\leq&\;\sqrt{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}\left(2\sqrt{n}-\left(\frac{\hat{a}}{1-t}\right)n^{-1/6}\right).\end{split}

So using that
\|\mathbf{x}\|,\|\mathbf{y}\|_{2}<n^{1/100}
,
1-t>n^{-1/100}
, we have

\begin{split}A_{i}\leq&\;2n^{2/3}\sqrt{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}-\frac{\hat{a}}{1-t}\sqrt{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}-2n^{1/3}(y_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime})\\
&-2(1-t)n^{2/3}-2n^{2/3}\sqrt{t(t+2(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})n^{-1/3})}+\hat{a}\sqrt{t+2(y_{i}^{\prime}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}t^{-1/6}\\
\leq&\;2(1-t)n^{2/3}\sqrt{1+2(y_{i}-x_{i})n^{-1/3}}-2(1-t)n^{1/3}(y_{i}-x_{i})-2(1-t)n^{2/3}-\frac{d\hat{a}}{1-t}\\
<&\;-(1-t)(y_{i}-x_{i})^{2}+c(1-t)^{1/3}-\frac{d\hat{a}}{1-t}\end{split}

When
|z_{i}-y_{i}^{\prime}|\leq c\left(\frac{\hat{a}}{1-t}\right)^{2}(1-t)^{1/3}<cn^{1/20}
, using (
105
) and (
107
) we have

\begin{split}A_{i}\leq&\;-\frac{(y_{i}-z_{i})^{2}}{1-t}+c\hat{a}(1-t)^{1/3}+c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}}-\frac{(z_{i}-x_{i})^{2}}{t}\\
&+c\hat{a}{t}^{1/3}+c\frac{|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{{t}^{1/3}}+\frac{({y_{i}}^{\prime}-x_{i})^{2}}{t}\\
<&\;-\frac{(y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i})^{2}}{t(1-t)}-(1-t)(y_{i}-x_{i})^{2}+c\hat{a}+\frac{c|y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i}|}{(1-t)^{1/3}}\\
<&\;-\frac{(y_{i}^{\prime}-z_{i})^{2}}{2(1-t)}-(1-t)(y_{i}-x_{i})^{2}+c\hat{a}+c(1-t)^{1/3}.\end{split}

Thus by the above two inequalities, and using that
\|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{z}\|_{2}\geq 1
, we get

A(\mathbf{z})<c\hat{a}+c(1-t)^{1/3}-\frac{d}{1-t}-(1-t)\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}<c\hat{a}^{4/3}(1-t)^{1/3}-(1-t)\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}.

Finally, from these bounds on
A(\mathbf{z})
in each case, we conclude that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y},1)-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{x},0;\mathbf{y}^{\prime},t)>a(1-t)^{1/3}|\log(1-t)|)\\
<&\;\mathbb{P}(\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}}A(\mathbf{z})>-\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\|_{2}^{2}(1-t)+a(1-t)^{1/3}|\log(1-t)|)<ce^{-da^{9/8}}.\end{split}

The conclusion follows.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
8.10

In this appendix, we extend the main result of Calvert, Hegde, and Hammond
[
9
]
to prove Theorem
8.10
.
For brevity, we don’t give full context for the paper
[
9
]
here and refer the interested reader to that paper. The paper
[
28
]
may also be a useful reference, as the work
[
9
]
builds on results from that paper. We strive to use the same notation as
[
9
]
so the interested reader can refer back easily. The main exception to this is that we use the notation
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=2^{-1/2}\mathcal{B}_{i}
for lines in the (rescaled) parabolic Airy line ensemble. In
[
9
]
, the authors use the notation
\mathcal{L}(i,\cdot)
for these lines, which conflicts with our notation for the directed landscape. The factor of
2^{-1/2}
is introduced in
[
9
]
so that comparison statements can be made with Brownian motions with diffusion parameter
1
, rather than
2
.

Throughout this section, we let
b>0
be a large constant and
b^{\prime}>0
be a small constant, whose values may change from line to line but do not depend on any parameters. Other constants will retain the definitions used in
[
9
]
.

First, fix an interval
[-d,d]
with
d\geq 1
and a collection of line indices
\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
. For universal positive constants
c,C
, as in
[
9
]
we define

\displaystyle c_{k}

\displaystyle=((3-2^{3/2})^{3/2}2^{-1}5^{-3/2})^{k-1}(2^{-5/2}c\wedge 1/8),

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{10\cdot 20^{k-1}5^{k/2}\left(\frac{10}{3-2^{3/2}}\right)^{k(k-1)/2}C,e^{c/2}\right\},

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{k^{1/3}c_{k}^{-1/3}(2^{-9/2}-2^{-5})^{-1/3},36(k^{2}-1),2\right\}.

The precise values of these constants are not important for our purposes here, but we will record the bounds

\displaystyle b^{\prime}\leq D_{k}\leq e^{bk},\quad b^{\prime}\leq C_{k}\leq e^{bk^{2}}.

(109)

Next, let
\epsilon>0
satisfy the
(k,d)
-dependent upper bound

\epsilon<e^{-1}\wedge(17)^{-1/k}C_{k}^{-1/k}D_{k}^{-1}\wedge\exp(-(24)^{6}d^{6}/D_{k}^{3}).

This simplifies to

\epsilon<e^{-bd^{6}-bk}.

(110)

Finally, we set
T=D_{k}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{1/3}
.

Now, for a function
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
, define its
bridge version

f^{[a,b]}=f-L
, where
L
is the linear function satisfying
L(a)=f(a)
and
L(b)=f(b)
.
Next, with all parameters
d,k,\epsilon,T
fixed as above, let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by

•

all the lower curves
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\geq k+1
,

•

the top
k
curves
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
restricted to the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|x|\geq 2T\}
,

•

certain
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1})
-measurable random variables
\mathfrak{l}\leq\mathfrak{r}\in[-T,T]
, and

•

the
2k
bridges
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{[-2T,\mathfrak{l}]},i=1,\dots,k
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{[\mathfrak{r},2T]},i=1,\dots,k
.

Here we use the notation
\sigma(X)
for the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
.
We let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\;|\;{\mathcal{F}_{k}})
be the conditional law given
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
. The precise nature of the random variables
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{r}
is not important for us here, only their potential ranges and that they are functions of the
(k+1)^{\mathrm{st}}
curve
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1}
. For precise definitions, see the beginning of Section 4.1.5 in
[
9
]
.

With parameters
d,k,\epsilon,T
fixed as above, in
[
9
]
and previously in
[
28
]
, the authors define a collection of random functions
J=\{J_{i}:[-2T,2T]\to\mathbb{R},i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
known as the
jump ensemble
. First, for any sequence of functions
X=\{X_{i}:[-2T,2T]\to\mathbb{R},i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
, we can define a
resampled ensemble

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},X}=\{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},X}_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. For this definition we let
L(x,a;b,y)
denote the affine function with
L(x)=a,L(y)=b
.

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{\operatorname{re},X}(x)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(x),\quad&(i,x)\notin\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times[-2T,2T]\\
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{[-2T,\mathfrak{l}]}(x)+L(-2T,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(-2T);\mathfrak{l},X_{i}(\mathfrak{l}))(x),\qquad&x\in[-2T,\mathfrak{l}],i\leq k\\
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{[\mathfrak{r},2T]}(x)+L(\mathfrak{r},X_{i}(\mathfrak{r});2T,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(2T))(x),\qquad&x\in[\mathfrak{r},2T],i\leq k\\
X_{i}(x),&x\in[\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}],i\leq k.\end{cases}

(111)

Note that in
[
9
]
, the same object is only defined for the top
k
lines.
Next, in
[
9
]
, the authors define an
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
-measurable finite set
P\subset[\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}]
called a
pole set
, see the discussion in
[
9
, Section 4.1.5]
. The precise nature of this set is not important for us. Let
B=\{B_{i}:[-2T,2T]\to\mathbb{R},i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
be a collection of Brownian bridges with
B_{i}(\pm 2T)=0
that are independent of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
and each other. Finally, we define the ensemble
J
in the following way.

•

First let
J^{\prime}=\{J^{\prime}_{i}:[-2T,2T]\to\mathbb{R},i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\}
be given by connecting up the points
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(\pm 2T)
with the Brownian bridges
B_{i}
. That is, for all
i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket
,

J^{\prime}_{i}=B_{i}+L(-2T,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(-2T);2T,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(-2T)).

•

Next, let
J
be given by the ensemble
J^{\prime}
, conditionally on the events

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{\operatorname{re},J^{\prime}}(x)>\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{\operatorname{re},J^{\prime}}(x)>\dots>\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1}^{\operatorname{re},J^{\prime}}(x)\quad

\displaystyle\;\text{for}\;x=[-2T,\mathfrak{l}]\cup[\mathfrak{r},2T],\quad\;\text{and}\;

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{\operatorname{re},J^{\prime}}(x)\geq\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1}^{\operatorname{re},J^{\prime}}(x),\qquad

\displaystyle\text{ for all }i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket,x\in P.

This is the same as the definition given at the beginning of Section 4.1.6 in
[
9
]
.
Next, let
\mathsf{Pass}(J)
be the indicator of event where

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{\operatorname{re},J}(x)>\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i+1}^{\operatorname{re},J}(x)\qquad\text{ for all }x\in[-2T,2T],i\in\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket.

The relevance of the jump ensemble
J
lies in the following lemma..

Lemma B.1
.

We have

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{\operatorname{re},J}|_{\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times[-2T,2T]}\in\cdot\;|\;\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}|_{\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times[-2T,2T]}\in\cdot).

Here the restriction to
\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times[-2T,2T]
is a restriction to the top
k
lines and the interval
[-2T,2T]
.

This is a special case of Lemma 4.5 in
[
9
]
. To compare with that lemma, we take
X^{\prime}=J
, and replace the deterministic values
\ell
and
r
and the set
A
with random
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
-measurable values
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{r}
and the
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
-measurable set
P
. As noted in
[
9
]
in the discussion immediately following Equation (17) (at the end of Section 4.1.6), this replacement with
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
-measurable random variables follows does not affect the lemma since the claim is about
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
-conditional distributions.

The usefulness of Lemma
B.1
in practice comes from the following four facts.

(I)

There exists an
{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
-measurable event
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1)\geq\exp\left(-3973k^{7/2}(d_{ip})^{2}D_{k}^{2}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{2/3}\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}).

This is Proposition 4.2 in
[
28
]
, quoted as Proposition 4.9 in
[
9
]
. For use in
[
28
]
, the quantity
d_{ip}
above is a parameter related to the pole set
P
, but in
[
9
]
and for our purposes, we take
d_{ip}=5d
(see Equation (18) in
[
9
, Section 4.1.8]
and surrounding discussions). Moving forward, we work with the simplified version of the above bound given by

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1)\geq\exp\left(-d^{2}e^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{2/3}\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}).

(II)

The event
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
satisfies

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}^{c})\leq\epsilon.

This bound uses Lemma 4.1 in
[
28
]
, cited as Lemma 4.10 in
[
9
]
.

(III)

On
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
, we have
[-d,d]\subset[\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}]
. This follows from the paragraph before Lemma 4.10 in
[
9
]
, which states that
[-T/2,T/2]\subset[\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}]
, and the discussions in
[
9
]
after Lemma 4.10, which shows that
[-d,d]\subset[-T/2,T/2]
.

(IV)

Let
\mathcal{C}_{d}
denote the space of continuous functions from
[-d,d]
to
\mathbb{R}
that vanish at
-d
, equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra in the topology of uniform convergence. Let
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
denote the law of a standard Brownian motion on
[-d,d]
started from the initial condition
B(-d)=0
. This is a measure on
\mathcal{C}_{d}
.
Then there exists an absolute positive constant
G
such that if
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)=\epsilon
, then

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(J_{k}(\cdot)-J_{k}(-d)\in A\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})\leq\epsilon Gd^{1/2}D_{k}^{4}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{4/3}\exp\left(792dD_{k}^{5/2}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right).

This is Theorem 4.11 in
[
9
]
. The most important term to keep in mind here is the
(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}
in the exponent. Moving forward, we will work with the simplified version of the bound given by

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(J_{k}(\cdot)-J_{k}(-d)\in A\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})\leq\epsilon\exp\left(de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right).

(112)

Observe also that the inequality (
112
) for all
A
with
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)=\epsilon
implies that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(J_{k}(\cdot)-J_{k}(-d)\in B\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})\leq\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(B)\exp\left(de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)

(113)

for all
B
with
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(B)\geq\epsilon
.

In
[
9
]
, the authors use these three bounds with Lemma
B.1
to find explicit Radon-Nikodym derivative estimates for individual parabolic Airy lines versus Brownian motion. With only slightly more work, we can upgrade these estimates to give bounds for multiple parabolic Airy lines versus several independent Brownian motions. We start with a lemma that translates the bounds on the jump ensemble to a conditional bound on parabolic Airy lines. For this lemma, we will also need to define
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
when
\epsilon
does not satisfy the bound in (
110
). In this case, we set
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
to be the whole space.

Lemma B.2
.

With
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
as above, for every
d\geq 1,k\in\mathbb{N},
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, for every Borel measurable set
A
in
\mathcal{C}_{d}
with
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)\geq\epsilon
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in A\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})<\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right).

(114)

Moreover, if we let
f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
denote the (random) Radon-Nikodym derivative of the random measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in\cdot\right)
with respect to
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
, then almost surely,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mathbf{1}\left(f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\geq\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})\leq\epsilon\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right).

(115)

Proof.

First, the bound (
114
) holds trivially whenever
\epsilon
does not satisfy (
110
) as long as
b
is taken large enough. Therefore we may assume that (
110
) holds.

In this case, we can let
J
be the jump ensemble defined with parameters
d,k
, and
\epsilon
.
Then by Lemma
B.1
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in A\right)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},J}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},J}_{k}(-d)\in A\;|\;\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1\right).

Now, by assertion (III) above,
[-d,d]\subset[\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}]
on
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
. Therefore by the definition (
111
) of the resampled ensemble
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},J}_{k}
, on
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},J}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{re},J}_{k}(-d)\in A\;|\;\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(J_{k}(\cdot)-J_{k}(-d)\in A\;|\;\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(J_{k}(\cdot)-J_{k}(-d)\in A\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\mathsf{Pass}(J)=1\right)}.

By assertion (I) and (
113
) above, on
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
the right-hand side above is bounded by

\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)\exp\left(de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}+d^{2}e^{bk}(\epsilon^{-1})^{2/3}\right).

The bound (
110
) on
\epsilon
implies that this is bounded above by the right-hand side of (
114
).
It remains to show (
115
).

We first claim that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in\cdot\right)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
and so the Radon-Nikodym derivative
f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
is well-defined. To see this, observe that
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is contained in the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}_{k}
generated by
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}|_{S^{c}}
, where
S=\left\llbracket 1,k\right\rrbracket\times[-T,T]
, so it suffices to prove the same absolute continuity for
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in\cdot\right)
. Conditional on
\mathcal{G}_{k}
, the Brownian Gibbs property for
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
(Theorem
2.14
) ensures that the process
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}|_{[-T,T]}
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian bridge between the
\mathcal{G}_{k}
-measurable endpoints
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\pm T)
at times
\pm T
. Since Brownian bridge increments are absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian motion increments away from the endpoints of the bridge interval,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{G}_{k}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in\cdot\right)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
.

Next, let

A=\left\{f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\geq\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right\},

so that the left-hand side of (
115
) is equal to

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in A)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}).

By the definition of
A
, this is bounded below by

\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}).

By (
114
), this implies that
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A)\leq\epsilon
. Therefore we can find a set
S
such that
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}(A\cup S)=\epsilon
. Then by (
114
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in A)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(-d)\in A\cup S)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right),

giving (
115
).
∎

The next theorem is a restatement of Theorem
8.10
.

Theorem B.3
.

Let
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}
denote the law of
k
-tuples of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{d}
given by the product of
k
copies of
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
. Define
\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}=(\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{1},\dots\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k})
by letting
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{i}=\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(\cdot)-\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{i}(-d)
, restricted to the interval
[-d,d]
. Then for any set
A,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
d\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}\in A)\leq\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)\exp\left(bkd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log[\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)]^{-1})^{5/6}\right),

where
b>0
is a universal constant.

Proof.

Fix
d\geq 1
. We will first show that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, that there exists an
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-measurable set
\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k,\epsilon})\leq k\epsilon
such that for every
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{d}
-measurable set
A
with
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)\geq\epsilon
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}\in A\right)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon})\leq\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)\left(4\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right)^{k}.

(116)

The set
\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon}
is not from
[
9
]
, and is contained in
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
but does not have an explicit construction. Think of the ‘!’ as indicating that it is an extra favourable version of the set
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
.
To prove (
116
) we use induction on
k
. For the proof of (
116
), we fix the constant
b
, since increasing
b
during the inductive step would be problematic.
The
k=1
case for all
\epsilon\in(0,1]
is given in Lemma
B.2
with the set
\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon}=\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
. Now suppose that the claim holds for
k-1
and all
\epsilon\in(0,1]
.
Let
A\subset\mathcal{C}_{d}^{k}
be a Borel measurable set with
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)=\epsilon
. For every
x\in\mathcal{C}_{d}
, define the fibre

A_{x}=\{y\in\mathcal{C}_{d}^{k-1}:(y,x)\in A\}.

Then we can write

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k}\in A)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}})\right).

(117)

where the last equality uses that
\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
.
We use the inductive hypothesis to estimate
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}})
. First, let
S
be any set with
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(S)=\epsilon^{2}
.
Then we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}})\leq\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}_{k-1,\epsilon^{2}})+\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k-1,\epsilon^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)\left(4\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{b(k-1)}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right)^{k-1}+\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k-1,\epsilon^{2}}).

(118)

Here the final inequality uses the inductive hypothesis, and the fact that
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)
is always greater than
\epsilon^{2}
.
Next, we want to apply
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
to the right-hand side of (
118
). We start with the term
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)
. As in Lemma
B.2
, let
f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}
denote the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left(\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\in\cdot\right)
with respect to
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
. Letting
W
be an independent Brownian motion drawn from the distribution
\mu_{0,*}^{[-d,d]}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)=

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(W)\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{W}\cup S)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(W)\mathbf{1}\left(f_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(W)\geq\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right)

(119)

\displaystyle+\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{W}\cup S).

Now, by the definition of the sets
A_{x}
and a union bound, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{W}\cup S)\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}[\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{W})+\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(S)]=\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)+\epsilon^{2}\leq 2\epsilon.

Also, on the event
\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
in Lemma
B.2
, we can bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
119
) above using (
115
). Therefore

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k-1}(A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}\cup S)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon})\leq 3\epsilon\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right).

(120)

We now bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
118
). We have

\mathbb{E}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k-1,\epsilon^{2}}))=\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k-1,\epsilon^{2}})\leq(k-1)\epsilon^{2},

by the inductive hypothesis,
so by Markov’s inequality, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k-1,\epsilon^{2}}))\leq\epsilon

(121)

on a set
B
of probability
1-(k-1)\epsilon
. We now set
\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon}=B\cap\mathsf{Fav}_{k,\epsilon}
. Assertion (II) and a union bound shows that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav!}^{c}_{k,\epsilon})\leq k\epsilon
. Finally, gathering the inequalities (
118
), (
120
), and (
121
), we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}^{k-1}\in A_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{k}})\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon})\\
\leq&\;\left(4\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{b(k-1)}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right)^{k-1}3\epsilon\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)+\epsilon.\end{split}

This is bounded above by (
116
) when
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)=\epsilon
. As in (
113
), the extension of (
116
) to all
A
with
\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)\geq\epsilon
is immediate.

The theorem then follows by averaging over
\mathcal{F}_{k}
. More precisely, let
A
be any set, and define
\epsilon=\mu_{d}^{\bigotimes k}(A)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}\in A)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}(\hat{\mathcal{B}}\in A)\mathbf{1}(\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon})+\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav!}_{k,\epsilon}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\left(4\exp\left(bd^{6}+de^{bk}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{5/6}\right)\right)^{k}+k\epsilon,

This gives the desired bound after increasing
b
.
∎
